II. General causal models (knowledge-based).

The graphical models discussed so far based on templates conveniently encapsulate structural information about the state, decision, and outcome variables.

14
14

14

In reality, this class of causal diagrams was introduced under the rubric of
clustered diagrams
and different properties investigated, we refer readers to
(
4
)
for further details.

As will become apparent in the following sections,
this will naturally incur a cost in many practical CRL tasks.
For now, we note that in some settings, additional information may be available that could be leveraged by the CRL agent.

For concreteness, consider the diagram in Fig.
8(c)
. One way of thinking about it is as a refinement of the MAB diagram shown in Fig.
8(a)
, where a confounder
Z
and a mediator
W
are now explicit. One natural question is how to test a model
designed by the AI engineer. Interestingly enough, there are constraints imprinted by the SCM over the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
, as well as the other PCH’s distribution, that will allow the CRL agent to check whether its current working hypothesis is plausible, regardless of the idiosyncrasies of the properties of the distribution of exogenous
P(\bm{U})
and the causal mechanisms
\mathscr{F}
(e.g., monotonicity, linearity, separability).

We will discuss for now constraints known as
conditional independences
accompanied with a criterion known as
d
-separation
(
146
)
that allows us to read such constraints from the model. A path
p
from a node
X
to a node
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
is a sequence of edges that does not include a particular node more than once. It may go either along or against the direction of the edges. A path consisting of only bidirected edges is called a bidirected path. Formally, it goes as follows.

Definition 18

(
d
-separation
(
146
)
)
.

A set
\bm{Z}\subseteq\bm{V}
is said to block a path
p
in
\mathcal{G}
if either

1.

p
contains at least one arrow-emitting node that is in
\bm{Z}
, or

2.

p
contains at least one collision node outside
\bm{Z}
and has no descendant in
\bm{Z}
.

If
\bm{Z}
blocks
all
paths from set
\bm{X}
to set
\bm{Y}
, it is said to “
d
-separate
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
.’’

15
15

15

See
63
,
124
, and
143
for a gentle introduction to
d
-separation.

\blacksquare

Before discussing some examples, we state one of the main results that connect d-separation statements made over the diagram
\mathcal{G}
with constraints observed in the distribution
P(\bm{V})
.

Theorem 19

(Probabilistic Implications of
d
-Separation
(
146
)
)
.

If
\bm{X},\bm{Y}
are
d
-separated by
\bm{Z}
in a causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
, then
\bm{X}
is independent of
\bm{Y}
conditional on
\bm{Z}
in every distribution
P
compatible with
\mathcal{G}
. Conversely, if
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are not
d
-separated by
\bm{Z}
in a diagram
\mathcal{G}
, then
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
are dependent conditional on
\bm{Z}
in at least one distribution
P
compatible with
\mathcal{G}
.
\blacksquare

To illustrate these results, consider the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
in Fig.
8(c)
and whether
X
and
Y
are d-separated. Note that there are two paths from
X
to
Y
,

\displaystyle p_{1}:X\leftarrow\framebox{$Z$}\rightarrow Y,

(74)

\displaystyle p_{2}:X\rightarrow\framebox{$W$}\rightarrow Y.

(75)

Since
\bm{Z}=\{\}
in this case, both paths
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are opened.
One interpretation of this result is that there is a flow of information from
X
that is transmitted through
Z,W
that affects
Y
.
Now, let’s consider the separation statement when the conditioning set
\bm{Z}=\{Z,W\}
, or the confounder and mediator are in
\bm{Z}
.
The first condition of the criterion is then immediately satisfied, and the paths
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are “blocked.”
We can see through Thm.
19
that the following independence holds in
P(\bm{V})
:

\displaystyle\left(Y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X\mid\{Z,W\}\right).

(76)

Intuitively, once the values of
Z
and
W
are known, there is no information about
X
that will affect the likelihood of
Y
through
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, respectively. Readers are invited to check that the criterion is not satisfied if any intermediate variables are removed from the conditioning set.

III. General causal models (learning-based).

Based on the marks imprinted by
\mathcal{M}^{*}
on
P(\bm{V})
, the agent may test whether the hypothesized graph
\mathcal{G}
is compatible with the available data. There exists a traditional literature known as
causal discovery
that attempts to perform the reverse process
(
146
;
172
;
148
)
.
In other words, from the marks readable from
P(\bm{V})
, the agent should infer what the compatible
\mathcal{G}
that could have left these traces is. In practice, assumptions regarding the simplicity of these models (à la Occam’s razor) are used to avoid situations described above, in which a saturated model would be preferred.

A growing, more recent literature is concerned with combining both observational and experimental distributions to learn a more restrictive equivalence class of causal diagrams
(
83
;
82
;
201
;
2
;
122
;
74
)
. In fact, a richer set of constraints other than conditional independences emerge when we consider multiple distributions across different regimes (observational and interventional).

3
Elements of Causal Reinforcement Learning

In this section, we will introduce a unified framework that lets us view the decision-making problem through causal lenses and solve reinforcement learning tasks using causal inference tools. First, Sec.
3.1
formalizes the decision-making problem in the causal language by introducing a mathematical object called causal decision models (CDMs).
Every CDM comprises of a structural causal model representing the underlying environment, a policy space encoding what the agent can control and observe during the intervention, and a reward function that gauges the agent’s performance.

Armed with this new formalism, we can represent many canonical decision-making settings found in the literature within the semantic framework of SCMs.
These include multi-armed bandits (MABs), Markov decision processes (MDPs), and dynamic treatment regimes (DTRs), among others.
In practical scenarios, a detailed parametrization of the environment isn’t always fully known, giving rise to reinforcement learning in SCMs.
Sec.
3.2
introduces the concept of
causal reinforcement learning task
, and provides an initial catalog of tasks that will be studied through this section.
Each task is delineated by the manner in which the agent interacts with the environment (regime), any prior structural assumptions about that environment, and the specific policy space and reward function the agent seeks to optimize.
Lastly, Sec.
3.3
delves into the importance of causal knowledge by examining policy learning within the conventional decision-making model of MDPs.
Without explicitly acknowledging the learning regime and structural assumptions, we’ll demonstrate that the underlying data-generating mechanisms can yield multiple MDPs, all compatible with the observed data, but with diverging implications for the optimization of decision-making. In simpler terms, the observed data typically doesn’t fully dictate an optimal policy.

3.1
Causal Decision Models

We now formalize the policy optimization problem in SCMs based on the causal machinery introduced earlier in this section. The underlying environment will be represented as an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\bm{U},\bm{V},\mathscr{F},P\rangle
. We study the problem of interacting on action variables
\bm{X}\subseteq\bm{V}
to optimize some performance measures over reward signals
\bm{Y}\subseteq\bm{V}
evaluated by
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. The agent determines values of actions
\bm{X}
by performing intervention
\text{do}(\pi)
following some policies
\pi
. The collection of all candidate policies
\pi
determining values of actions
\bm{X}
defines a
policy space

\Pi
. Formally,

Definition 20

(Policy Space)
.

For an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\bm{U},\bm{V},\mathscr{F},P\rangle
, a policy space
\Pi
is a collection of policies
\pi
over actions
\bm{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{H}\}
. Each policy
\pi
is a sequence of decision rules
\left(\pi_{1}\left(X_{1}\mid\bm{S}_{1}\right),\dots,\pi_{H}\left(X_{H}\mid\bm{S}_{H}\right)\right)
such that for every
i=1,\dots,H
,
16
16

16

The policy space
\Pi
is also referred to a policy scope in
(
104
)
, which characterizes the agent’s action and state scope - what it could interaction with and what it could observe at the time of interaction.

•

Action
X_{i}
is a non-descendent of
X_{i+1},\dots,X_{H}
, i.e.,
X_{i}\in\bm{V}\setminus\mathit{De}(X_{i+1},\dots,X_{H})
;

•

States
\bm{S}_{i}
are non-descendants of
X_{i},\dots,X_{H}
, i.e.,
\bm{S}_{i}\subseteq\bm{V}\setminus\mathit{De}(X_{i},\dots,X_{H})
.

Henceforth, we will consistently denote such a policy space by
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{1},\bm{S}_{1}\rangle,\dots,\langle X_{H},\bm{S}_{H}\rangle\right\}
.
\blacksquare

Policies
\Pi_{\text{full}}
\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
\Pi_{\text{stationary}}

Figure 9
:
Policy spaces for a 2-stage DTR environment.

Every policy
\pi\in\Pi
is a sequence of decision rules
\left(\pi_{1}\left(X_{1}\mid\bm{S}_{1}\right),\dots,\pi_{H}\left(X_{H}\mid\bm{S}_{H}\right)\right)
. An agent following policy
\pi
selects values of actions
\bm{X}
following a temporal ordering
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
. At every step of intervention
i=1,\dots,H
, it performs the following

1.

Observe some state variables
\bm{S}_{i}=\bm{s}_{i}
;

2.

Select a value of action
x_{i}\sim\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid\bm{S}_{i}=\bm{s}_{i})
following the decision rule
\pi_{i}
;

3.

Perform an intervention
\text{do}(X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i})
following the selected action
x_{i}
.

In words, a policy space
\Pi
defines the action space
\bm{X}
that the agent could control after being deployed in the environment and the state space
\bm{S}_{1},\dots,\bm{S}_{H}
that the agent could perceive at the time of intervention for every action
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
. The subsequent examples illustrate the concept of policy space in dynamic treatment regimes (for short, DTRs), which is a class of sequential decision-making environments widely applied in healthcare and personalized medicine.

Example 21

(Dynamic Treatment Regimes
(
128
)
)
.

In healthcare, a typical patient is often treated at multiple stages; the physician repeatedly adapts each treatment, tailoring it to the patient’s time-varying, dynamic state. Dynamic treatment regimes provide an appealing framework for managing personalized medicine in the longitudinal setting.

For instance, consider a DTR for managing alcohol-dependent patients, adapted from
(
127
;
28
)
. The physician (i.e., the agent) has to decide the initial treatment
X_{1}
and the secondary treatment
X_{2}
. Based on the condition of an alcohol-dependent patient (
S_{1}
), the physician may use behavioral therapy
(X_{1}=0
) or prescribe medication (
X_{1}=1
). The patient is then classified as a responder or a non-responder (
S_{2}
) based on the level of drinking within the next two months.
The physician must then decide whether to continue the initial treatment
(X_{2}=0)
or switch to a more intensive plan combining medication and behavioral therapy (
X_{2}=1
). We are interested in the primary outcome
Y
that measures the patient’s days of abstinence over 12 months after the treatment.

More formally, consider a DTR environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}}
that is a tuple given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}}=\langle\bm{U}=\{U,U_{1},\dots,U_{4}\},\bm{V}=\{S_{1},S_{2},X_{1},X_{2},Y\},\mathscr{F}_{\textsc{dtr}},P(\bm{U})\rangle,

(77)

where the underlying causal mechanisms
\mathscr{F}_{\textsc{dtr}}
are given by,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\textsc{dtr}}=\begin{cases}S_{1}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{U_{3}>0\},\\
X_{1}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{3S_{1}+\alpha_{1}U+U_{1}>0\},\\
S_{2}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{0.1+0.1S_{1}+0.1X_{1}+U_{4}>0\},\\
X_{2}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{3S_{2}+\alpha_{2}U+U_{2}>0\},\\
Y\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{3U-3S_{1}-3X_{1}-3S_{1}X_{1}+3X_{2}-3S_{2}X_{2}+3X_{1}X_{2}>0\},\end{cases}

(78)

Among quantities in the above equations, we set coefficients
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
. However, for examples in subsequent sections, these coefficients will be non-zero.

The exogenous distribution
P(\bm{U})
is defined such that for
i=1,\dots,4
,
U_{i}\sim\texttt{Logistic}(0,1)
is an independent variable drawn from a logistic distribution

\displaystyle P\left(U_{i}<u\right)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-u}}

(79)

and
U\sim\texttt{Unif}(0,1)
is an independent variable drawn uniformly over a real interval
[0,1]
.
\blacksquare

It is possible to define different policy spaces in the DTR environment described above, depending on the choices of input states
\bm{S}_{i}
for every action
X_{i}
, and parametric forms of decision rules
\pi_{i}
.

Example 22

(DTR, Policy Space)
.

For the 2-stage DTR environment described in Eq.
78
. We consider a policy space
\Pi_{\text{full}}=\{\langle X_{1},\{S_{1}\}\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\}\rangle\}
satisfying the
perfect recall

(
84
)
. That is, the agent determines every action based on all the past states and actions’ history. More specifically, every adaptive treatment strategy (i.e., a policy)
\pi\in\Pi_{\text{full}}
is a pair of decision rules

\displaystyle\pi=\left(\pi_{1}\left(X_{1}\mid S_{1}\right),\pi_{2}\left(X_{2}\mid S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\right)\right),

(80)

where
\pi_{1}
prescribes an initial treatment
X_{1}
based on the patient’s initial condition
S_{1}
; and
\pi_{2}
decides whether to continue or switch the previous plan
X_{2}
based on the patient’s responses
S_{2}
to the previous treatment, the initial treatment, and condition
X_{1},S_{1}
.

One could also define a more restricted policy space
\Pi_{\text{Markov}}=\{\langle X_{1},\{S_{1}\}\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{S_{2}\}\rangle\}
. Every policy
\pi\in\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
is a pair of decision rules

\displaystyle\pi=\left(\pi_{1}\left(X_{1}\mid S_{1}\right),\pi_{2}\left(X_{2}\mid S_{2}\right)\right),

(81)

where every
\pi_{i}
,
i=1,2
, prescribes a treatment
X_{i}
based on the patient’s condition
S_{i}
at the current stage. Such policies are also referred to as Markov policies in planning literature
(
151
)
. Note that for every Markov policy
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
, one could simulate it with a general policy
\pi\in\Pi_{\text{full}}
by setting
\pi_{2}(X_{2}\mid S_{1},X_{1},S_{2})=\pi^{\prime}_{2}(X_{2}\mid S_{2})
. It follows that
\Pi_{\text{Markov}}\subset\Pi_{\text{full}}
, i.e., every Markov policy is contained in the general policy space
\Pi_{\text{full}}
.

Finally, we define a stationary policy space
\Pi_{\text{stationary}}\subset\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
. Every policy
\pi\in\Pi_{\text{stationary}}
is a Markov policy satisfying an additional parametric constraint such that decision rule
\pi_{i}
remains invariant across all stages
i=1,2
. That is, for a stationary policy
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
,

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\left(X_{1}\mid S_{1}\right)=\pi_{2}\left(X_{2}\mid S_{2}\right)

(82)

Since
\Pi_{\text{Markov}}\subset\Pi_{\text{full}}
, we must have
\Pi_{\text{stationary}}\subset\Pi_{\text{Markov}}\subset\Pi_{\text{full}}
. Fig.
9
shows a Venn diagram representing the relationship between
\Pi_{\text{stationary}}
,
\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
, and
\Pi_{\text{full}}
. The outer rectangle represents all possible policies over actions
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, including over a singleton action
X_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
\blacksquare

The agent’s performance is measured by a reward function that takes a set of reward signals in the environment as input.

Definition 23

(Reward Function)
.

For an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\bm{U},\bm{V},\mathscr{F},P\rangle
, a reward function
\mathcal{R}
is a function
\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
mapping domains of a subset of endogenous variables
\bm{Y}\subseteq\bm{V}
to a real value in
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the endogenous variables
\bm{Y}
are called reward signals.
\blacksquare

Def.
23
covers most performance criteria in the decision-making literature. For instance, for a sequence of reward signals
\bm{Y}=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{H}\}
, the
cumulative reward

\mathcal{R}_{\text{total}}(\bm{Y})
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\text{total}}(\bm{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{H}Y_{i}.

(83)

When the total number of reward signals
H\to\infty
, the above cumulative reward does not necessarily converge. In this case, a reasonable criterion is to consider the
average reward
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\text{average}}(\bm{Y})=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{i=1}^{H}Y_{i}.

(84)

The above reward function is ensured to converge as the total number of reward signals
H\to\infty
. Alternatively, let a discount factor
\gamma\in(0,1)
and define the
discounted cumulative reward
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\text{discount}}(\bm{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{H}\gamma^{i-1}Y_{i}.

(85)

The discount factor can be interpreted in several ways; as an interest rate, the probability of living another step, or the mathematical trick for bounding the infinite sum.

Definition 24

(Causal Decision Model)
.

A causal decision model (CDM) is a tuple
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
where
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\bm{U},\bm{V},\mathscr{F},P\rangle
is an SCM,
\Pi
is a policy space over actions
\bm{X}\subseteq\bm{V}
, and
\mathcal{R}
is a reward function over reward signals
\bm{Y}\subseteq\bm{V}
.
\blacksquare

Among elements in Def.
24
, the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
represents the underlying environment; the policy space
\Pi
indicates the agent’s capabilities after deployed in the environment, i.e., what it could control and observe at the time of interaction; the reward function
\mathcal{R}
measures the performance of the agent, from the system designer’s perspective. Formally, every CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
characterizes a planning/decision-making task
(
21
)
that attempts to find an optimal strategy from the policy space
\Pi
dictating the agent’s behaviors, provided with the complete parametrization of the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. An optimal policy
\pi^{*}
for a CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
is a policy in space
\Pi
that maximizes the reward function
\mathcal{R}
evaluated by the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\pi^{*}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}\right);\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]

(86)

We will graphically represent every CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
using an augmented causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
constructed from the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Fig.
16
); actions
\bm{X}
and reward signals
\bm{Y}
are highlighted in blue and red respectively; for every action,
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
, its input states
\bm{S}_{i}
are highlighted in light blue. Fig.
10
shows the graphical representation for some canonical planning tasks.

A few observations are worth making at this point. First, the number of action variables
H=|\bm{X}|
represents the horizon of the decision sequence.
17
17

17

In episodic reinforcement learning, the agent collects data by interacting with the environment for repeated episodes
t=1,\dots,T
. The decision horizon
H
represents the total steps of actions
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
that the agent has to decide in every episode. We will further elaborate on the episodic learning for optimizing CDMs in Sec.
3.2
.
When
H=1
, the CDM corresponds to the single-stage decision models such as MABs
(
153
)
.
On the other hand, when
H>1
, the CDM defines a sequential decision-making problem, e.g., MDPs
(
151
)
, when the agent has to sequentially determine values of actions
X_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,H
, based on values of the observed states
\bm{S}_{i}
at the time of the intervention.

Second, it is possible to define multiple CDMs in the same environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
by changing the policy space
\Pi
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}
, resulting in different optimal policies. In other words, the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
is defined with regard to the agent’s capabilities to interact the environment after being deployed and how the system designer incentives its behaviors. Consequently, changing the forms of policy
\Pi
and reward
\mathcal{R}
affects the optimal solution
\pi^{*}
.

Example 25

(CDMs in DTR)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}}
be a DTR environment compatible with Fig.
10(c)
. The primary outcome
Y
is given by
Y\leftarrow X_{2}\oplus S_{1}
; values of
S_{1}
are uniformly drawn from the binary domain
\{0,1\}
. Let
\Pi_{\text{full}}
and
\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
be policy spaces defined in Example
22
.

We could define CDMs
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}},\Pi_{\text{full}},Y\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}},\Pi_{\text{Markov}},Y\right\rangle
, which represent two different planning tasks. The former searches for a general policy
\pi^{*}_{\text{full}}\in\Pi_{\text{full}}
determining actions
X_{i}
based on the complete states and actions’ history
S_{1},\dots,S_{i}
,
X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}
; while the latter finds a Markov policy
\pi^{*}_{\text{Markov}}\in\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
selecting action
X_{i}
based on the current state
S_{i}
.

First, for any Markov policy
\pi_{\text{Markov}}=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
in
\Pi_{\text{Markov}}
, its expected reward is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\text{Markov}}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{2},s_{1}}\mathbb{E}[x_{2}\oplus s_{1}]P(s_{1})\sum_{s_{2}}\pi_{2}(x_{2}\mid s_{2})P(s_{2})

(87)

\displaystyle=0.5

(88)

The last step holds since
S_{1}
is uniformly drawn over the binary domain
\{0,1\}
. Also, solving the CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}},\Pi_{\text{full}},Y\right\rangle
gives an optimal policy
\pi^{*}_{\text{full}}=(\pi^{*}_{1},\pi^{*}_{2})
such that
\pi^{*}_{2}\triangleq X_{2}\leftarrow\neg S_{1}
. Evaluating the expected reward of
\pi^{*}_{\text{full}}
in
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}_{\text{full}}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[S_{1}\oplus\neg S_{1}]

(89)

Computing the above equation implies
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}_{\text{full}}}\left[Y\right]=1
which outperforms the best possible Markov policy
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}_{\text{Markov}}}\left[Y\right]=0.5
. Moreover, suppose the sign of the reward function is changed to
\mathcal{R}(Y)\leftarrow-Y
. The same solution
\pi^{*}_{\text{full}}
is no longer optimal in a CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}},\Pi_{\text{full}},-Y\right\rangle
since it now minimizes the expected primary outcome
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[-Y\right]
instead.
\blacksquare

More broadly, the formulation of CDMs permits one to represent canonical planning tasks (or equivalently, decision-making models) in the literature across disciplines, including RL and healthcare, when the detailed parametrization of the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is known. These canonical tasks are graphically represented in Fig.
10
, which we will briefly describe below.

Figure 10
:
Causal diagrams for CDMs representing canonical decision-making models.

•

Multi-Armed Bandit
(
153
)
.
Fig.
10(a)
is induced by a MAB model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}},\Pi,Y\rangle
consisting of an arm choice
X
and reward
Y
. Policy space
\Pi=\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\}
defines a set of policies
\pi
that selects values of action
X
following a probability distribution
\pi(X)
. Please visit Example
26
for a detailed instance of a MAB model.

•

Contextual Bandit
(
94
)
.
Fig.
10(b)
is the graphical representation of a contextual bandit (C-MAB) model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{cmab}},\Pi,Y\rangle
. Compared with MABs, a context variable
S
is now observed. The policy space
\Pi=\{\langle X,\{S\}\rangle\}
consists of candidate policies
\pi(X|S)
which selects values of action
X
based on the observed context
S
.

•

Dynamic Treatment Regime
(
127
)
.
In a DTR model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}},\Pi,Y\rangle
, the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\{S_{1},\dots,S_{i},X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
is a set of policies
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{H})
consisting of a finite sequence of decision rules. For every
i
-th stage, the decision rule
\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{1},\dots,S_{i},X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1})
selects values of action
X_{i}
based on all the past action and states’ history
S_{1},\dots,S_{i},X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}
. The goal is to maximize the primary outcome
Y
after intervening on all actions
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
. Fig.
10(c)
is the graphical representation of a
2
-stage DTR model; a detailed instance is provided in Example
21
.

•

Markov Decision Process
(
20
;
151
)
.
Consider a Markov decision process (MDP) model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mdp}},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
. Environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
consists of a set of states
\bm{S}=\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, a set of actions
\bm{X}=\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and a set of reward signals
\bm{Y}=\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
. The policy space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},S_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
consists of a set of decision rules
\pi=(\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
. The reward function
\mathcal{R}
can be described as the average reward
\mathcal{R}_{\text{average}}(\bm{Y})
in Eq.
84
or the discounted reward
\mathcal{R}_{\text{discount}}(\bm{Y})
in Eq.
85
. In the discounted case, rewards obtained later are discounted more than rewards obtained earlier. If the discounted factor
\gamma=0
, the agent is said to be myopic, i.e., it is only concerned about immediate rewards. Fig.
10(d)
represents the causal diagram of an MDP model spanning over steps
i=1,2,3
. See Example
27
for a detailed instance of a policy planning task in an MDP environment.

•

Partially Observable MDP
(
6
)
.
A partially observable MDP (POMDP) is a generalization of MDP in which system dynamics are determined by an MDP environment, but the agent could not directly utilize the underlying state
\bm{S}=\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
as input to determine its actions
\bm{X}=\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
. Instead, it only receives a set of observation variables
\bm{O}=\{O_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
depending on the underlying states. Fig.
10(e)
shows a POMDP model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{pomdp}},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
spanning over steps
i=1,2,3
. The policy space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\{O_{1},\dots,O_{i},X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
defines a set of non-Markov policies
\pi=(\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid O_{1},\dots,O_{i},X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
. For every
i
-th stage of intervention, an agent following a non-Markov policy
\pi\in\Pi
selects an action
x_{i}\sim\pi_{i}\left(X_{i}\mid O_{1},\dots,O_{i},X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\right)
based on all the past observations and actions.

When the policy space
\Pi
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}
are well-specified, and detailed parameters of the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
are provided, there exist efficient algorithms in the planning literature to solve for an optimal policy in a CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle

(
20
;
151
;
164
)
.
For instance, for an MDP model graphically described in Fig.
10(d)
, one could obtain an optimal policy using standard dynamic programming algorithms
(
20
;
151
)
. The same planning procedure applies to a DTR model
(
128
;
127
;
130
)
, e.g., Fig.
10(c)
.
Due to the latent nature of underlying states, planning in POMDP models (e.g.,
10(e)
) is more computationally challenging, and requires the planning algorithm to maintain memory and possibly reason about beliefs over the states. A variety of heuristics for approximate planning in POMDPs have been proposed
(
72
;
61
;
62
)
. Optimizing policies in a general CDM has been studied under the rubrics of influence diagrams; several algorithms and approximate procedures have been proposed, including
(
164
;
85
;
98
)
.

The following examples illustrate CDMs in some canonical decision-making settings in the literature, together with the planning procedure for computing the optimal policy.

Example 26

(MAB Planning)
.

Consider a CDM described by the tuple

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}=\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}},\Pi=\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\},\mathcal{R}(Y)=Y\rangle,

(90)

where
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}}
is the MAB environment described in Example
2
. Fig.
10(a)
shows the graphical representation of this CDM where action
X
and reward
Y
are highlighted in blue and red respectively.

Every policy
\pi(X)\in\Pi
is a probability distribution over domains of action
\mathscr{D}(X)=\{0,1\}
. Evaluating the expected reward
Y
in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}_{\pi}}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\sum_{u}\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid x,u\right]P(u)\pi(x)

(91)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]\pi(X=0)+\mathbb{E}_{X\leftarrow 1}\left[Y\right]\pi(X=1)

(92)

The last step follows from marginalizing over the exogenous variable
U
. Note that evaluation of expected rewards
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
of atomic interventions
\text{do}(X\leftarrow x)
is provided in Example
12
. Replacing interventional queries
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
in the above equation gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=0.4\pi(X=0)+(0.4-\Delta)\pi(X=1)

(93)

\displaystyle=0.4-\Delta\pi(X=1)

(94)

The last step follows from
\sum_{x}\pi(x)=1
. Since the coefficient
\Delta>0
, the reward function
\mathcal{R}(Y)=Y
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}}
is maximized when probability
\pi(X=1)=0
, That is, the optimal policy is determinsitic
\pi^{*}:X\leftarrow x^{*}
with the optimal arm choice
x^{*}=0
.
\blacksquare

Example 27

(MDP Planning, Dynamic Programming)
.

Consider the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mdp}}
given by Eq.
5
. We are interested in optimizing the MDP model described by a CDM given by

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}=\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mdp}},\Pi=\{\langle X_{i},\{S_{i}\}\rangle\}_{i=1}^{\infty},\mathcal{R}_{\text{discount}}(\bm{Y})\right\rangle

(95)

where
\mathcal{R}_{\text{discount}}(\bm{Y})
is the discounted reward function given by Eq.
85
with
\gamma=0.9
. We will focus on stationary policies
\pi=\left(\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
such that decision rules
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=\dots
remain invariant across decision horizons
i=1,2,\dots
.
18
18

18

Indeed, it has been shown that there always exists a stationary policy that could optimize the cumulative reward in an MDP model
(
48
)
. It thus suffices to focus on stationary policies.

Let
\mathscr{D}(S)
and
\mathscr{D}(X)
denote the domain of state
S_{i}
and action
X_{i}
at every stage
i
, respectively. For any stationary policy
\pi\in\Pi
, a state-action value function
Q_{\pi}:\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X)\to\mathbb{R}
(also called a Q-function) is defined as the expected cumulative reward following policy
\pi
given the starting state
s
and initial action
x
, i.e.,

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}(s,x)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{j}Y_{i+j}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]

(96)

Since the structural functions
f_{S_{i}}
and
f_{Y_{i}}
remains invariant across decision horizon
i=1,2,\dots
, for any policy
\pi
, any state
s
, and any action
x
, the above expression can be recursively defined in terms of a so-called
Bellman Equation

(
21
)
:

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y_{i}+\gamma Y_{i+1}+\gamma^{2}Y_{i+2}+\cdots\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]

(97)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y_{i}+\gamma Q_{\pi}(S_{i+1},X_{i+1})\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]

(98)

The last step follows from the recursive definition of the value function
Q_{\pi}(s,x)
and the Markov property in the interventional distribution; see Example
14
for details. The above equation could be further written as, by expanding on next state
S_{i+1}
and action
X_{i+1}
,

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Q_{\pi}(S_{i+1},X_{i+1})\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]

(99)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]

(100)

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime}}P_{\pi}\left(S_{i+1}=s^{\prime}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}\leftarrow x\right)\sum_{x^{\prime}}\pi_{i+1}(x^{\prime}\mid s^{\prime})Q_{\pi}(s^{\prime},x^{\prime})

(101)

Since the transition distribution and the conditional reward remain invariant across atomic and policy interventions (Eqs.
60
and
63
), the above equation could be further written as

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}(s,x)+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime}}\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}(s,x,s^{\prime})\sum_{x^{\prime}}\pi_{i+1}(x^{\prime}\mid s^{\prime})Q_{\pi}(s^{\prime},x^{\prime})

(102)

where the transition probability
\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}(s,x,s^{\prime})=P_{X_{i}\leftarrow x}\left(S_{i+1}=s^{\prime}\mid S_{i}=s\right)

(103)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}(s,x)=\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}\leftarrow x}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=s\right]

(104)

Detailed parametrizations of interventional quantities
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
are provided in Fig.
6
.

S

X

Q_{*}

S

X

Q_{*}

0

0

8.2

1

7.56

0

8.2

Table 2
:
Optimal Q-function
Q_{*}(s,x)
evaluated in the MDP model of Example
27
.

An optimal policy
\pi^{*}
is such that
Q_{\pi^{*}}(s,x)\geq Q_{\pi}
for all state-action pair
s,x
and all policies
\pi
. Optimizing Q-function leads to an expression called the
Bellman optimality equation
, i.e,

\displaystyle Q_{*}(s,x)=\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}(s,x)+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime}}\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}(s,x,s^{\prime})\max_{x^{\prime}}Q_{*}(s^{\prime},x^{\prime})

(105)

The optimal policy
\pi^{*}
is given by, for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle\pi^{*}_{i}(S_{i}=s)=\argmax_{x}Q_{*}(s,x)

(106)

for any state
s\in\mathscr{D}(S)
.
We can compute the optimal Q-function evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mdp}}
using value iteration
(
177
)
. Detailed parametrizations are provided in Table
2
. Complete computations are provided in Appendix
B
.
The optimal policy
\pi^{*}=\left(\pi^{*}_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
is given by
\pi^{*}_{i}\triangleq X_{i}\leftarrow S_{i}
, for every
i=1,2,\dots
. Evaluating its expected return gives
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{i-1}Y_{i}\right]=8.2
; detailed derivation steps are provided in Example
15
.
\blacksquare

Example 28

(DTR Planning, Dynamic Programming)
.

Consider the DTR environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}}
in Eq.
78
. Our goal is to maximize the patient’s days of abstinence
Y
over 12 months after the treatment. This decision-making problem is described by a CDM given by

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}=\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}},\Pi=\{\langle X_{1},\{S_{1}\}\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\}\rangle\},\mathcal{R}(Y)=Y\rangle

(107)

Fig.
10(c)
describes a causal diagram of
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}}
where actions
X_{1},X_{2}
are highlighted in blue, primary outcome
Y
in red, and input covariates
\{S_{1}\}
and
\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\}
in light blue.

Table 3

.

For every policy
\pi\in\Pi
, evaluating recovery rate
Y
in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{dtr}_{\pi}}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]=\sum_{s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2}}\pi_{2}(x_{2}\mid s_{1},x_{1},s_{2})\pi_{1}(x_{1}\mid s_{1})\bigg(\sum_{u}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2},u\right]

\displaystyle P\left(s_{2}\mid s_{1},x_{1},u\right)P(s_{1}\mid u)P(u)\bigg)

(108)

Since
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
are not functions of the exogenous variable
U
, summing over domain of
U
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]=\sum_{s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2}\right]\pi_{2}(x_{2}\mid s_{1},x_{1},s_{2})P_{x_{1}}\left(s_{2}\mid s_{1}\right)\pi_{1}(x_{1}\mid s_{1})P(s_{1})

(109)

As shown in
(
127
)
, the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
is deterministic, and satisfies the Bellman equation
(
20
)

\displaystyle\pi^{*}_{1}(s_{1})

\displaystyle=\argmax_{x_{1}}Q^{(1)}_{*}\left(s_{1},x_{1}\right)

(110)

\displaystyle\pi^{*}_{2}(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=\argmax_{x_{2}}Q^{(2)}_{*}\left(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right)

(111)

The optimal Q-function is

\displaystyle Q^{(1)}_{*}(s_{1},x_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{2}}\max_{x_{2}}Q^{(2)}_{*}\left(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right)P_{x_{1}}\left(s_{2}\mid s_{1}\right)

(112)

\displaystyle Q^{(2)}_{*}\left(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2}\right]

(113)

We compute the parametrization of Q-functions evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and provide them in Table
3(a)
. See Appendix
B
for complete computation.
The optimal actions
\pi^{*}_{1}(s_{1})
,
\pi^{*}_{2}(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2})
given every state-action’s history is highlighted. Solving for an optimal policy gives
\pi_{1}^{*}\triangleq X_{1}\leftarrow 0
and
\pi^{*}_{2}\triangleq X_{2}\leftarrow 1
. In words, in order to maximize the patient’s days of abstinence, the physician should start with behavioral therapy and follow up with an intensive treatment combining both behavioral therapy and medication.
The expected reward of this policy
\pi^{*}
is computable as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1}}Q^{(1)}_{*}(s_{1},X_{1}=0)P(s_{1})

(114)

\displaystyle=Q^{(1)}_{*}(S_{1}=0,X_{1}=0)P(S_{1}=0)+Q^{(1)}_{*}(S_{1}=1,X_{1}=0)P(S_{1}=1)

(115)

Evaluating the above equation gives the optimal expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[Y\right]=0.6758
.
\blacksquare

3.2
Causal Reinforcement Learning Tasks

The causal decision model described so far assumes the full knowledge of the underlying environment. However, in many real-world practical applications, the detailed parametrization of the environment is very rarely known, which means that standard planning algorithms are not immediately applicable. In order for the agent to optimize the performance of the underlying system, a learning process must take place, leading to the learning paradigm of
causal reinforcement learning
.

To make the argument more precise, we start the discussion of a CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
. Recall that it defines a planning task of finding an optimal policy in space
\Pi
that maximizes the reward function
\mathcal{R}
evaluated in the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. A CRL agent
\mathbb{C}
is assumed to have access to the policy space
\Pi
and the performance measurement
\mathcal{R}
.

19
19

19

The policy space
\Pi
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}
are provided in most of the learning tasks considered in this paper. However, there exist practical applications where the reward function
\mathcal{R}
is not fully known. In this case, the agent has to “guess” a surrogate reward function from a hypothesis class
\mathbb{R}
and then compute an optimal policy estimate with it.
This setting is studied under the rubric of causal imitation learning in Sec.
8
.

However, the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is not fully known. Instead, the agent
\mathbb{C}
only has access to some structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
encoding qualitative knowledge about the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, and a learning regime
\mathcal{L}
dictating how it interacts with the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
to collect data. This partial knowledge constitutes new dimensions for the task formulation of optimal decision-making under uncertainty, which we will briefly discuss below.

Learning Regimes (
\mathcal{L}
).

Following the discussion of the PCH, each CRL agent may be able to interact with
\mathcal{M}^{*}
in different ways, including through passive observations (i.e.,
\mathcal{L}=\text{see}
) or by active interventions (
\mathcal{L}=\text{do}
). These learning regimes model distinct types of interactions of the agent with the environment. The former corresponds to off-policy reinforcement learning tasks
(
109
;
110
)
; while the latter corresponds to online reinforcement learning tasks
(
9
)
. More specifically, an agent passively observing the environment does not actively determine actions. Instead, it receives observational data
\mathcal{D}\sim P(\bm{V})
summarizing trajectories of another agent (e.g., a human demonstrator) already operating in the environment, following a behavioral policy. On the other hand, an agent may actively control actions
\bm{X}
by performing interventions
\text{do}(\pi)
, following some policies
\pi
, and receiving experimental data
\mathcal{D}\sim P_{\pi}\left(\bm{V}\right)
. We will investigate these reinforcement learning algorithms in depth later on in this paper.

Structural Assumptions (
\mathcal{A}
).

Structural assumptions specify a hypothesis class of possible environmental models that the agent is operating with. One common way of specifying assumptions about the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is through a causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
(Def.
16
). The hypothesis class
\mathbb{M}
is thus defined as the family of SCMs
\mathcal{M}
compatible with the diagram
\mathcal{G}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{G}
. For instance, the causal diagram in Fig.
10(d)
specifies a family of MDP environments consisting of states
S_{i}
, actions
X_{i}
, and reward signals
Y_{i}
, for
i=1,2,\dots
; the underlying transition and reward distributions remain un-specified. Other assumptions include constraints over the features of the behavior policy
(
141
)
, or equivalence classes of causal diagrams
(
207
)
, to cite a few.

SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}

(Unobserved)

Agent
\mathbb{C}

\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A},\Pi,\mathcal{R}

\mathcal{L}_{1}

\mathcal{L}_{2}

\mathcal{L}_{3}

PCH

Policy Space
\Pi

\left\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\right\}

Reward Function
\mathcal{R}

\mathscr{D}(Y)\mapsto[0,1]

Structural Assumptions
\mathcal{A}

See
Do
X
Z
Y

Figure 11
:
Graphical representation of a causal reinforcement learning task

Consider again a CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
, where the model of the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is not fully revealed to the agent. Replacing
\mathcal{M}^{*}
with the learning regime
\mathcal{L}
and structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
leads to a new signature
\left\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
which characterizes a
causal reinforcement learning task
. Formally,

Definition 29

(Causal Reinforcement Learning Task)
.

For an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\left\langle\bm{U},\bm{V},\mathscr{F},P\right\rangle
, a CRL task
\mathcal{T}
in the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is a 4-tuple
\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
, where

1.

\mathcal{L}
is a learning regime of an agent’s interaction with the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, possibly
see
or
do
;

2.

\mathcal{A}
is a set of structural assumptions about the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
;

3.

\Pi
is a policy space over actions
\bm{X}
;

4.

\mathcal{R}
is a reward function over reward signals
\bm{Y}
.
\blacksquare

Formally, every CRL task
\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
describes an optimal decision-making problem under uncertainties about the underlying environment. Provided with input
\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
, the CRL agent attempts to estimate an optimal policy
\pi^{*}\in\Pi
defined in Eq.
86
maximizing the reward
\mathcal{R}
evaluated in the unknown environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Since
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is not fully observed, we substitute it with the learning regime
\mathcal{L}
and structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
about the environment, depending on the specific task.
The goal of the agent is then to find a policy
\pi^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\pi^{*}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}\right)\bigg|\;\mathcal{A},\mathcal{L}\right]

(116)

Compared to the optimization given by Eq.
86
, we move the unobserved SCM that evaluates the agent as a superscript and leave what the agent has access to as part of the conditioning set.

For instance, Fig.
11
shows a graphical instance of a CRL task
\mathcal{T}
in an unknown bandit environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
consisting of an arm choice
X
, reward
Y
, and a covariate
Z
. The goal is to find an optimal policy
X\leftarrow x^{*}
in the policy space
\Pi=\left\{X,\emptyset\right\}
maximizing the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathcal{R}(Y)\right]
. The agent could passively observe the environment (
\mathcal{L}=\text{See}
) and receives observational data drawn from
P(X,Y,Z)
; it could also actively intervene on the arm (
\mathcal{L}=\text{Do}
) and receive interventional data drawn from
P_{x}\left(Y,Z\right)
. The causal diagram
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G}_{\text{backdoor}}
(bottom left) encodes structural knowledge that the agent has about the environment: there is no spurious correlation between
Z
and
Y
and between
X
and
Y
.

Algorithm 1

Causal Reinforcement Learning Agent
\mathbb{C}

1:

CRL task
\mathcal{T}=\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle

2:

a policy estimate
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi
optimizing a CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
.

3:

Let data
\mathcal{D}=\{\}
.

4:

for all
every episode
t=1,\dots,T

do

5:

Interact with the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
following regime
\mathcal{L}
and receive samples
\bm{V}^{(t)}\sim P_{\mathcal{L}}\left(\bm{V};\mathcal{M}^{*}\right)
.

6:

Update data
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}\cup\left\{\bm{V}^{(t)}\right\}
.

7:

end

for

8:

return
an empirical estimate
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi
of the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
from data
\mathcal{D}
and assumptions
\mathcal{A}
.

Alg.
1
provides pseudo-code describing the general learning strategy of a CRL agent to solve a task
\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{A},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
. We follow the episodic reinforcement learning setting
(
177
)
where the agent interacts with the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for repeated episodes
t=1,\dots,T
. For each episode
t
, the agent interacts with the environment following the learning regime
\mathcal{L}
and receives sample
\bm{V}^{(t)}
, consisting of realized actions
\bm{X}^{(t)}
, observed states
\bm{S}^{(t)}
, reward signals
\bm{Y}^{(t)}
, and other endogenous variables. For the observational regime
\mathcal{L}=\textit{see}
, the CRL agent passively observes another agent, currently deployed, to determine values of every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
following a behavioral policy
f_{\bm{X}}
. For the interventional regime
\mathcal{L}=\textit{do}
, the CRL agent actively intervenes on every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
following a policy
\pi
, and receives subsequent states
\bm{S}
and rewards
\bm{Y}
. Finally, the agent
\mathbb{C}
computes an empirical estimate of an optimal policy
\pi^{*}
in the policy space
\Pi
from the combination of the collected data
\mathcal{D}
and structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
.

The formulation of the task signature and the CRL agent summarizes existing policy learning problems and learning strategies in the reinforcement learning and causal inference literature. The following examples illustrate CRL tasks in single-stage decision-making settings.

Example 30

(Off-Policy Learning
(
177
)
, MAB)
.

Consider first a MAB model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\},Y\rangle
graphically described in Fig.
10(a)
. A CRL agent
\mathbb{C}
aims to learn an arm

\displaystyle x^{*}=\argmax_{x}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]

(117)

with the maximal expected reward.
The detailed parametrization of SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is unknown.
Instead, the agent could only passively observe the environment and receive the observational distribution
P(X,Y)
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
This leads to an
off-policy learning
task described through the following signature:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{off}}=\langle\mathcal{L}=\text{see},\mathcal{A}=\text{NUC},\Pi=\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\},\mathcal{R}=Y\rangle,

(118)

NUC
stands for the assumption of “No Unmeasured Confounder”: there is no unobserved confounder affecting the action
X
and the reward
Y
simultaneously.
All the observed correlations between
X
and
Y
are fully explained by the causal relationships among them, which implies that

\displaystyle P_{x}\left(Y\right)=P(Y\mid X=x)

(119)

Therefore, the agent could evaluate the expected reward of every arm
x
from the observational data
P(X,Y)

20
20

20

We assume that the number of the observational data is sufficient, and joint distribution
P(X,Y)
is recovered.
, and find optimal an optimal arm
x^{*}
with the maximal empirical reward estimates.
\blacksquare

Example 31

(Online Learning
(
177
)
, MAB)
.

We will continue with the previous example of the MAB model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\},Y\rangle
. Suppose the CRL can now actively intervene in the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. This leads to an
online learning
task described by a signature

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{on}}=\langle\mathcal{L}=\text{do},\mathcal{A}=\emptyset,\Pi=\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\},\mathcal{R}=Y\rangle,

(120)

In order to evaluate every arm
x
, the CRL agent performs an intervention
\text{do}(x)
in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and receives subsequent reward signals drawn from
P_{x}\left(Y\right)
. The expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
is thus estimable from the experimental data by computing the empirical means.
\blacksquare

Example 32

(Causal Identification
(
146
)
, MAB)
.

Consider the off-policy learning task of Eq.
118
again.
Suppose now that the NUC assumption no longer holds.
Instead, a previously unobserved covariate
Z
is now revealed; the CRL agent has access to a more detailed causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\text{backdoor}}
(Fig.
11
, bottom left) describing the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
Replacing the NUC with structural assumptions encoded in diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\text{backdoor}}
leads to a
causal identification
task

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{id}}=\langle\mathcal{L}=\text{see},\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G}_{\text{backdoor}},\Pi=\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\},\mathcal{R}=Y\rangle,

(121)

Like off-policy learning, the CRL agent observes the environment and receives the observational distribution
P(X,Y,Z)
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Provided with the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\text{backdoor}}
, applying the backdoor adjustment formula
(
146
, Ch. 3.3)
implies

\displaystyle P_{x}\left(y\right)=\sum_{z}P\left(y\mid z,x\right)P\left(x\right)

(122)

That is, the expected reward of every arm
x
is computable from the observational data
P(X,Y,Z)
. Optimizing the expected reward over the action domain
X
leads to an optimal arm.
\blacksquare

Table 4
:
Summary of causal reinforcement learning tasks investigated in this paper, in terms of their signatures and sections. We highlight in gray the most distinct feature introduced by the task.

Broadly speaking, the formalization of the CRL dimensions and the corresponding tasks, semantically defined through structural causal models, allows us to describe most of the popular learning settings studied in the literature.
It also enables us to explore and study novel CRL learning tasks beyond the current literature and that arises naturally in real-world applications.
We first summarize the more traditional RL-CI tasks with their corresponding signatures in Table
4
.
Specifically, we will discuss in Sec.
4
, the policy learning methods for traditional CI and RL tasks through the language of CRL.
In practice, this encompasses tasks such as off-policy and online learning and causal identification. All of these focus on optimizing policies within an experimental policy space
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}
(Def.
20
).
These tasks can be viewed as variations of the first two dimensions described in the table, namely, the interactive regime and the structural assumptions, while the other dimensions remain constant.
Even though these tasks are prevalent in current literature, certain critical conditions weren’t formally understood prior to our new formalization.
For instance, determining the validity of an off-policy method (e.g., inverse propensity weighting and dynamic programming), specifically, whether it can find a policy consistent with optimal one given by the underlying SCM.
Analyzing these classical tasks through CRL perspective will be instrumental in illuminating other foundational issues and illustrating how causal and RL formalisms intersect.

3.3
Comparison with Markov Decision Processes

The CRL tasks described so far (Def.
29
) assume that the agent has access to either the learning regime
\mathcal{L}
under which the data are collected, or structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
encoding causal invariances about the environment.
In this section, we will demonstrate that such causal knowledge is generally indispensable (necessary) for learning an optimal policy in an unknown SCM. Our discussion focuses on standard MDPs, which is a class of sequential decision-making models widely used in practice.

Definition 33

(Standard MDP
(
151
)
)
.

A Markov decision process is tuple
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
where

1.

\mathscr{D}(S)
is a set of states called the state space;

2.

\mathscr{D}(X)
is a set of actions called the action space;

3.

\mathcal{T}(s,x,s^{\prime})\in[0,1]
is a transition probability that action
X_{i}=x
in state
S_{i}=s
at stage
i
will lead to state
S_{i+1}=s^{\prime}
at stage
i+1
;

4.

\mathcal{R}(s,x)
is the immediate reward received in state
S_{i}=s
due to action
X_{i}=x
at stage
i
.

A policy
\pi(x|s)
in a standard MDP is a function mapping from the state space
\mathcal{D}(S)
to a probability distribution over the action space
\mathcal{D}(X)
. For any indices
i<j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\bar{\bm{V}}_{i:j}
denote a sequence
\{V_{i},V_{i+1},\dots,V_{j}\}
.
Given a policy
\pi
and a distribution over the initial state
P(S_{1})
, every standard MDP model defines a joint distribution over states
\bar{\bm{S}}_{1:H}
, actions
\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:H}
, and rewards
\bar{\bm{Y}}_{1:H}
up to decision horizon
H
, i.e.,

\displaystyle P_{\pi}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})=P(s_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{H}\pi(x_{i}\mid s_{i})\mathcal{T}(s_{i},x_{i},s_{i+1})\mathds{1}\{\mathcal{R}(s_{i},x_{i})=y_{i}\}

(123)

The key assumption of a standard MDP is that the transition probability and reward functions depend on the past only through the current state of the system and the action selected by the decision maker in that state. This assumption is called the
Markov property

(
151
)
and can be characterized using the following independence relationships, for every stage
i=2,3,\dots
,

\displaystyle\left(\bar{\bm{S}}_{1:i-1},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i-1},\bar{\bm{Y}}_{1:i-1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\bar{\bm{S}}_{i+1:\infty},\bar{\bm{X}}_{i:\infty},\bar{\bm{Y}}_{i:\infty}\mid S_{i}\right)

(124)

In other words, the standard MDP could be seen as a compact representation of a family of joint distributions over observed trajectories of states
\bar{\bm{S}}_{1:H}
, actions
\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:H}
, and rewards
\bar{\bm{Y}}_{1:H}
, provided that the Markov property holds.
In the language of structural causality, the Markov property could hold in both the observational distribution
P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})
and the interventional distribution
P_{\pi}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})
.

21
21

21

In this case, the decision rule
\pi(x|s)
is set as the conditional distribution
P(X_{i}=x\mid S_{i}=s)
defined by the behavioral policy
f_{X}
.
We showed in Examples
6
and
14
the compression of the observational and interventional distributions of an SCM instance to standard MDP models respectively.

To make the argument more precise, consider the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
graphically described in the MDP diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{mdp}}
of Fig.
10(d)
. For every state
i=1,2,\dots
, conditioning on state
S_{i}
and action
X_{i}
blocks all paths from history
S_{j},X_{j},Y_{j}
for
j<i
to any future state
S_{k}
, action
X_{k}
, and reward
Y_{k}
for
k>i
(Def.
18
).
The observational distribution evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
thus satisfies the Markov property in Eq.
124
and can be represented using a standard MDP.

22
22

22

We will consistently assume that structural functions
f_{S_{i}},f_{Y_{i}}
and distributions
P(\bm{U}_{S_{i}},\bm{U}_{Y_{i}})
remain invariant across decision horizons
i=1,2,\dots
. This is a common assumption for solving infinite-horizon MDP
(
151
)
.

Example 34

(MDP, Observational)
.

Consider the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Eq.
5
. Its observational distribution
P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})
defines a standard MDP
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{obs}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{obs}}\right\rangle
where the transition probability and the reward function are observational quantities given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{obs}}(s,x,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=P\left(S_{i+1}=s^{\prime}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right)

(125)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\text{obs}}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]

(126)

Detailed parametrizations of system dynamics
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
can be compactly represented as a finite-state machine and are shown in Fig.
5(b)
.

Table 5
:
Optimal Q-function
Q_{*}(s,x)
evaluated in the MDP model of Example
34
.

Following the Bellman equation in Eq.
105
, we solve for the optimal Q-function
Q_{*}(s,x)
in the standard MDP
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{obs}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{obs}}\right\rangle
and provide it in Table
5
. Maximizing the action
x
for every state
s
in
Q_{*}(s,x)
gives an optimal decision rule
\pi^{*}_{\text{obs}}\triangleq X_{i}\leftarrow\neg S_{i}
.
\blacksquare

Consider a policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\{S_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
. Following a similar argument, we could show that the interventional distribution induced by any policy
\pi\in\Pi
evaluated in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
satisfies the Markov property, leading to an alternative standard MDP representation.

Example 35

(MDP, Interventional)
.

Consider the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Eq.
5
. For every policy
\pi=\left(\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
, its interventional distribution
P_{\pi}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})
satisfies the Markov property in Eq.
124
. It defines a standard MDP
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}\right\rangle
where the transition probability and the reward function are interventional quantities given by Eqs.
103
and
104
. Their parametrizations are described in the finite-state machine of Fig.
6(b)
.

We also solve for the optimal Q-function
Q_{*}(s,x)
in the MDP
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}\right\rangle
and obtain an optimal decision rule
\pi^{*}_{\text{exp}}\triangleq X_{i}\leftarrow S_{i}
. Revisit Example
27
for detailed computations.
\blacksquare

Some important observations follow from these two examples. First, the Markov property holds in both the observational and interventional distributions evaluated in the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Eq.
5
, resulting in two standard MDPs.
Second, solving these MDPs leads to different policies
\pi^{*}_{\text{obs}}
and
\pi^{*}_{\text{exp}}
.
The previous discussion in Example
27
showed that only
\pi^{*}_{\text{exp}}
is the optimal policy in the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
; while
\pi^{*}_{\text{obs}}
is sub-optimal.
This suggests that the model assumptions of standard MDPs are generally insufficient in determining the optimal policy in the underlying causal model, however many samples are provided.

Consider now a CRL agent that interacts with the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and receives observed data
\mathcal{D}
. Without specifying the learning regime
\mathcal{L}
(see or do) or causal knowledge
\mathcal{A}
, the agent cannot determine whether data
\mathcal{D}
is drawn from the observational or interventional distribution from the Markov property. If data
\mathcal{D}
is collected from passive observations, optimizing the learned standard MDP model could lead to a sub-optimal policy, resulting in unsatisfactory performance. One may wonder if it is possible to recover interventional quantities
\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}
form the observational data
\mathcal{D}\sim P(\bm{V})
in MDP environments. Unfortunately, our next result suggests otherwise.

Figure 12
:
Causal Hierarchy Theorem (CHT) in MDP environments.

Proposition 36
.

For any SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
compatible with the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{mdp}}
of Fig.
10(d)
, there is an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
compatible with
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{mdp}}
such that for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle P^{(1)}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right)=P^{*}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{*}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right]

(127)

while

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{x_{i}}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i}\right)\neq P^{*}_{x_{i}}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}_{x_{i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i}\right]\neq\mathbb{E}^{*}_{x_{i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i}\right]

(128)

\blacksquare

The following example constructs an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
that generates the observational distribution as the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, but differs significantly in interventional distributions.

Example 37

(MDP, Observational
\not\Rightarrow
Interventional)
.

Consider the following MDP environment

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3}\},\bm{V}=\{X_{i},Y_{i},S_{i}\},\mathscr{F}=\left\{\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{i}\right\},P^{(1)}(\bm{U})\right\rangle_{i=1,2,\dots},

(129)

where the causal mechanisms
\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{t}
are defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{t}=\begin{cases}S_{i}\leftarrow S_{i-1}\oplus U_{i-1,2},\\
X_{i}\leftarrow S_{i}\oplus U_{i,1},\\
Y_{i}\leftarrow U_{i,3},\end{cases}

(130)

and
P^{(1)}(U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3})
is such that
U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3}
are independent variables drawn from distribution
P(U_{i,1}=1)=0.9
, and
P(U_{i,2}=1)=P(U_{i,3}=1)=0.1
.

We compute the observational distributions
P(S_{i+1}\mid S_{i},X_{i})
and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mid S_{i},X_{i}]
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
and show their parametrization in the finite-state machine in Fig.
12
(a). It is verifiable that MDP models
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(defined in Eq.
5
) generate the same observational distributions (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
), i.e., the equalities in Eq.
510
hold. On the other hand, we also derive the interventional distribution
P_{X_{i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid S_{i}\right)
,
\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}\left[Y\mid S_{i}\right]
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
. Their parametrization could also be summarized using Fig.
12
(a). It follows from previous discussions (Examples
6
and
14
) that
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Eq.
5
) differ in the interventional distribution (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
), i.e., inequalities in Eq.
511
hold.
\blacksquare

The above example shows that the optimal policy in an unknown MDP environment is generally underdetermined by the observational distribution and the Markov property. Conversely, we also show that one cannot recover observational quantities from the interventional data in MDP environments.

Proposition 38
.

For any SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
compatible with the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{mdp}}
of Fig.
10(d)
, there is an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
compatible with
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{mdp}}
such that for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle P^{(2)}_{x_{i}}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i}\right)=P^{*}_{x_{i}}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(2)}_{x_{i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{*}_{x_{i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i}\right]

(131)

while

\displaystyle P^{(2)}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right)\neq P^{*}\left(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(2)}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right]\neq\mathbb{E}^{*}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i},x_{i}\right]

(132)

\blacksquare

Our next example corroborates the aforementioned proposition by constructing an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
that generates the same interventional distribution as the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
but appears different from passive observations.

Example 39

(MDP, Interventional
\not\Rightarrow
Observational)
.

Consider the following MDP environment

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(2)}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3}\},\bm{V}=\{X_{i},Y_{i},S_{i}\},\mathscr{F}=\left\{\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{i}\right\},P^{(2)}(\bm{U})\right\rangle_{i=1,2,\dots},

(133)

where the causal mechanisms
\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{t}
are defined as:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{t}=\begin{cases}S_{i}\leftarrow\left(S_{i-1}\vee X_{t-1}\right)\oplus U_{i-1,2},\\
X_{i}\leftarrow S_{i}\oplus U_{i,1},\\
Y_{i}\leftarrow S_{i}\oplus X_{i}\oplus U_{i,3},\end{cases}

(134)

and
P^{(2)}(U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3})
is such that
U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3}
are independent variables drawn from distribution
P(U_{i,1}=1)=0.9
, and
P(U_{i,2}=1)=0.82=P(U_{i,3}=1)=0.82
.

We compute the interventional distribution
P_{X_{i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid S_{i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}\left[Y\mid S_{i}\right]
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
and summarize them in the finite-state machine described in Fig.
12
(b).
It is verifiable that MDP models
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Eq.
5
) define the same interventional distributions (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
), i.e., the equalities in Eq.
526
hold.
We also compute the observational distributions
P(S_{i+1}\mid S_{i},X_{i})
and
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid S_{i},X_{i}]
of
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
and provide their parametrizations in Fig.
12
(b).
The previous discussions (Examples
6
and
14
) implied that
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Eq.
5
) differ significantly in the observational distribution (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
), i.e., inequalities in Eq.
527
hold.
This complements previous examples and illustrates that interventional queries are generally under-determined by observational data in MDP environments.
\blacksquare

The last two examples are summarized and the results are illustrated in Fig.
12
. In the middle of the figure, we show the true MDP model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
initially discussed in Example
3
and its induced observational and interventional distributions (described in more detail in Figs.
5
and
6
respectively). Assuming only observational data (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
) is available, one can construct an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
(left side) that matches the observational distribution but have different interventional behavior (i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1}=\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{2}\neq\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_{2}
).
Formally, the interventional distribution is underdetermined by the observational distribution.
Practically, this means that passively observing another agent acting in the environment and collecting samples from it may not be enough to make claims about the agent’s policies and their corresponding performance.

On the other hand, the same is the case in the reverse direction;
say, whenever interventional data (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) is available, one can then construct an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
(right side) that matches the interventional distribution but has a different observational one (i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1}\neq\mathcal{L}^{(2)}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{2}=\mathcal{L}^{(2)}_{2}
). Formally, the observational distribution is underdetermined by the interventional distribution.
This may be counter-intuitive since interventions are usually believed to be more informative than just passively observing the system unfold in time. Still, in practice, it doesn’t allow the CRL agent to predict how other agents will behave when interacting in the environment. This impossibility will translate into challenges when considering the communication and exchange of experience across agents with the intent of accelerating learning.

More generally, the Causal Hierarchy Theorem
(
15
, Thm. 1)
states that this impossibility result is strict for almost all causal models. This means it is generically impossible to draw higher-layer inferences using only lower-layer information.
Given that the actual underlying SCM is rarely observable in practice, and no inferences across the layers of the PCH are possible, the CRL agent will need to resort to some causal knowledge and assumptions to make claims about these underlying mechanisms, as discussed in Sec.
2.3
.
Since the more typical language to describe standard MDPs is constrained to one particular distribution, it’s somewhat limiting to consider it as a baseline to the model of the environment/agent relationship given the more general types of tasks that can be represented in terms of the PCH, as discussed earlier in this manuscript.

4
Reinforcement Learning through Causal Lenses

The formalization of causal reinforcement learning tasks (Def.
29
) allows us to describe some of the most common and popular learning settings studied in the classic literature of reinforcement learning (RL) and causal inference (CI). This section will investigate learning methods for these classic RL-CI tasks, including off-policy learning (Sec.
4.1
), online learning (Sec.
4.2
), and causal identification from observational data (Sec.
4.3
). These tasks are briefly described in Table
4
and can be seen as variations of the first two dimensions described in the table, i.e., interaction regime and structural assumptions, while the other dimensions are fixed. Lastly, Sec.
4.4
varies these dimensions and moves toward a catalog of novel CRL tasks.

Even though the tasks of off-policy learning, online learning, and causal identification all come from the classic literature, there are still subtle interplays between reinforcement learning and causal invariances that were only formally understood with the CRL formalization. For instance, in the language of structural causality, we will provide a formal justification for off-policy learning algorithms, e.g., inverse propensity weighting and dynamic programming. This permits one to determine when and how to apply RL algorithms to more generalized settings where unobserved confounders exist in the observational dataset. Analyzing these classic tasks through CRL lenses will shed light on other foundational issues and how CI and RL connect.

We will introduce some additional notations before studying these learning tasks in detail. Specifically, we will optimize over a policy space with a finite decision horizon
H=|\bm{X}|<\infty
. Let actions
\bm{X}
be ordered by
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{H}
,
H=|\bm{X}|
, following a topological order in the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. For any indices
i<j
, let
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i:j}=\{X_{i},X_{i+1},\dots,X_{j}\}
denote a sequence of actions ranging from stage
i
to stage
j
. Similarly, let
\bar{\bm{S}}_{i:j}=\{\bm{S}_{i},\bm{S}_{i+1},\dots,\bm{S}_{j}\}
denote the sequence of states from stage
i
to stage
j
. For convenience, we will consistently write
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i}=\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}
and
\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}=\bar{\bm{S}}_{1:i}
. Fix a policy
\pi\in\Pi
. For any indices
i\leq j
, let
\left(\pi_{i},\dots,\pi_{j}\right)
denote a subsequence of decision rules constrained in
\pi
determining values of actions
X_{i},\dots,X_{j}
.

4.1
Off-Policy Learning

This section investigates the off-policy learning problem where an agent attempts to learn an optimal policy from observational data generated by a different behavior policy
(
177
)
, provided that there is no unmeasured confounder (NUC) in the data (to be defined). First, we will introduce the NUC assumption and discuss how it justifies the off-policy learning approach. We then describe in Sec.
4.1.1
two primary methods in evaluating candidate policies in the off-policy setting, including inverse propensity weighting and dynamic programming
(
20
)
.

An off-policy learning agent interacts with the underlying environment (SCM) through passively observing events unfolding over time. Fig.
13
is a graphical representation of the CRL agent interacting with the environment for repeated episodes
t=1,\dots,T
. For every episode
t
, the agent “sees” the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Def.
4
) and receives an observation
\bm{V}^{(t)}\sim P\left(\bm{V}\right)
. The CRL agent currently aims to use these observations to learn a policy from candidate space
\Pi
that maximizes a reward function
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})
. This will be a useful representation to compare other learning modalities and types of interactions. The following signature characterizes this learning setting:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{off}}=\left\langle\mathcal{I}=\text{see},\mathcal{A}=\text{NUC},\Pi=\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\}_{i=1}^{H},\mathcal{R}=\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\right\rangle.

(135)

where the agent uses observational data combined with the critical assumption
\mathcal{A}=\text{NUC}
, which means “
No Unmeasured Confounder
”. The agent’s goal is to obtain an optimal policy estimate from the combination of the observational data and the NUC assumption, i.e.,

\displaystyle\pi^{*}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}\right)\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\;\mathcal{A}=\text{NUC},\;\mathcal{D}_{\text{obs}}\sim P(\bm{V})}\right].

(136)

In practice, the NUC assumption will require that at every stage of intervention on action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
, its observed correlations with the reward
Y
given past actions and covariates’ history, is entirely determined by the causal relationships between
X_{i}
and
Y
. In other words, no other variables generate non-causal variations between the decision
X_{i}
and the outcome
Y
. The following definition formalizes this idea.

Definition 40

(No Unmeasured Confounder)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{*}
be a SCM and
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
be a policy space (Def.
20
). The “no unmeasured confounder” (for short, NUC) condition holds if for every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
, its endogenous parents
\bm{\mathit{PA}}_{i}
and exogenous parents
\bm{U}_{i}
satisfy the following conditions:

1.

Endogenous parents
\bm{\mathit{PA}}_{i}
are contained in the history
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}\cup\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
, i.e.,
\bm{\mathit{PA}}_{i}\subseteq\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}\cup\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
;

2.

Exogenous parents
\bm{U}_{i}
are independent from exogenous noises
\bm{U}_{j}
associated with all the other endogenous variables
V_{j}
in the system, i.e.,
\bm{U}_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\left\{\bm{U}_{j}\mid\forall V_{j}\in\bm{V}\setminus\{X_{i}\}\right\}
.

Episode t

(1)

(1)

\bm{V}^{(4)}\sim P\left(\bm{V}\right)
