Figure 13
:
Temporal diagram showing an off-policy learning agent interacting with the environment for repeated episodes.

In the above definition, Condition 1 says that all endogenous parents of every action
X_{i}
are observed, contained in the past states and actions
\bar{\bm{S}}_{i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}
; Condition 2 says that given the past history
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}\cup\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
, values of every action
X_{i}
are decided by an independent noise
\bm{U}_{i}
. Note that in the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, observational data are generated by a behavior policy
f_{\bm{X}}
which determines values of every action
X_{i}
based on the endogenous
\bm{\mathit{PA}}_{i}
and exogenous parents
\bm{U}_{i}
for all time steps
i=1,\dots,H
. The NUC condition implies that one could simulate the behavior policy using a sequence of decision rules
\left(\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{H}
such that for every step
i=1,\dots,H
,
\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i})=P(X_{i}\mid\bm{\mathit{PA}}_{i})
. Allocating actions following these decision rules leads to the following independence relationships, for any sequence of actions
\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}
,
23
23

23

The NUC assumption could also be characterized with a series of graphical conditions based on structural causality, known as
sequential backdoor condition
. We will further elaborate on the graphical implication of NUC in Sec.
4.3
.

\displaystyle\left(X_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}S_{i+1_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}},\dots,S_{H_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1}}},Y_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}},\mid\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}\right)\;\;\;\forall i=1,\dots,H

(137)

Among quantities in the above equation, the potential response
S_{i_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1}}}
,
i=1,\dots,H
, is the observed state in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1}}
induced by the atomic intervention
\text{do}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}\leftarrow\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})
; similarly,
Y_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}
is the future potential reward evaluated in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}
. The meaning of the NUC condition is illustrated in the next examples.

Example 41

(DTR models where NUC holds)
.

Consider a 2-stage DTR model
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
where SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is described in Eq.
78
and the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{1},\{S_{1}\}\right\rangle,\left\langle X_{2},\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\}\right\rangle\right\}
. We will next examine conditions of Def.
40
and show that they hold in this CDM.

First, note that for every treatment
X_{i}
,
i=1,2
, its endogenous parent
\bm{\mathit{PA}}_{i}=\{S_{i}\}
, which is contained in the corresponding input state
\bm{S}_{i}
. This implies Condition (1) of NUC holds.

Second, each structural function
f_{X_{i}}
,
i=1,2
affecting treatment
X_{i}
, the coefficients
\alpha_{i}=0
of the exogenous variable
U
is equal to zero. This means that there is no unobserved confounder affecting
X_{i}
and other variables in the system, i.e., Condition (2) of NUC also holds. Therefore, we conclude the NUC condition holds in the DTR model
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
.
\blacksquare

Example 42

(DTR models where NUC fails)
.

Continuing with the DTR model
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
in the previous example, we now consider an alternative policy space
\Pi^{\prime}=\left\{\left\langle X_{1},\emptyset\right\rangle,\left\langle X_{2},\emptyset\right\rangle\right\}
. Every policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}
decides values of treatment
X_{1},X_{2}
independently, regardless of values of other variables in the system. For
i=1,2
, the history
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}\cup\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}=\emptyset
prior to stage
i
is an empty set and does not contain the endogenous parent
S_{i}
of treatment
X_{i}
. Therefore, Condition (1) of NUC fails.

Alternatively, consider an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
where for the structural function
f_{X_{i}}
of treatment
X_{i}
,
i=1,2
, the coefficient of the exogenous variable
U
is equal to
\alpha_{i}=-3
. This means that there exists an unobserved confounder affecting treatments
X_{1},X_{2}
and the primary outcome
Y
simultaneously. Consequently, Condition (2) of NUC does not hold.
\blacksquare

4.1.1
Off-Policy Evaluation

Whenever the NUC assumption holds, there exist different strategies that allow one to estimate and compare the effects of candidate policies from observational data without having to perform online experiments in the environment. The first algorithm we discuss that implements this idea is based on a technique known as “
inverse probability weighting
” (IPW) and is widely applied in practice
(
161
;
155
;
129
;
200
;
178
;
116
)
.
Formally,

Theorem 43

(Inverse Propensity Weighting, under NUC)
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a CDM where the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. If NUC holds, for any
\pi\in\Pi
, the expected reward is computable from the observational distribution
P\left(\bm{V}\right)
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]P\left(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right)}_{\text{observational distribution}}\underbrace{\prod_{i=1}^{H}\frac{\pi_{i}\left(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i}\right)}{P\left(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)}}_{\text{ratio $\pi$ and obs. probabilities}}.

(138)

Among the above quantities,
P\left(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
measures the natural propensity of the behavior policy for action
X_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,H
, which is known as the
propensity score
. The IPW estimation requires what is called the
positivity
assumption, i.e., the propensity scores
P\left(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)>0
for every entry
\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}
.

24
24

24

This quantitative assumption is called
overlap
in
(
156
;
71
)
. There are attempts in the literature to relax it by assuming some parametric models that allow the interpolation of the unobserved areas, e.g., refer to
(
157
;
79
)
.

The following example illustrates the application of the IPW method.

Example 44
.

Consider again the CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
described in Eq.
78
where coefficients
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
. We will apply IPW estimation to evaluate the effects of the policy
\pi=(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1)
from the observational distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y)
. Applying the estimation formula provided by Thm.
43
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{ipw}}_{X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2}}P\left(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2},Y=1\right)\frac{\mathds{1}\{x_{1}=0\}}{P\left(x_{1}\mid s_{1}\right)}\frac{\mathds{1}\{x_{2}=1\}}{P\left(x_{2}\mid s_{1},x_{1},s_{2}\right)}

(139)

:

evaluated in the 2-stage DTR environment described in Example

.

The detailed parametrization of the observational distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y)
is provided in Table
6
. The above equation could be further written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{ipw}}_{X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{P\left(S_{1}=0,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=0,X_{2}=1,Y=1\right)}{P\left(X_{1}=0\mid S_{1}=0\right)P\left(X_{2}=1\mid S_{1}=0,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=0\right)}

(140)

\displaystyle+\frac{P\left(S_{1}=0,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=1,X_{2}=1,Y=1\right)}{P\left(X_{1}=0\mid S_{1}=0\right)P\left(X_{2}=1\mid S_{1}=0,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=1\right)}

(141)

\displaystyle+\frac{P\left(S_{1}=1,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=0,X_{2}=1,Y=1\right)}{P\left(X_{1}=0\mid S_{1}=1\right)P\left(X_{2}=1\mid S_{1}=1,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=0\right)}

(142)

\displaystyle+\frac{P\left(S_{1}=1,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=1,X_{2}=1,Y=1\right)}{P\left(X_{1}=0\mid S_{1}=1\right)P\left(X_{2}=1\mid S_{1}=1,X_{1}=0,S_{2}=1\right)}

(143)

Evaluating the above equation gives
\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{ipw}}_{X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1}\left[Y\right]=0.6757
, which matches the expected reward in Eq.
78
, evaluated directly in the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
\blacksquare

The numeric example above is one instantiation of the larger implication of the theorem showing that the agent does not have to go online and try different actions, but it can learn a policy by simply re-weighting the observational data whenever the conditions of the theorem hold.
Interestingly, we further note that by iteratively applying Bayes’ rule, the IPW formula in Eq.
138
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]\prod_{i=1}^{H}P\left(s_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i-1}\right)\pi_{i}\left(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i}\right).

(144)

Computing the above equation following a reverse topological ordering
i=H,\dots,1
over actions leads to an alternative algorithm for evaluating the effects of candidate policies, based on
dynamic programming
(for short, DP). DP was first introduced in
(
20
)
and has been widely applied in reinforcement learning
(
151
;
177
)
. The following proposition describes details for applying DP for off-policy evaluation from the observational distribution, provided that the NUC condition holds.

Theorem 45

(Dynamic Programming)
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a CDM where
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. If NUC holds, for any
\pi\in\Pi
, the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
is computable from the joint distribution
P\left(\bm{V}\right)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x_{1}}Q_{\pi}^{(1)}(x_{1},\bm{S}_{1})\pi_{1}(x_{1}\mid\bm{S}_{1})\right],

(145)

where the value function
Q_{\pi}^{(i)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})
, for
i=1,\dots,H-1
, is given by:

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}^{(i)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x_{i+1}}Q_{\pi}^{(i+1)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bm{S}_{i+1})\pi_{i+1}\left(x_{i+1}\mid\bm{S}_{i+1}\right)\,\middle|\,\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right]

(146)

and

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}^{(H)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]

(147)

\blacksquare

It follows from the derivation in Eq.
144
that IPW and DP estimation are, in principle, equivalent. That is, they return the same evaluation for
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]
provided with the same candidate policy
\pi
and observational data
P(\bm{V})
.
The following example illustrates this equivalence.

Example 46
.

Consider again the CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
described in Eq.
78
where coefficients
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
. We will apply the DP estimation to evaluate the effect of the policy
\pi=(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1)
. Thm.
45
allows us to estimate the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]
from the observational distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y)
as follows:

\displaystyle Q^{(1)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1})=\sum_{s_{2},x_{1}}Q^{(2)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2})\mathds{1}\{x_{2}=1\}P\left(s_{2}\mid x_{1},s_{1}\right)

(148)

\displaystyle Q^{(2)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2})=P\left(Y=1|s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2}\right)

(149)

Table 7
:
Evaluation of value functions
Q^{(1)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1}),Q^{(2)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2})
for the policy
\pi=(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1)
in evaluated in 2-stage DTR environment described in Example
21
.

We compute the parametrization of value functions
Q^{(1)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1}),Q^{(2)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},x_{2})
and provide them in Table
7
. The expected reward of the policy
\pi=(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1)
is computable as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{dp}}_{X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1}[Y]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1}}Q^{(1)}_{\pi}(s_{1},x_{1})\mathds{1}\{x_{1}=0\}P(s_{1})

(150)

Evaluating the above equation gives
\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{dp}}_{X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1}\left[Y\right]=0.6757
, which matches the expected reward in Example
21
, evaluated in the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
\blacksquare

Once IPW and DP evaluation formulas are obtained, efficient methods in the literature estimate the expected rewards of candidate policies from finite samples drawn from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
. For the IPW evaluation, the agent could weigh every observed reward signal with the odds ratio between the target policy
\pi
and the propensity score
P(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})
, i.e., the second term of Eq.
138
. The expected reward is estimable by computing the empirical mean on the weighted rewards. This IPW estimate was first developed to estimate the effects of candidate policies in the single-stage decision setting, i.e., the decision horizon
H=1
, but later adapted to the problem of estimating the effects of policies in the sequential setting, with the decision horizon
H>1
. See
(
156
;
155
;
200
;
132
)
for detailed explanations of how to apply IPW estimation from finite observations provided with the NUC condition.

As for the DP evaluation, the agent first approximates the state-action value function Q in Eq.
145
from the observational data using parametric models and then computes the expected reward of a candidate policy
(
191
)
. For instance, it could approximate Q-functions using a parametric family of linear functions; function parameters are obtainable using the standard least squares regression
(
130
)
. Other more flexible families of parametric models for the Q-functions include regression trees
(
45
)
, kernels
(
137
)
, and neural networks
(
120
)
. The value function approximation has been studied in the literature under the rubrics of batch reinforcement learning
(
25
;
93
)
.

When the expected rewards of candidate policies are computable, the agent could then search over the policy space
\Pi
and obtain an optimal policy estimate using policy gradient
(
176
)
. Moreover, when the Q-function
Q^{(i)}_{\pi}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})
only relies on the state-action value
x_{i},\bm{s}_{i}
for every stage of intervention
i=1,\dots,H
, one could solve for an optimal policy through iteratively optimizing every decision rule
\pi_{i}
following a reverse topological ordering over actions
\bm{X}

(
98
;
85
)
. This local optimization procedure is analogous to the well-celebrated Q-learning algorithm
(
202
)
.
We refer readers to
(
195
)
for a recent literature review on standard off-policy evaluation from finite observational data under the NUC assumption.

Despite the positive results discussed above, IPW and DP methods may fail to recover the effects of candidate policies from observational data whenever the NUC condition (Def.
40
) does not hold. The following examples demonstrate this looming challenge.

Example 47
.

Consider a MAB model
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\left\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\right\},Y\right\rangle
graphically described in Fig.
10(a)
where SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is defined in Example
2
. In this model, the NUC condition does not hold due to unobserved confounder
U
affecting action
X
and reward
Y
. This implies that IPW is not necessarily applicable to recover the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
from the observational distribution
P(X,Y)
.

We will proceed regardless and try to learn a policy
\pi:X\leftarrow 0
. Applying Thm.
43
gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{ipw}}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid x\right]P(x)\frac{\mathds{1}\{x=0\}}{P(x)}

(151)

\displaystyle=\frac{P\left(X=0,Y=1\right)}{P(X=0)}.

(152)

Evaluating the above equation gives
\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{ipw}}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]=0
, which deviates significantly from the actual expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]=0.4
(Eq.
39
) evaluated in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

We also apply DP to evaluate the effect of pulling arm
X\leftarrow 0
, and through Thm.
45
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{dp}}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid x\right]\mathds{1}\{x=0\}

(153)

\displaystyle=P\left(Y=1\mid X=0\right)

(154)

Evaluating the above equation gives
\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{dp}}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]=0
, which, again, deviates from the actual expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]=0.4
(Eq.
39
) evaluated directly in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
\blacksquare

The above examples show that the validity of off-policy learning methods introduced so far hinges on the NUC assumption. Such a critical assumption could be fragile and does not necessarily hold in many practical settings. For instance, in electronic healthcare records, the physician might prescribe a new drug to patients who are more likely to access high-quality healthcare, thus making the drug appear more effective. For the remainder of this section, we will introduce alternative policy learning assumptions and methods to overcome this issue.

4.2
Online Learning

An online learning agent evaluates candidate policies in space
\Pi
by directly deploying them in the underlying environment. A temporal graph illustrating this interaction is described in Fig.
14
. In causal language, the agent intervenes in the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for repeated episodes
t=1,\dots,T
. For every episode
t
, it picks a policy
\pi^{(t)}\in\Pi
, performs interventions
\text{do}\left(\bm{X}\leftarrow\pi^{(t)}\right)
on actions
\bm{X}
following
\pi^{(t)}
, and receives subsequent observations
\bm{V}^{(t)}\sim P_{\pi^{(t)}}\left(\bm{V}\right)
.

Formally, an online learning task is described by the following signature:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{on}}=\left\langle\mathcal{R}=\text{do},\mathcal{A}=\emptyset,\Pi=\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\}_{i=1}^{H},\mathcal{R}=\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\right\rangle

(155)

To see the specific optimization in this task, the agent will search for a policy
\pi^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\pi^{*}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}\right)\;\big|\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\;\mathcal{D}_{\text{exp}}\sim P_{\bm{x}}\left(\bm{V}\right)}\right],

(156)

Compared with the off-policy learning task (
\mathcal{T}_{\text{off}}
), an online agent does not make additional structural assumptions about the underlying environment (
\mathcal{A}=\emptyset
), beyond the temporal ordering over state and action variables in the policy space
\Pi
(Def.
20
). This means that the NUC assumption (Def.
40
) discussed earlier does not necessarily hold.
Note that for every policy
\pi\in\Pi
, in the submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
induced by intervention
\text{do}(\pi)
, all input covariates
\bm{S}_{i}
affecting every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
and other variables in the system are observed and measured. This means that the NUC condition is implied in the post-interventional system. The following proposition formalizes this intuition.

Episode t

\pi^{(1)}

\bm{V}^{(3)}\sim P_{\pi^{(3)}}\left(\bm{V}\right)
\bm{V}^{(4)}\sim P_{\pi^{(4)}}\left(\bm{V}\right)

Figure 14
:
Temporal diagram showing an online learning agent interacting with the environment for repeated episodes.

Lemma 48

(Experimental NUC)
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a CDM where
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{X_{i}\in\bm{X}}
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. For any policy
\pi\in\Pi
, the NUC condition (Def.
40
) holds in
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
induced by intervention
\text{do}(\pi)
.
\blacksquare

Following the NUC condition, the result above can be seen as a formal justification for using standard off-policy learning methods, including IPW and DP, to evaluate other candidate policies
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi
from data drawn from the interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{V}\right)
through Thms.
43
and
45
.

Example 49
.

Consider the 2-stage DTR
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
where SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is described in Eq.
78
with coefficients
\beta_{i}=-3
; and the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{1},\{S_{1}\}\right\rangle,\left\langle X_{2},\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\}\right\rangle\right\}
. It has been shown in Example
42
that the NUC condition does not hold in this model. Now consider an online agent that is deployed in the environment and follows a policy
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, where

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\triangleq\mathds{1}\left\{3S_{i}+U_{i}>0\right\}

(157)

and
U_{i}
,
i=1,2
, are independent variable drawn from distribution
\texttt{Logistic}(0,1)
. Performing interventions
\text{do}(\pi)
following this policy leads to a submodel described by the following tuple

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U,U_{1},\dots,U_{5}\},\bm{V}=\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y\},\mathscr{F}_{\pi},P(\bm{U})\right\rangle,

(158)

where the structural functions
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\pi}=\begin{cases}S_{1}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{U_{3}>0\},\\
X_{1}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{3S_{1}+U_{1}>0\},\\
S_{2}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{0.1+0.1S_{1}+0.1X_{1}+U_{4}>0\},\\
X_{2}\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{3S_{2}+U_{2}>0\},\\
Y\leftarrow\mathds{1}\{3U-3S_{1}-3X_{1}-3S_{1}X_{1}+3X_{2}-3S_{2}X_{2}+3X_{1}X_{2}>0\}.\end{cases}

(159)

In the above equations, the unobserved confounder
U
no longer affects treatments
X_{1},X_{2}
, and the NUC condition holds in the submodel
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
. See Example
41
for a detailed discussion.
\blacksquare

4.2.1
Randomized Controlled Trials

We will discuss different algorithms that systematize the discussion above and operate over the environment in an online fashion.
The first algorithm we consider will be called
randomized controlled trials
(for short,
RCT
) and follows the idea of randomization, which dates back at least to
(
50
)
.
Fisher’s very motivation for considering randomizing the treatment assignment was to eliminate the influence of unmeasured confounders in the collected data.

25
25

25

Fisher’s motivation at the time was to understand the effect of some pesticides on the yield of certain crops
(
49
)
.
Farmers were biased in how they used pesticides which tended to be applied in the best parts of the land. At the end of the season, the pesticides had a higher effect.
Fisher was suspicious of this procedure since, in modern terminology, the NUC assumption did not hold.
He then had the idea of allocating the treatment randomly to the different plots of land.
This insight departed from a tradition led by Pearson
(
147
)
and started a new and fundamental discipline of
experimental design

(
50
)
.

It is a “explore-then-commit” strategy where the agent first explores the environment by determining values of actions
\bm{X}
uniformly at random for a fixed number of times, and then exploits by committing to a policy that appeared best during exploration.

Alg.
2
shows the detailed experimental design of
RCT
. It interacts with the underlying environment by repeated episodes of interventions
t=1,2,\dots
. More specifically, for every episode
t
,
RCT
selects a policy
\pi^{(t)}\in\Pi
, performs an intervention
\text{do}\left(\pi^{(t)}\right)
, and receives a subsequent observation
V^{(t)}\sim P_{\pi^{(t)}}\left(\bm{V}\right)
.
During the initialization, the algorithm considers a natural number
N\in\mathbb{N}
, called the
total number of trials
. It determines the total episodes of interventions for the algorithm to explore the environment before committing to a specific policy. For episode
t\leq N
, the algorithm selects a uniform policy
\pi_{\textsc{unif}}
which determines values of every action
X^{(t)}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,H
, uniformly at random during the exploration phase. Note that the NUC condition holds under
\text{do}(\pi_{\textsc{unif}})
intervention (Lem.
48
). This means that
RCT
could compute reward estimates
\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{\pi}[Y]
for candidate policies
\pi\in\Pi
from the experimental data
P_{\pi_{\textsc{unif}}}\left(\bm{V}\right)
collected during the exploration. The evaluation procedures were previously described in Thms.
43
and
45
. Finally,
RCT
selects a policy with the highest empirical reward estimates and commits to it for all future episodes
t>N
.

26
26

26

The
RCT
algorithm described in Alg.
2
is also referred to as sequential multiple assignment randomized trials (for short, SMART
(
131
)
) where every subject (at episode
t
) is randomized multiple times, one for each stage of decision
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
. When the decision horizon
H=1
, Alg.
2
reduces to the original randomized controlled trials introduced by
(
50
)
.

Algorithm 2

Randomized Controlled Trails (
RCT
)

1:

the policy space
\Pi
, the total number of trials
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

2:

for all
episodes
t=1,2,\dots

do

3:

Choose a policy
\pi^{(t)}
as follows.

4:

if

t\leq N

then

5:

Let
\pi^{(t)}
be a uniform policy

\displaystyle\pi_{\textsc{unif}}=\left(X_{1}\sim\texttt{Unif}(\mathscr{D}(X_{1})),\dots,X_{H}\sim\texttt{Unif}(\mathscr{D}(X_{H}))\right).

(160)

6:

else

7:

Let
\pi^{(t)}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{\pi}[Y]
.

8:

end

if

9:

Perform
\text{do}(\pi^{(t)})
for episode
t
and receive observations
\bm{V}^{(t)}
.

10:

end

for

Example 50
.

We illustrate
RCT
in an MAB model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\left\{\left\langle X,\emptyset\right\rangle\right\},Y\rangle
where
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is an SCM described in Eq.
3
consisting of an arm choice
X
and reward signal
Y
. For any policy
\pi(x)
, the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]
is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]=\pi(X=0)\mathbb{E}_{X\leftarrow 0}\left[Y\right]+\pi(X=1)\mathbb{E}_{X\leftarrow 1}\left[Y\right]

(161)

This means that for any stochastic policy
\pi(x)>0
,
\forall x\in\mathscr{D}(X)
, its performance could always be improved by a deterministic policy
X\leftarrow x^{*}
where the optimal arm choice given by

27
27

27

It can be shown that in any fixed CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
, the performance of a stochastic policy
\pi
could always be improved by a deterministic one. This means that one could optimize the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
over only deterministic policies
\pi\in\Pi
without loss of generality
(
115
, Lem. 2.1)
.

\displaystyle x^{*}=\argmax_{x\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]

(162)

It is thus sufficient to estimate the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
induced by atomic intervention
\text{do}(x)
.

Fix the total number of trials
N
(say,
N=1,000
). For every episode
t\leq N
,
RCT
selects an action
X^{(t)}
uniformly at random over the binary domain
\{0,1\}
, performs an intervention
\text{do}\left(X\leftarrow X^{(t)}\right)
, and receives a reward
Y^{(t)}\sim P_{X^{(t)}}\left(P\right)
.
When the exploration phase is done (
t>N
),
RCT
estimates the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
for every action
x\in\{0,1\}
from finite samples
\left\{X^{(t)},Y^{(t)}\right\}_{t=1,\dots,N}
. Applying the DP estimation formula (Thm.
45
) implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\textsc{unif}}}\left[Y|x\right]

(163)

The empirical reward estimate for an arm
x
is thus given by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x}[Y]=\frac{1}{N(x)}\sum_{t=1}^{N}Y^{(t)}\mathds{1}\{X^{(t)}=x\},

(164)

where
N(x)=\sum_{t=1}^{N}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{(t)}=x\right\}
is the total occurrence of event
X^{(t)}=x
up to episode
N
. We could also apply the IPW estimation (Thm.
43
) and obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\textsc{unif}}}\left[Y\mid x^{\prime}\right]\frac{\mathds{1}\{x^{\prime}=x\}}{\pi_{\textsc{unif}}\left(x\right)}

(165)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\texttt{unif}}}\left[Y\frac{\mathds{1}\{X=x\}}{\pi_{\textsc{unif}}\left(X\right)}\right]

(166)

The last step follows from the definition of expected values. Given samples
\left\{X^{(i)},Y^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1,\dots,N}
collected by
RCT
during exploration (
t<N
), the IPW empirical estimate for the expected reward of pulling arm
x
is thus given by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x}[Y]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t=1}^{N}Y^{(t)}\frac{\mathds{1}\{X^{(t)}=x\}}{\pi_{\textsc{unif}}\left(X^{(t)}\right)}=\frac{2}{N}\sum_{t=1}^{N}Y^{(t)}\mathds{1}\{X^{(t)}=x\}.

(167)

The last step holds since the uniform policy
\pi_{\textsc{unif}}(x)=1/2
for any action
x\in\{0,1\}
. The empirical estimates of DP (defined in Eq.
164
) and IPW (Eq.
167
) coincide if every arm
x\in\{0,1\}
is equally explored for episodes
t\leq N
, i.e., the total occurrences
N(x)=N/2
.
\blacksquare

Cumulative Regret

We will analyze the performance of
RCT
algorithm to understand its properties and theoretical guarantees better.
Our analysis will focus on an MAB model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}},\Pi,Y\rangle
, where
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}}
is an MAB environment graphically described in Fig.
10(a)
and the policy space
\Pi=\{\langle X,\emptyset\rangle\}
. Recall that the optimal arm
x^{*}=\argmax_{x}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
. We denote by
\Delta_{x}=\mathbb{E}_{x^{*}}\left[Y\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
the gap between the expected reward of playing a suboptimal action
x\in\mathscr{D}(X)
and the optimal arm
x^{*}
.
For the analysis’ convenience, we also assume that every arm
x\in\mathscr{D}(X)
is played the same amount of times during the exploration phase, i.e.,
N(x)=N/K
where
K=|\mathscr{D}(X)|
.

28
28

28

For a uniform policy
\pi_{\texttt{unif}}
, every arm
x
is expected to be played for
\mathbb{E}[N(x)]=N/K
on average during exploration.

There are several ways to measure the performance of online learning algorithms.
One popular criterion is to study the algorithm’s
cumulative regret

(
8
)
, which measures its cumulative loss relative to an optimal strategy that always selects the optimal arm
x^{*}
. Formally, the cumulative regret for an online learning algorithm in an MAB environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
after
T
episodes of trials can be defined as:

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})=\underbrace{T\mathbb{E}_{x^{*}}\left[Y;\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]}_{\text{Optimal Reward}}-\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{X^{(t)}}\left[Y;\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]}_{\text{Realized}}.

(168)

Naturally, minimizing the regret
R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})
is equivalent to maximizing the total expected reward the agent obtains.
A reasonable objective is to design an online learning algorithm that could achieve a sublinear regret, i.e.,
R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})=o(T)
.

29
29

29

Here we use
\mathcal{O}
notation, where
f(n)=\mathcal{O}(g(n))
if function
f
is bounded above by function
g
(up to constant factor) asymptotically. That is,
\exists k,\exists n_{0}
such that
f(n)\leq kg(n)
for
\forall n>n_{0}
. Similarly,
f(n)=o(g(n))
if
\exists k,\exists n_{0}
,
f(n)<kg(n)
for
\forall n>n_{0}
.
For further details on this notation, see
(
33
, Ch. 1.3)
.

This would imply that its average cumulative
regret per episode is converging to zero
(
97
)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})/T=0

(169)

The online learner will eventually close the gap between the optimal strategy that always commits to an optimal arm
x^{*}
.
In other words, the learner is choosing an optimal arm almost all the time as the total number of episodes
T
tends to be infinite, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X^{(t)}}\left[Y\right]\to\mathbb{E}_{x^{*}}\left[Y\right]

(170)

The analysis follows
(
97
, Theorem 6.1)
. Suppose that there are
\mathscr{D}(X)=\{1,\dots,K\}
possible arms. Observe that the
RCT
algorithm only incurs regret in episodes
t
where it plays a sub-optimal arm
x
with
\Delta_{x}>0
. The cumulative regret after
T>1
episodes of interventions could be written as:

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})=\sum_{x:\Delta_{x}>0}\Delta_{x}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{(t)}=x\right\}\right]

(171)

In the first
N
episodes, every arm is played exactly
N/K
times. Subsequently, it chooses a single action to maximize the empirical reward during exploration. This implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{(t)}=x\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{K}+(T-N)P\left(X^{(t)}=x\right)

(172)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{N}{K}+(T-N)P\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x}[Y]\neq\max_{x^{\prime}\neq x}\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x^{\prime}}[Y]\right)

(173)

The last step holds since for episodes
t\geq N
, a suboptimal arm
x\neq x^{*}
is picked if and only if its empirical reward estimate
\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x}[Y]
is maximal (i.e., the largest). The error probability could thus be bounded by

\displaystyle P\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x}[Y]\neq\max_{x^{\prime}\neq x}\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x^{\prime}}[Y]\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x}[Y]>\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x^{*}}[Y]\right)

(174)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x}[Y]-\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}_{x^{*}}[Y]-(\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x^{*}}\left[Y\right])>\Delta_{x}\right)

(175)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{N\Delta^{2}_{x}}{4K}\right)

(176)

The last step follows the standard concentration inequality
(
67
)
. Replacing the error probability Eq.
176
into Eq.
173
and summing over regret gives the following bound.

Theorem 51

(Regrets of
RCT

(
97
)
)
.

For an MAB
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\left\{\left\langle X,\emptyset\right\rangle\right\},Y\rangle
, let
Y\in\bm{V}
be the reward variable with support on
[0,1]
and let the domain of action
X
be
\mathscr{D}(X)=\{1,\dots,K\}
. Fix the total number of trials
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. The regret of
RCT
in MAB
\mathcal{M}^{*}
after
T>1
episodes of interventions is bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq\underbrace{\frac{N}{K}\sum_{x:\Delta_{x}>0}\Delta_{x}}_{\text{exploration}}+\underbrace{(T-N)\sum_{x:\Delta_{x}>0}\Delta_{x}\exp\left(-\frac{N\Delta^{2}_{x}}{4K}\right)}_{\text{exploitation}}

(177)

\blacksquare

The regret bound in Thm.
51
illustrates a trade-off between the exploration and the exploitation stages of the agent’s strategy. The first term is the regret cumulated during the exploration (
t\leq N
), and the second term is the expected regret of
RCT
for picking a suboptimal arm during the exploitation stage.
If the total number of trials
N
is large, the algorithm explores too long, and the regret cumulated during the exploration phase will be large. On the other hand, if
N
is too small, then the empirical estimate
\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{(N)}[Y]
is more likely to deviate from the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
, and the regret in the exploitation phase increases.

One fundamental question is, therefore, how to choose the optimal number of trials
N
to balance the amount of exploration versus exploitation. Assume that the number of arms
K=2
and the optimal arm
x^{*}=1
, and write
\Delta=\Delta_{2}
. The bound in Eq.
177
simplifies to

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq\frac{N}{2}\Delta+T\Delta\exp\left(-\frac{N\Delta^{2}}{8}\right).

(178)

For a large
T
, the right-hand side of Eq.
178
is minimized up to a rounding error by

\displaystyle N=\left\lceil{\frac{8}{\Delta^{2}}\log\left(\frac{T\Delta^{2}}{4}\right)}\right\rceil.

(179)

For this choice and any
T>1
, after a few simplifications, the regret of
RCT
is bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq\Delta+C\sqrt{T}

(180)

where
C
is a universal constant. That is,
RCT
is able to achieve a sublinear regret
R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})=\mathcal{O}\left(T^{1/2}\right)
by fine-tuning the total number of trials
N
.
However, note that the choice of
N
in Eq.
179
depends on the suboptimal gap
\Delta
and the total number of episodes
T
, which are not necessarily known in advance. In the next section, we will see an online algorithm that does not depend on the prior knowledge of the model parameter
\Delta
and the total episodes
T
.

Figure 15
:
The regret of
RCT
with varying total number of trials.

Experiment 1
.

Fig.
15
shows the cumulative regrets of
RCT
when deploying in the MAB model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\left\{\left\langle X,\emptyset\right\rangle\right\},Y\rangle
described in Example
50
where the optimal arm choice
X\leftarrow 0
and the suboptimal gap
\Delta=0.1
. The optimal arm choice is
X\leftarrow 0
, as shown in the derivation in Example
12
.

Recall that the
T
represents the total number of episodes that the
RCT
algorithm interacts with the environment, and the total number trials
N
represents the amount of exploration it performs (out of
T
episodes). We evaluate the
RCT
algorithm with the number of episodes set to
T=5,000
and the number of trials set to
N=100,300,500,700,900
.
Each data point is the average of
1,000
simulations, which makes the error bars invisible.
The simulations show that
RCT
with
N=500
performs the best among all strategies, which is close to the analytical result in Eq.
179
, setting
N\approx 878
.
This means Eq.
179
provides a near-optimal choice of the total number of trials.
\blacksquare

4.2.2
The Upper Confidence Bound Algorithm

The upper confidence bound (UCB) algorithm is based on the principle of
optimism in the face of uncertainty
(OFU,
9
), which states that the agent should act as if the environment is as close to the best-case scenario as possible, given past observations.
It offers several advantages over
RCT
introduced in the previous section, which we summarize below:

•

It does not depend on the prior knowledge of the parametrization of the underlying environment, i.e., the gap
\Delta
in the expected rewards between an optimal and a suboptimal policy.

•

It does not rely on prior knowledge of the total number of episodes
T
that the online algorithm will intervene in the underlying environment.

•

It achieves the same theoretical guarantees as
RCT
that fine-tunes the total number of trials
N
based on prior knowledge of the suboptimal gap
\Delta
and the total episodes
T
.

The insight of the
UCB
algorithm is to evoke an
adaptive randomization
strategy
(
153
;
92
;
24
)
, which means that the agent repeatedly adjusts the probability of action assignment according to the past assigned actions and observed outcomes during the experimentation. To make the argument more precise, for an MAB model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\left\{\left\langle X,\emptyset\right\rangle\right\},Y\rangle
,
UCB
will utilize the prior actions and rewards’ history to compute an
upper confidence bound
to each arm
x
, which is an overestimate of the unknown expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
with high probability.

In order to better understand the construction of the confidence bound, we need to introduce some basic concentration results. Let
\left\{Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(n)}\right\}
be finite i.i.d. reward signals drawn from a discrete distribution
P(Y)
. Let empirical estimates be
\hat{\mathbb{E}}[Y]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}Y^{(i)}
. Applying Hoeffding’s inequalities
(
67
)
on the reward signal
Y
bounded in a real interval
[0,1]
gives

\displaystyle P\left(\mathbb{E}[Y]\geq\hat{\mathbb{E}}[Y]+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{2n}}\right)\leq\delta\;\;\;\text{for all }\delta\in(0,1)

(181)

Fix a sequence of arm selections
x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(t)}\in\mathscr{D}(X)
. Let
N_{t}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathds{1}\{X^{(t)}=x\}
be the total occurrence of arm
x
being played for every
x\in\mathscr{D}(X)
. Given finite samples
\left\{Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(t)}\right\}
drawn from interventional distributions
P_{x^{(1)}}\left(Y\right),\dots,P_{x^{(t)}}\left(Y\right)
respectively, it follows from Eq.
181
that the upper confidence bound for the reward
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
is defined as
30
30

30

For consistency, we also define
\text{UCB}_{t}(x,\delta)=\infty
if
N_{t}(x)=0
.

\displaystyle\text{UCB}_{t}(x,\delta)=\underbrace{\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{x}[Y]}_{exploration}+\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{2N_{t}(x)}}}_{exploitation}

(182)

Among quantities in the above equation, the second term is the confidence width computed from the concentration bound in Eq.
181
. The first term is the empirical mean estimate
\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{x}[Y]
for the expected reward and is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{x}[Y]=\frac{1}{N_{t}(x)}\sum_{i=1}^{t}Y^{(i)}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{(i)}=x\right\}

(183)

We summarize in Alg.
3
the details of the
UCB
algorithm when deployed in an unknown MAB model
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\textsc{mab}}
.
For every episode
t
, it computes confidence bounds
\text{UCB}_{t-1}(x,\delta)
for every arm
x\in\mathscr{D}(X)
from prior interventional data
\left\{Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(t-1)}\right\}
.
It then selects an arm
x^{(t)}
with the maximal upper confidence bound, performs intervention
\text{do}\left(x^{(t)}\right)
, and receives subsequent reward
Y^{(t)}
.
It can be shown that such an arm allocation strategy based on the upper confidence bound in Eq.
182
balances the trade-off between exploration and exploitation.
The algorithm is more likely to play an arm
x
if it is (1) close to optimal since the empirical reward estimate
\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{x}[Y]
is large, or (2) not sufficiently explored and
N_{x}(t)
is small.
At Step
3
, the error probability
\delta=t^{-4}
decreases as the episode number
t
increases.
This means that the upper confidence bound estimates for every arm
x
become increasingly accurate as the online learning process continues.

Algorithm 3

Upper Confidence Bound (
UCB
) in MAB

1:

a policy scope
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X,\emptyset\right\rangle\right\}
.

2:

for all
episodes
t=1,2,\dots

do

3:

Choose an arm
x^{(t)}=\argmax_{x}\text{UCB}_{t-1}\left(x,\delta\right)
where
\delta=t^{-4}
.

4:

Perform
\text{do}\left(x^{(t)}\right)
for episode
t
and receive reward
Y^{(t)}
.

5:

end

for

Theorem 52

(Regrets of
UCB
in MABs
(
9
)
)
.

For an MAB
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\left\{\left\langle X,\emptyset\right\rangle\right\},Y\rangle
, let
Y
be the reward variable with support on
[0,1]
, and let the domain of action
X
be
\mathscr{D}(X)=\{1,\dots,K\}
. It holds the regret of
UCB
in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
after
T>1
episodes is bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq 8\sum_{x:\Delta_{x}>0}\frac{\log(T)}{\Delta_{x}}+\left(1+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\right)\sum_{x:\Delta_{x}>0}\Delta_{x}

(184)

\blacksquare

After a few simplifications
(
97
)
, the regret bound in Eq.
184
could be further written as:

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 5\sum_{x:\Delta_{x}>0}\Delta_{x}+C\sqrt{KT\log(T)}

(185)

where
C
is a universal constant. In words,
UCB
achieves a sublinear regret
\mathcal{O}\left(T^{1/2}\log(T)^{1/2}\right)
, which is close to the regret bound obtained by
RCT
up to logarithmic terms. By employing sharper concentration inequalities for the expected reward estimates, it is possible to shave the dependence on the logarithmic term in the regret bound of
UCB

(
7
)
.
Broadly speaking, the theory supports the claim that
UCB
is able to overcome the limitation of
RCT
by removing the dependence on the prior knowledge of suboptimal gaps
\Delta
and the total number of episodes
T
while achieving the same asymptotic guarantees. Still, in practice, two algorithms’ having similar regret bounds does not mean they will perform the same when deployed in the environment.
The reason is that the analysis might be loose for one algorithm and not to the other, or by a different margin. For this reason, we now compare the empirical performance of
UCB
and
RCT
.

Experiment 2
.

Fig.
16
shows the cumulative regret of
UCB
when deploying in the MAB model described in Example
50
. The setup is the same as in Experiment
1
, which has
T=10000
and parameter
\alpha=0.1
. As a baseline, we also include
RCT
with various choices for the total number of trials set to
N
. The simulation shows a common phenomenon – if
RCT
is fine-tuned with the optimal choice of the trial number, it can outperform
UCB
by a small margin in the cumulative regret. However, if the trial number
N
must be chosen without prior knowledge of the model parameter
\Delta
and the total episodes of interventions
T
,
UCB
will generally dominate
RCT
in performance.
\blacksquare

Figure 16
:
Simulation results comparing performance of online learning algorithms
UCB
and
RCT
.

The principle underpinning
UCB
has been applied to other RL environments and leads to sublinear regrets, including contextual bandit
(
108
)
, Markov decision processes
(
11
)
, and factored MDPs
(
139
)
, just to name a few. In Sec.
5
, we will also see a more generalized implementation of
UCB
that could find an optimal policy in an arbitrary CRL system.

4.3
Causal Identification

Online learning algorithms ensure the NUC condition (Def.
40
) holds by deploying candidate policies in the environment (Lem.
48
).
However, randomized experiments are not applicable in all settings; for example, the effect of a risk factor such as smoking cannot ethically be addressed with randomized controlled trials
(
35
)
.
Furthermore, performing randomized experiments is expensive, and may even be infeasible due to financial constraints. For instance, in a survey of phase trials of new drugs approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) of the United States from 2015-2016
(
123
)
, the trial cost was estimated at a median of $41,117 per patient. The increasing cost of certain trials calls for more generalized policy learning methods without direct interventions.

31
31

31

The situation is more challenging in practice since even in settings where RCTs are applicable, there are still serious concerns due to issues of transportability (also known in the literature as external validity/generalizability). For further discussion, refer to
(
18
;
37
)
.

An alternative approach for policy evaluation is to relax the NUC assumption and explore other more general structural assumptions in the underlying environment, represented as a causal diagram. This leads to the setting of causal identification, characterized by a signature:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{id}}=\left\langle\mathcal{R}=\text{see},\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G},\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H},\mathcal{R}=\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\right\rangle

(186)

The optimization in this task goes as follows:

\displaystyle\pi^{*}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}\right)\;\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G},\;\mathcal{D}_{\text{obs}}\sim P(\bm{V})}\right],

(187)

Similar to off-policy learning previously discussed in Sec.
4.1
, a causal identification agent collected data from the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
through repeated episodes of passive observations (
\mathcal{I}=\text{see}
). or every episode
t
, the agent observes the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, and receives a sample drawn from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
. The key difference is that the agent does not assume the NUC condition (Def.
40
). Instead, it now has access to a causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
associated with the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Def.
16
).
32
32

32

There exist causal discovery algorithms to learn (an equivalence class of) causal diagrams from observational
(
172
;
146
)
and
experimental data
(
83
;
82
;
74
)
.
33
33

33

This settings can be generalized to consider input distributions in which the other agent is known to have collected data under a randomized regime, albeit of another action variable different than
\bm{X}
, say
\bm{Z}
. This problem has been studied in the literature under the rubric of g-identification
(
14
;
105
)
.

It will then incorporate experts’ domain knowledge to obtain a causal diagram compatible with the underlying environment. See Sec.
2.3
for more discussion. This interaction is illustrated in the temporal diagram in Fig.
17
.

Episode t
X
(1)
Y
(1)
Z
(1)
X
(2)
Y
(2)
Z
(2)
X
(3)
Y
(3)
Z
(3)
X
(4)
Y
(4)
Z
(4)
\bm{V}^{(1)}\sim P\left(\bm{V}\right)
\bm{V}^{(2)}\sim P\left(\bm{V}\right)
\bm{V}^{(3)}\sim P\left(\bm{V}\right)
\bm{V}^{(4)}\sim P\left(\bm{V}\right)
0
1
2
3
see
see
see
see

Figure 17
:
Temporal diagram showing a causal identification agent interacting with the environment for repeated episodes.

The agent aims to learn an optimal policy
\pi^{*}\in\Pi
from the combination of the observational data
P(\bm{V})
and structural assumptions encoded in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
. A key challenge is to find a function of the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
that is guaranteed to be equal to the probability query of interest in the intervened submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\pi}
, for any SCM
\mathcal{M}
compatible with structural assumptions encoded in the diagram
\mathcal{G}
. We introduce next this notion articulated more formally.

Definition 53

(Identifiability)
.

Let subsets of variables
\bm{X},\bm{Y}\subseteq\bm{V}
and
\pi
be a policy over
\bm{X}
.
The interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
is identifiable from the structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
if
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
is uniquely computable from any positive observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
in any SCM
\mathcal{M}
satisfying
\mathcal{A}
. That is, if for every pair of SCMs
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
compatible with structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
,
P_{\pi}(\bm{Y};\mathcal{M}_{1})=P_{\pi}(\bm{Y};\mathcal{M}_{2})
whenever
P(\bm{V};\mathcal{M}_{1})=P(\bm{V};\mathcal{M}_{2})>0
.

For a causal identification task, the structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
will be encoded as a causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
. We say an interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
is identifiable from a causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
if for any pair of SCMs
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}
,
P_{\pi}(\bm{Y};\mathcal{M}_{1})=P_{\pi}(\bm{Y};\mathcal{M}_{2})
whenever
P(\bm{V};\mathcal{M}_{1})=P(\bm{V};\mathcal{M}_{2})>0
and
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{M}_{1})=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{M}_{2})=\mathcal{G}
. On the other hand, as for an off-policy learning task described in Sec.
4.1
, the structural assumptions
\mathcal{A}
are specified using the NUC condition (Def.
40
), which restricts the form of the behavior policy
f_{\bm{X}}
determining values of actions
\bm{X}
in the underlying environment. It follows as a corollary from Thms.
43
and
45
that for any policy
\pi\in\Pi
, the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
is identifiable provided with the NUC condition (Def.
40
).

Corollary 54
.

For a policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and reward function
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, let a policy
\pi\in\Pi
.
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
is identifiable from the NUC condition (Def.
40
) w.r.t. the policy space
\Pi
.

The following example shows the identifiability guarantee from the NUC assumption.

Example 55

(Identification under NUC)
.

We consider a 2-stage DTR
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
described in Fig.
10(c)
where the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{1},\left\{S_{1}\right\}\right\rangle,\left\langle X_{2},\left\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\right\}\right\rangle\right\}
. We further assume that the NUC condition (Def.
40
) holds, i.e., the unobserved confounder
U
does not affect actions
X_{1},X_{2}
. This means that for every action
X_{1}
(or
X_{2}
), when its direct parents
S_{1}
(or
S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}
) are observed and measured, its values are only affected by independent noise.

It follows from IPW estimation of Thm.
43
that the expected reward of any policy
\pi\in\Pi
is computable from the observational distribution and policy’s definition, and is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]=\sum_{s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},s_{2}}\underbrace{\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},s_{2}\right]P(s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},s_{2})}{P(x_{1}|s_{1})P(x_{2}|s_{1},x_{1},s_{2})}}_{\text{observational distribution}}\underbrace{\pi_{1}(x_{1}|s_{1})\pi_{1}(x_{2}|s_{1},x_{1},s_{2})}_{\text{policy $\pi$}}.

(188)

The above equation is a function of the candidate policy
\pi
(the second term) and the observational distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y)
(the first term).
Formally, the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]
is identifiable from
P(\bm{V})
provided with the NUC condition, i.e., no matter the specific form of the mechanisms
\bm{F}
and exogenous conditions
P(\bm{u})
of the underlying, true SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. We also compute the value function of policy
\pi\in\Pi
following the DP estimation in Thm.
45
:

\displaystyle Q^{(1)}_{\pi}(x_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{2},x_{2}}Q^{(2)}_{\pi}(x_{1},x_{2},s_{1},s_{2})P(s_{2}|s_{1},x_{1})\pi_{2}(x_{2}\mid s_{1},x_{1},s_{2})

(189)

\displaystyle Q^{(2)}_{\pi}(x_{1},x_{2},s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y|s_{1},x_{1},s_{2},s_{2}].

(190)

Finally, the expected reward is identifiable by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]=\sum_{s_{1},x_{1}}Q^{(1)}_{\pi}(x_{1},s_{1})\pi_{1}(x_{1}\mid s_{1})P(s_{1}),

(191)

and value functions
Q^{(1)}_{\pi},Q^{(2)}_{\pi}
are both computable from distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y)
.
\blacksquare

4.3.1
Sequential Backdoor for Policy Evaluation

As previously discussed in Sec.
4.1
, both IPW (Thm.
43
) and DP (Thm.
45
) are popular off-policy evaluation algorithms in causal inference and reinforcement learning literature.
Therefore, it is worth understanding the conditions under which these algorithms are applicable for identifying the expected rewards of policies in a policy space
\Pi
, provided with an arbitrary causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
.
There exists a graphical condition called he
sequential backdoor

(
141
)
that delimits whether the effect of performing a sequence of atomic interventions can be identified by covariates adjustments.
In this section, we generalize the sequential backdoor criterion to evaluate the effects of sequential policies (i.e., not necessarily atomic
34
34

34

For a more nuanced and detailed discussion on the difference between atomic and non-atomic interventions, please refer to
(
36
, Appendix B)
.
), which select actions based on values of other observed covariates in the system.

Before describing the details of the criterion, we first introduce some necessary notations. For every policy
\pi\in\Pi
, let
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
denote the causal diagram associated with the submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
induced by policy intervention
\text{do}(\pi)
. Operationally, the manipulated diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
is obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by performing the following procedures: for every
i=1,\dots,H
,

1.

Remove all incoming arrows pointing into action node
X_{i}
;

2.

Add arrows from nodes in input states
\bm{S}_{i}
to the action node
X_{i}
.

For a policy
\pi=\left(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{H}\right)
, let
\left(\pi_{i},\dots,\pi_{j}\right)
be a sub-policy consisting of decision rules with restriction to indices
1\leq i<j\leq H
. The manipulated graph
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{i},\dots,\pi_{j}}
is thus a causal diagram obtained by intervention
\text{do}\left(\pi_{i},\dots,\pi_{j}\right)
following the sub-policy
\left(\pi_{i},\dots,\pi_{j}\right)
.
35
35

35

Note that for
i=1,\dots,H
, covariates
\bm{S}_{i}
are non-descendent of actions
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i:H}
. It is verifiable that for every policy
\pi\in\Pi
, the manipulated graph
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{i+1},\dots,\pi_{H}}
,
i=1\dots,H
, is acyclic and preserves the topological ordering
\bm{S}_{1}\prec X_{1}\prec\dots\prec\bm{S}_{H}\prec X_{H}
.
As an example, consider the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
described Fig.
18(a)
. For a policy
\left(\pi_{1}\left(X_{1}\mid S_{1}\right),\pi_{2}\left(X_{2}\mid S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\right)\right)
, Fig.
18(b)
shows the manipulated causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2}}
induced by intervention
\text{do}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
; the added incoming arrows to actions
X_{1},X_{2}
are highlighted in blue. The diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{2}}
induced by intervention
\text{do}(\pi_{2})
following a sub-policy with restriction on action
X_{2}
is shown in Fig.
18(c)
. Formally, the sequential backdoor condition for identifying policy interventions is defined as follows.

Definition 56

(Sequential Backdoor Condition (Policy Intervention))
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be causal diagram and
\bm{Y}\subseteq\bm{V}
be reward signals. A policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
is said to satisfy the
sequential backdoor condition
w.r.t.
\bm{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
(for short,
\Pi
is backdoor admissible) if for every policy
\pi\in\Pi
, the following condition hold: for every
i=1,\dots,H
,

\displaystyle\left(X_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\bm{Y}\mid\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}\right)_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{i}},\pi_{i+1},\dots,\pi_{H}}}

(192)

That is, conditioning on nodes
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}\cup\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
d-separates all
backdoor paths
from action
X_{i}
to reward signals
\bm{Y}
that contains an arrow pointing into
X_{i}
in the manipulated graph
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{i+1},\dots,\pi_{H}}
.

In spirit, the independence relationship in Eq.
192
is similar to the celebrated backdoor criterion
(
146
, Def. 3.3.1)
. A backdoor path between nodes
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
is a sequence of edges starting with an arrow pointing into a node in
\bm{X}
. This criterion ensures that at every stage
i=1,\dots,H
, the actions and covariates’ history
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
effectively summarize all the information that the behavior policy uses to determine values of action
X_{i}
, which are also relevant to the reward signal
Y
.
In other words, all the confounders between action
X_{i}
and reward
Y
are measured, and no other variables could generate non-causal correlations between
X_{i}
and
Y
.

As a special case, let
\pi=\left(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{H}\right)
be an atomic policy such that every decision rule
\pi_{i}\triangleq X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}
for every step
i=1,\dots,H
. In this case, the manipulated graph
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{i+1},\dots,\pi_{H}}
,
i=1,\dots,H
, is a subgraph obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by removing the incoming arrows of every action node
X_{i+1},\dots,X_{H}
. The independence relationship in Eq.
192
reduces to the sequential backdoor condition for atomic interventions
\text{do}(\bm{x})
in
(
141
)
and could be written as:

\displaystyle\left(X_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\bm{Y}\mid\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}\right)_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{i}},\overline{X_{i+1},\dots,X_{H}}}}.

(193)

Note that in the above equation,
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{i}},\overline{X_{i+1},\dots,X_{H}}}
is a subgraph contained in the manipulated diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{i}},\pi_{i+1},\dots,\pi_{H}}
(w.r.t. an arbitrary policy
\pi
over actions
\bm{X}
). This means that the independence condition in Eq.
192
is stronger than the one in Eq.
193
. The atomic backdoor condition in
(
141
)
holds whenever the policy space
\Pi
is backdoor admissible in diagram
\mathcal{G}
.

Whenever the NUC condition (Def.
40
) holds, note that the variables
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
contain all direct parents of action
X_{i}
. There is no unobserved confounder affecting
X_{i}
and any other variable in the environment.
Conditioning on the actions and states’ history
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
thus “blocks” all backdoor path from action node
X_{i}
to reward nodes
\bm{Y}
. As a corollary, it follows immediately that Def.
56
subsumes the NUC condition (Def.
40
).

Corollary 57
.

For a CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
where
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{X_{i}\in\bm{X}}
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathcal{G}
be the causal diagram associated with SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. If the NUC holds in
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
, then
\Pi
is backdoor admissible w.r.t reward signals
\bm{Y}
in diagram
\mathcal{G}
.

Whenever the NUC condition holds in a CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
, the above corollary implies that the policy space
\Pi
must satisfy the sequential backdoor with regard to reward signals
\bm{Y}
in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
of the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. The following example demonstrates this intuition.

Example 58

(NUC
\Rightarrow
Sequential Backdoor)
.

Consider a 2-stage DTR model
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\right\rangle
consisting of actions
X_{1},X_{2}
, observed states
S_{1},S_{2}
, and a primary outcome
Y
; the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{1},\left\{S_{1}\right\}\right\rangle,\left\langle X_{2},\left\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\right\}\right\rangle\right\}
. Assume that the NUC condition holds (Def.
40
), which means that the unobserved confounder
U
does not affect actions
X_{1},X_{2}
. Fig.
18(a)
shows a more detailed causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
associated with the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
; the incoming arrows
U\rightarrow X_{1}
,
U\rightarrow X_{2}
are now removed.

We will next show that the policy space
\Pi
satisfies sequential backdoor condition with regard to the primary outcome
Y
in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
. Consider first the action
X_{1}
. For every policy
\left(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\right)\in\Pi
, the manipulated diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{2}}
is shown in Fig.
18(c)
. One could see by inspection that conditioning on covariate
S_{1}

d
-separates all backdoor paths from
X_{1}
to
Y
in
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{2}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left(X_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid S_{1}\right)_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{1}},\pi_{2}}}

(194)

We also examine the independence relationship of Eq.
192
with regard to
X_{2}
, which is the last action in the decision sequence. It is thus sufficient to consider the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
of Fig.
18(a)
. Again, conditioning input covariates
S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}

d
-separates backdoor paths from
X_{2}
to
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left(X_{2}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid S_{1},X_{1},S_{2}\right)_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{2}}}}

(195)

We thus conclude the policy space
\Pi
is backdoor-admissible w.r.t. the primary outcome
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
.
\blacksquare

Interestingly, Def.
56
covers more general conditions than the NUC. There are CDMs
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
where the NUC does not hold while the policy space
\Pi
satisfies the sequential backdoor condition in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
associated with the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

Example 59

(Sequential Backdoor
\not\Rightarrow
NUC)
.

More formally, consider an SCM

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{5},\bm{V}=\{S_{1},S_{2},X_{1},X_{2},Y\},\mathscr{F},P(\bm{U})\right\rangle

(196)

consisting of actions
X_{1},X_{2}
and a reward signal
Y
. The causal mechanisms
\mathscr{F}
are defined as:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}=\begin{cases}S_{1}\leftarrow U_{1},\\
X_{1}\leftarrow U_{1}\oplus U_{4},\\
S_{2}\leftarrow S_{1}\oplus X_{1}\oplus U_{2},\\
X_{2}\leftarrow U_{2}\oplus U_{5},\\
Y\leftarrow S_{1}\oplus X_{1}\oplus U_{3},\end{cases}

(197)

The exogenous distribution
P(\bm{U})
is defined such that
U_{1},U_{2},U_{3}
are independent binary variables following distribution
P(U_{i}=0)=0.9
,
i=1,\dots,3
; also,
U_{4},U_{5}
are independent noise uniformly drawn over
\{0,1\}
. Fig.
19(a)
shows the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
associated with the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

S
1

1
S

(a)

\mathcal{G}

\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2}}

Figure 19
:
Causal diagram satisfying the sequential backdoor and its manipulated diagrams.

Consider a policy space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{1},\{S_{1}\}\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{S_{1}\}\rangle\right\}
. One could see by inspection that the NUC condition does not hold in the CRL system
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\rangle
due to the presence of unobserved confounders
U_{i}
,
i=1,2
, affecting action (
X_{i}
) and state (
S_{i}
) variables, simultaneously. We next examine the scope
\Pi
and see if it satisfies the sequential backdoor criterion in the diagram
\mathcal{G}
. For a policy
\left(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\right)\in\Pi
, the manipulated diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{2}}
is shown in Fig.
19(c)
. Conditioning on the covariate
S_{1}

d
-separates the backdoor path
X_{1}\dashleftarrow\dashrightarrow S_{1}\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{2}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left(X_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid S_{1}\right)_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{1}},\pi_{2}}}

(198)

Similarly, conditioning on
S_{1}
also blocks the backdoor path
X_{2}\dashleftarrow\dashrightarrow S_{2}\leftarrow X_{1}\dashleftarrow\dashrightarrow S_{1}\rightarrow Y
between action
X_{2}
and reward
Y
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left(X_{2}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid S_{1}\right)_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{2}}}}

(199)

The above independence relationships imply
\Pi
satisfies the sequential backdoor w.r.t. the reward
Y
in the diagram
\mathcal{G}
even when the NUC does not hold in the decision model
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\rangle
.
\blacksquare

One important observation here is that the NUC condition is hard to ascertain unless implied by the (physical) randomization procedure or assumptions following the model, such as the ones required by the sequential back-door condition.
This means that despite the NUC condition being popular throughout the literature, the same is not a primitive but a byproduct of more fundamental notions. Our next result establishes the identifiability of the effects of candidate policies in
\Pi
, provided that the policy scope
\Pi
is backdoor-admissible in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
.

Theorem 60
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal diagram,
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
be a policy space, and
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a reward function. If
\Pi
is backdoor-admissible w.r.t.
\bm{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
(Def.
56
), for any policy
\pi\in\Pi
, the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
is identifiable from
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
is computable from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
following the IPW (Thm.
43
) or the DP (Thm.
45
) estimation.
\blacksquare

In words, the sequential backdoor condition in Def.
56
generalizes standard off-policy learning methods to settings where the NUC does not hold, and there exist unobserved confounders affecting actions and other variables in the system. As long as the sequential backdoor holds, one could apply IPW and DP algorithms to evaluate candidate policies from the observational data while ascertaining the validity of the estimation procedure in the limit.

Example 61
.

Consider again the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Eq.

. We are interested in evaluating a policy
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
such that
\pi_{1}:X_{1}\leftarrow S_{1},\pi_{2}:X_{2}\leftarrow\neg S_{1}
. The submodel entailed by intervention
\text{do}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is described by the tuple

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{5},\bm{V}=\{S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y\},\mathscr{F}_{\pi},P(\bm{U})\right\rangle,

(200)

where the structural functions
\mathscr{F}_{\pi}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\pi}=\begin{cases}S_{1}\leftarrow U_{1},\\
X_{1}\leftarrow S_{1},\\
S_{2}\leftarrow S_{1}\oplus X_{1}\oplus U_{2},\\
X_{2}\leftarrow\neg S_{1},\\
Y\leftarrow S_{1}\oplus X_{1}\oplus U_{3},\end{cases}

(201)

Evaluating the expected reward
Y
in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
results in

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X_{1}\leftarrow S_{1},X_{2}\leftarrow\neg S_{1}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[S_{1}\oplus\neg S_{1}\oplus U_{3}\right]

(202)

\displaystyle=P(0\oplus U_{3}=1)

(203)

Evaluating the above equation gives

. Since the agent does not have access to
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, we apply next the IPW estimation procedure (Thm.
43
) to evaluate the effects of policy
\pi=(X_{1}\leftarrow S_{1},X_{2}\leftarrow\neg S_{1})

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{ipw}}_{X_{1}\leftarrow S_{1},X_{2}\leftarrow\neg S_{1}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},x_{1},x_{2}}P\left(s_{1},x_{1},x_{2},Y=1\right)\frac{\mathds{1}\{x_{1}=s_{1}\}}{P^{\prime}\left(x_{1}\mid s_{1}\right)}\frac{\mathds{1}\{x_{2}=\neg s_{1}\}}{P\left(x_{2}\mid s_{1},x_{1}\right)}

\displaystyle=4\sum_{s_{1},x_{1},x_{2}}P\left(s_{1},x_{1},x_{2},Y=1\right)\mathds{1}\{x_{1}=s_{1},x_{2}=\neg s_{1}\}

,

; and
U_{4}
,
U_{5}
are independent noise uniformly drawn over
\{0,1\}
. The complete parametrization for the observational distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},X_{2},Y)
is provided in Table
8
. The above equation could thus be further written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\textsc{ipw}}_{X_{1}\leftarrow S_{1},X_{2}\leftarrow\neg S_{2}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=4P(S_{1}=0,X_{1}=0,X_{2}=1,Y=1)

\displaystyle+4P^{\prime}(S_{1}=1,X_{1}=1,X_{2}=0,Y=1)

(205)

The above evaluation matches the expected reward in Eq.
203
, evaluated in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
\blacksquare
