:
Simulation results comparing
UCB
+
learner augmented with causal bounds over the expected rewards, standard
UCB
, and
UCB
-
with direct transfer of observational data.

Experiment 4
.

Fig.
25
shows the cumulative regret of
UCB
+
in the MAB environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Example
2
with the suboptimal gap
\Delta=0.3
and
\Delta=0.1
respectively. It takes as input the natural bounds
\left[l_{x},r_{x}\right]
over the expected rewards of arm
x=0,1
, computed from taking as input
5,000
observational samples drawn from the distribution
P(X,Y)
. As a baseline, we also include a vanilla
UCB
starting from scratch without utilizing any prior observations, and
UCB
-
with the confounded observational data directly transferred.

One can see by inspection the significant disparity between the performance of
UCB
+
and
UCB
for
\Delta=0.3
. In this case, the causal bound for the suboptimal arm
x=1
is
r_{1}=0.6-\Delta=0.3<\mu_{0}
, and
UCB
+
converges to the optimal arm
x=0
almost immediately after the learning starts. On the other hand, when the suboptimal gap
\Delta=0.1
and the causal bound
r_{1}=0.6-\Delta=0.5>\mu_{0}
, the performance of
UCB
+
and
UCB
virtually coincides. The simulation results corroborate the theory that the transfer strategy of
UCB
+
enjoys no negative impact. As expected, the direct transfer
UCB
-
performs the worst among all strategies due to unobserved confounding.
\blacksquare

5.2
Online Learning in Sequential Decision-Making

The offline-to-online learning strategy described so far focuses on the MAB models with a single decision horizon
H=1
.
The remainder of this section will extend this strategy to optimize a general CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
where the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
taking a set of signals
\bm{Y}
as input. We will first introduce a purely online learning algorithm to optimize a CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
without detailed parametrization of the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Compared to bandit algorithms previously described for MAB models, our proposed algorithm also interacts with the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for repeated episodes
t=1,\dots,T
. For each episode
t
, instead of selecting a single arm
X
, our algorithm will determine values of a sequence of actions
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
. More specifically, it will pick a policy
\pi^{(t)}=\left(\pi^{(t)}_{1},\dots,\pi^{(t)}_{H}\right)
at the beginning of episode
t
. For every step
i=1,\dots,H
of interventions, our algorithm observed state
\bm{S}^{(t)}_{i}
, selects an action
X^{(t)}_{i}\sim\pi^{(t)}_{i}\left(X_{i}\mid\bm{S}^{(t)}_{i}\right)
following the decision rule
\pi^{(t)}_{i}
, and perform intervention
\text{do}\left(X_{i}\leftarrow X^{(t)}_{i}\right)
. The cumulative regret for an online learning algorithm operating in a CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
after
T>1
episodes of interventions is defined as follows, compared to an idealized agent following the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
for all episodes of interactions
t=1,\dots,T
,

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})=T\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(t)}}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}\right)\right].

(262)

Similar to the bandit setting, a nice property for an online algorithm is to achieve a sublinear regret
R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})=o(T)
so that it eventually converges to an optimal policy
\pi^{*}
.
43
43

43

See Sec.
4.2
for a detailed discussion of the online learning task and properties of cumulative regret.

There exists an experimental design of randomized trials for optimizing policies over a finite sequence of actions
\bm{X}
in an unknown environment, called sequential multiple assignment randomized trials (for short, SMART
(
131
)
). It is an explore-then-commit algorithm. More specifically, fix a total number of trials
N\in\mathbb{N}
. For the first
t\leq N
episodes, the SMART algorithm explores by sampling values
X^{(t)}_{i}
of every action
X_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,H
, from its domain
\mathscr{D}(X_{i})
uniformly at random. For episodes
t>N
, the algorithm commits to a policy
\pi^{(t)}
maximizing the empirical reward estimates
\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
computed from experimental data
\mathcal{D}_{\text{exp}}^{(N)}
collected during exploration. Details of the algorithm is summarized in Alg.
2
.

When the total number of trials
N
is sufficiently large, SMART is able to recover the effects of candidate policies and find an optimal policy from the experimental data
(
127
;
130
)
. However, as previously discussed in Sec.
4.2
, explore-then-commit algorithms suffer from a linear regret during the exploration phase (
t\leq N
). Determining the optimal trial number
N
is theoretically challenging, requiring prior parametric knowledge about the underlying environment and the total episodes of interactions
T
.

We will next describe a novel online learning algorithm for optimizing a policy space over a sequence of actions
\bm{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{H}\}
. It is able to achieve a sublinear regret
R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})=o(T)
without parametric knowledge of the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and total episodes
T
. Our discussion begins with the decision with some necessary notations and technical tools. Recall that for every
i=1,\dots,H
,
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{i}\}
is a sequence of actions up to stage
i
and
\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}=\{\bm{S}_{1},\dots,\bm{S}_{i}\}
is the sequence of corresponding states. For any policy
\pi\in\Pi
, using basic probabilistic operations and the Bayes’ rule, the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
could be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]}_{\text{reward}}\prod_{i=0}^{H-1}\underbrace{P_{\pi}\left(s_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)}_{\text{transition probabilities}}\underbrace{\pi_{i+1}\left(x_{i+1}\mid\bm{s}_{i+1}\right)}_{\text{policy}}

(263)

The above equation follows that in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
, values of every action
X_{i}
are determined by the function
\pi_{i}
. Among the quantities above, probabilities of policy
\pi
are known. It is sufficient to estimate transition distributions
P_{\pi}\left(s_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
and the conditional reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
.

The NUC condition holds in the post-interventional system
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
(Lem.
48
). Therefore, for every
i=0,\dots,H-1
, conditioning on past states
\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
d-separates all backdoor paths between actions
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i}
and another variables in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
. Applying Rule 2 of do-calculus (Thm.
67
),

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left(s_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)=P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(s_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right),

(264)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]

(265)

In words, the transition distribution
P_{\pi}\left(s_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
and conditional reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
remain invariant across policies
\pi\in\Pi
. Therefore, they could be consistently estimated by pooling interventional data collected by different candidate policies in
\Pi
.

Throughout this section, we will consistently assume that the domains of the states
\bm{S}
and actions
\bm{X}
are finite; the reward function
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})
is bounded in a real interval
[0,1]
. Fix a finite sequence
\pi^{(1)},\dots,\pi^{(t)}\in\Pi
. Given finite samples
\left\{\bm{V}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{V}^{(t)}\right\}
drawn from distributions
P_{\pi^{(1)}}\left(\bm{V}\right)\dots,P_{\pi^{(t)}}\left(\bm{V}\right)
respectively, empirical mean estimates for the transition distribution
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1}}\left(\bm{S}_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i-1}\right)
,
i=1,\dots,H-1
, and the conditional reward
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[Y\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
are defined as:

\displaystyle\forall i=1,\dots,H-1,\;\;

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{s}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{t}\mathds{1}\left\{\bar{\bm{X}}^{(j)}_{i}=\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{S}}^{(j)}_{i+1}=\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}\right\}}{N_{t}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})},

(266)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{t}\mathds{1}\left\{\bar{\bm{X}}^{(j)}_{H}=\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{S}}^{(j)}_{H}=\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right\}\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}^{(j)}\right)}{N_{t}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})},

(267)

Among quantities in the above equations, for every
i=1,\dots,H
,
N_{t}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})
is the event count for every state-action pair
(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})\in\mathscr{D}(\bar{\bm{X}}_{i}\cup\bar{\bm{S}}_{i})
defined as

\displaystyle\forall i=1,\dots,H,\;\;N_{t}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})=\max\left\{1,\sum_{j=1}^{t}\mathds{1}\left\{\bar{\bm{X}}^{(j)}_{i}=\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{S}}^{(j)}_{i}=\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right\}\right\}.

(268)

Alg.
6
shows details of
UCB
algorithm capable of optimizing an unknown CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
. It works in phases of model construction, optimistic planning, and policy execution. In Step 2,
UCB
constructs a set
\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
of plausible SCMs from interventional data
\left\{\bm{V}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{V}^{(t-1)}\right\}
. For every SCM
\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
, its transition distributions
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{S}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}\right)
,
i=1,\dots,H-1
, and the conditional reward
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
are contained in convex intervals centering around their corresponding empirical estimates computed from interventional data collected prior to episode
t
.
The error probability
\delta
is set as a decreasing function of the episode number
t
so that
\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
contains the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
with high probability as the algorithm continues and more interventional data are collected. It then computes in Step 3 an optimal policy
\pi^{(t)}
of the most optimistic SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
that induces the maximal expected reward. We will discuss the details of the model construction and the optimistic planning later. Finally, policy
\pi^{(t)}
is executed throughout episode
t
and new samples
\bm{V}^{(t)}\sim P_{\pi^{(t)}}\left(\bm{V}\right)
are collected (Step 4).

Algorithm 6

Upper Confidence Bound (
UCB
) for CDMs

1:

a policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
, a reward function
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto[0,1]

2:

for all
episodes
t=1,2,\dots

do

3:

Construct a set
\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
of all candidate SCMs
\mathcal{M}
, with error probability
\delta=t^{-4}
, that are compatible with the interventional data
\left\{\bm{V}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{V}^{(t-1)}\right\}
collected up to episode
t
.

4:

Find the optimal policy
\pi^{(t)}
of an optimistic SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(t)}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{(t)}\right]=\max_{\pi,\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\Big[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}\Big]\;\;\text{s.t.}\;\;\pi\in\Pi,\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}_{t-1}\left(\delta\right).

(269)

5:

Perform
\text{do}\left(\pi^{(t)}\right)
for episode
t
and receive observations
\bm{V}^{(t)}
.

6:

end

for

Model Construction

Let
\left\{Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(n)}\right\}
be i.i.d. samples drawn from a discrete distribution
P(Y)
. Let
\hat{P}(y)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\mathds{1}\left\{Y^{(i)}=y\right\}
be empirical estimates of probabilities
P(y)
. Generally, the
L_{1}
-deviation of the true distribution and the empirical distribution is bounded according to
(
203
)

\displaystyle P\left(\left\lVert\hat{P}\left(\cdot\right)-P\left(\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{1}>\sqrt{\frac{2\left|\mathscr{D}(Y)\right|\log\left(2/\delta\right)}{n}}\right)\leq\delta

(270)

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
\mathbb{M}_{t}(\delta)
be the set of all SCMs
\mathcal{M}
with endogenous variables
\bm{V}
, and with its transition distribution
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{S}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
close to the empirical distribution
\hat{P}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{S}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
,
i=0,\dots,H-1
, and the reward
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
close to the empirical reward
\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall i=0\dots,H-1,\;\;

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\cdot|\bar{\bm{s}}_{i};\mathcal{M})-\hat{P}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\cdot|\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})\right\rVert_{1}\leq\epsilon_{i}(\delta),

(271)

and

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})|\bar{\bm{s}}_{H};\mathcal{M}]-\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})|\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}]\right|\leq\epsilon_{H}(\delta).

(272)

where the confidence width
\epsilon_{i}(\delta)
is a function given by,

\displaystyle\forall i=0,\dots,H-1,\;\;

\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}(\delta)=\sqrt{\frac{2\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{S}_{i+1})\right|\log(4H\left|\mathscr{D}(\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}\cup\bar{\bm{X}}_{i})\right|/\delta)}{N_{t}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})}}

(273)

and

\displaystyle\epsilon_{H}(\delta)=\sqrt{\frac{\log(8H\left|\mathscr{D}(\bar{\bm{S}}_{H}\cup\bar{\bm{X}}_{H})\right|/\delta)}{2N_{t}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})}}

(274)

Applying a union bound over the concentration inequalities in Eq.
270
and Hoeffding’s inequality in Eq.
181
, we obtain the following error probability:

\displaystyle P\left(\mathcal{M}^{*}\not\in\mathbb{M}_{t}(\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{H-1}\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}}P\left(\left\lVert P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\cdot|\bar{\bm{s}}_{i};\mathcal{M}^{*})-\hat{P}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\cdot|\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})\right\rVert_{1}>\epsilon_{i}(\delta)\right)

(275)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}}P\left(\left|\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})|\bar{\bm{s}}_{H};\mathcal{M}]-\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})|\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}]\right|>\epsilon_{H}(\delta)\right)

(276)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{H-1}\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}}\frac{\delta}{2H\left|\mathscr{D}(\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}\cup\bar{\bm{X}}_{i})\right|}+\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}}\frac{\delta}{2H\left|\mathscr{D}(\bar{\bm{S}}_{H}\cup\bar{\bm{X}}_{H})\right|}

(277)

\displaystyle<\delta

(278)

That is, the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is contained in SCM family
\mathbb{M}_{t}(\delta)
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Optimistic Planning

Step 7 of
UCB
tries to find an optimal policy
\pi^{(t)}
for an optimistic SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(t)}
. For a fixed SCM
\mathcal{M}
, standard planning algorithms
(
20
;
85
)
are applicable to allow one to find an optimal policy in space
\Pi
that maximizes the expected reward. However, the optimization problem of Eq.
269
also requires the learner to find an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(t)}
that defines the maximal optimal reward among all plausible SCMs in family
\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
.

Generally, we can formulate this problem as a polynomial program as follows. The decomposition in Eq.
263
, together the invariances in Eqs.
264
and
265
, allows one to write the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]\prod_{i=0}^{H-1}P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(s_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)\pi_{i+1}\left(x_{i+1}\mid\bm{s}_{i+1}\right)

(279)

where every decision rule
\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid\bm{S}_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,H
, is a proper conditional distribution mapping from the domains of states
\bm{S}_{i}
to action
X_{i}
. Probabilities of transition distributions
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}_{i}}\right)
for
i=0,\dots,H-1
are contained in the convex set defined in Eq.
271
; values of the conditional reward
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
are contained in the convex polytope
\bm{\mathcal{R}}
defined in Eq.
272
.

Solving for a policy
\pi^{(t)}
and an optimistic SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(t)}
in Eq.
269
is equivalent to solving a polynomial program with objective function
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
defined in Eq.
279
with transitional probabilities
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}_{i}}\right)\in\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
,
i=0,\dots,H-1
, and reward mean
E_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{K}}[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})|\bar{\bm{s}}_{K}]\in\bm{\mathcal{R}}
; and probabilistic constraints
\sum_{x_{i}}\pi_{i}(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i})=1
and
\pi_{i}(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i})\geq 0
, for every
i=1,\dots,H
. There exists an efficient dynamic programming procedure to solve this polynomial program when the policy space
\Pi
satisfies the
perfect recall
condition
(
84
, Def. 23.5)
. In words, this conditions states that
\bm{S}_{i}\cup\{X_{i}\}\subseteq\bm{S}_{j}
whenever
i<j
, which means that the agent does not forget the previous decision or information it once had.
44
44

44

In many real-world healthcare applications where the decision horizon
H
is low, the perfect recall assumption is quite natural and automatically satisfied.
On the other hand, in some practical settings where the horizon
H
is high or even infinite, the policy space
\Pi
satisfying the perfect recall is high-dimensional. Planning in
\Pi
is computationally challenging even when parameters of the underlying SCM are fully known
(
140
)
.

An optimistic policy
\pi^{(t)}
is obtainable by solving following extended Bellman equations, for
\forall i=1,\dots,H
,

\displaystyle Q^{*}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})=\max_{x_{i}}\left\{\max_{P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\cdot\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)\in\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{i}}\left\{\sum_{s_{i+1}}Q^{*}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(s_{i+1}|\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})\right\}\right\},

and

\displaystyle Q^{*}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1})=\max_{x_{H}}\max_{\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]\in\bm{\mathcal{R}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right],

(280)

The inner maximum in the above equation is a linear program (LP) over the convex polytope
\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{k}
(or
\bm{\mathcal{R}}
), which is solvable using by an iterative algorithm introduced by
(
173
)
. For grounding purposes, we provide the complete algorithm in Appendix.
D
.

Theorem 76
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a CDM where
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto[0,1]
. For any
\epsilon\geq 0
, the regret of
UCB
in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
after
T>1
episodes is bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{o}}:\Delta_{\pi}>\epsilon}\frac{17^{2}H^{2}\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|\log(T)}{\Delta_{\pi}}+\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{o}}:\Delta_{\pi}\leq\epsilon}\Delta_{\pi}T+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(281)

where
\Pi^{\mathrm{o}}=\{\pi\in\Pi:\pi\text{ is deterministic}\}
is the set of all deterministic policies in the policy space
\Pi
; and
\Delta_{\pi}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[Y;\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y;\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]
is the gap from the optimal reward for any policy
\pi\in\Pi
. Moreover, fix
\epsilon=0
. The regret of
UCB
could be further written as

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{o}}:\Delta_{\pi}>0}\frac{17^{2}H^{2}\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|\log(T)}{\Delta_{\pi}}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(282)

\blacksquare

Thm.
76
implies that Alg.
6
is able to achieve a sublinear regret
\mathcal{O}\left(H^{2}\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|\log(T)/\Delta\right)
where
H
is the total number of actions (i.e., the decision horizon),
\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|
is the cardinality of the state-action domain;
T
is the total episodes of online interventions; and
\Delta
is the gap in the expected reward between the second-best deterministic policy
\pi
and the optimal policy
\pi
evaluated in the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. This means that
UCB
is able to converge and eventually obtain an optimal policy
\pi^{*}
as the total episodes of intervention
T
increases. Moreover, suppose
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is an MAB model with
K
candidate arms, i.e.,
H=1
and
\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|=K
. The regret bound of Eq.
282
is equal to
\mathcal{O}\left(K\log(T)/\Delta\right)
, which matches the analytical result in Thm.
52
.

(a)

\alpha_{1}=3,\alpha_{2}=-3

(b)

\alpha_{1}=-12,\alpha_{2}=-3

Figure 26
:
Simulation results comparing
UCB
learner optimizing a 2-stage DTR model and
RCT
determining values of actions
X_{1},X_{2}
uniformly at random.

Experiment 5
.

Fig.
26
shows the cumulative regret of
UCB
+
in the 2-stage DTR environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Example
21
with the coefficients
\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\right)
set to
(3,-3)
and
(-12,-3)
respectively.
It takes as input the causal bounds over
P_{X_{1}}\left(S_{2}\mid S_{1}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\left[Y\mid S_{1},S_{2}\right]
, computed from the observational distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y)
. As a baseline, we include a randomized controlled trials (
RCT
) deciding treatments
X_{1},X_{2}
uniformly at random, as introduced by
(
131
)
, which extended the one-shot, classical treatment by
(
50
)
.

One can see by inspection
UCB
is able to achieve a sublinear regret in both DTR models. Simulations corroborate the analytical results
UCB
is able to eventually converge to an optimal policy
\pi^{*}
as the total number of trials
T
increases. As expected, the randomized strategy
RCT
performs the worst among all strategies due to the linear regret during exploration.
\blacksquare

5.3
Learning from Observational Data

Despite its performance guarantee, the online learning algorithm introduced in Alg.
6
does not make use of any knowledge in the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
.
When the NUC condition (Def.
40
) holds in the underlying CDM
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
, the state and actions’ history
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
blocks all backdoor paths every action
X_{i}
to any other variable in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
.
One could thus estimate the transition distribution
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{S}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
,
i=0,\dots,H-1
and reward
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
using the corresponding conditional distribution
P\left(\bm{S}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
.
The validity of the estimation procedure follows from Rule 2 of do-calculus (Def.
67
).
These estimations could then be directly transferred to “warm-start”
UCB
algorithm.
However, issues of non-identifiability could arise in general settings where the NUC does not hold and no additional causal assumptions are provided.
45
45

45

The non-identifiability of transition distributions
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{S}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
,
i=2,\dots,H-1
and reward
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
has been acknowledged in
(
105
;
38
)
.

Given such challenges, we then consider the
partial identification
of the transition distributions and the expected reward from the observational distribution. Our first result bounds interventional transition probabilities
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{s}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)
from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
.

Theorem 77
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a CDM where the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
. For every
i=1,\dots,H-1
,
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{s}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)\in\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})\right]
where

\displaystyle l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})=\frac{P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})}{\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})},

\displaystyle r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})=\frac{\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})}{\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})}.

(283)

and
\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})
is a function of the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
defined as:

\displaystyle\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})=\begin{cases}P(\bm{s}_{1})&\mbox{if $i=0$}\\
P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})-P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})+\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})&\mbox{if $i=1,\dots,H-1$}\end{cases}

(284)

\blacksquare

The upper bound in Eq.
283
could be written as:

\displaystyle{\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})\over\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})}

\displaystyle={P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})-P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})+\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})\over\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})}

(285)

\displaystyle=1-{P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})-P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})\over\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1})}

(286)

Considering the denominator, note that the gap
P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})-P(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{x}}_{i})>0
whenever observational probabilities
P(\bm{s},\bm{x})>0
are positive for all realizations of the state-action pair. This means that the causal bounds in Thm.
77
are generally informative, i.e., strictly contained in the real interval
[0,1]
.
The bounds following Thm.
77
can be seen as a generalization of the natural ones given in Thm.
73
to the sequential settings with multiple actions
H>1
. The following example illustrates this connection.

Example 78
.

Consider the
2
-stage DTR model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Example
21
. We will bound the interventional distribution
P_{x_{1}}\left(S_{2}\mid s_{1}\right)
from the observational distribution
P(S_{1},X_{1},S_{2},X_{2},Y)
. Applying Thm.
77
, we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle P_{x_{1}}\left(s_{2}\mid s_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{P(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})}{\Gamma(s_{1})}

(287)

\displaystyle\geq P(s_{2},x_{1}\mid s_{1})

(288)

The last step follows from
\Gamma(s_{1})=P(s_{1})
. Similarly, function
\Gamma(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})
could be written as:

\displaystyle\Gamma(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})

\displaystyle=P(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})-P(s_{1},x_{1})+\Gamma(s_{1})

(289)

\displaystyle=P(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})-P(s_{1},x_{1})+P(s_{1})

(290)

Therefore, we could obtain the upper bound

\displaystyle P_{x_{1}}\left(s_{2}\mid s_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Gamma(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})}{\Gamma(s_{1})}

(291)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{P(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})-P(s_{1},x_{1})+P(s_{1})}{P(s_{1})}

(292)

\displaystyle\leq P(s_{2},x_{1}\mid s_{1})-P(x_{1}\mid s_{1})+1

(293)

The above bounds could be seen as an application of natural bounds (Thm.
73
) with action
X_{1}
and outcome
S_{2}
conditioning on the covariate
S_{1}
. We evaluate transition probabilities
P_{x_{1}}\left(s_{2}\mid s_{1}\right)
in the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, compute their corresponding causal bounds in Table
13(a)
.
\blacksquare

Similarly, one could bound conditional rewards
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
from the observational data.

Theorem 79
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a CDM where the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto[0,1]
.
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]\in\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\right]
where

\displaystyle l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})={E\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}\right]P\left(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}\right)\over\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1})}

\displaystyle r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})=1-{\left(1-E\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}\right]\right)P\left(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}\right)\over\Gamma(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1})}

(294)

Since the conditional reward
E\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}\right]\in[0,1]
, the bounds in Eq.
294
must be strictly contained in
[0,1]
whenever the observational probability
P\left(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}\right)
is positive, and are thus informative. The bounds developed so far are functions of the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
, which is identifiable by the sampling process, and so generally can be estimated consistently. We could estimate causal bounds in Thms.
77
and
79
by the corresponding sample mean estimates. Standard concentration inequalities are applicable to control the uncertainties due to finite samples.

.

\mathbb{E}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\left[Y\mid S_{1},S_{2}\right]

Table 13
:
Interventional distributions
P_{X_{1}}\left(S_{2}\mid S_{1}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\left[Y\mid S_{1},S_{2}\right]
and their causal bounds defined by a 2-stage DTR environment described in Example

.

Continue with the 2-stage DTR model in Example
78
. We also apply Thm.
79
to bound the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2}\right]
. Precisely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right]P\left(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right)}{\Gamma(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})}

(295)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right]P\left(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right)}{P(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})-P(s_{1},x_{1})+P(s_{1})}

(296)

The last step follows from the evaluation of
\Gamma(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})
in Eq.
290
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right]\right)P\left(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right)}{\Gamma(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})}

(297)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right]\right)P\left(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\right)}{P(s_{1},s_{2},x_{1})-P(s_{1},x_{1})+P(s_{1})}

(298)

We evaluate the conditional reward
\mathbb{E}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left[Y\mid s_{1},s_{2}\right]
directly in the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, compute their corresponding causal bounds derived above and provide them in Table
13(b)
.
\blacksquare

Algorithm 7

Upper Confidence Bound with Causal Bounds (
UCB
+
)

1:

a policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
, a reward function
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto[0,1]
, causal bounds
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})\right]
,
i=1,\dots,H-1
, and
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\right]
for all state-action pairs
(\bm{x},\bm{s})\in\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})
.

2:

Let
\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}
be the set of all SCMs
\mathcal{M}
with endogenous variables
\bm{V}
, and with the transition distribution
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{S}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i};\mathcal{M}\right)
compatible with bounds
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})\right]
,
i=0,\dots,H-1
, and rewards
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[Y\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H};\mathcal{M}\right]
compatible with
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\right]
, that is,

\displaystyle\forall i=0\dots,H-1,\;\;

\displaystyle l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})\leq P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{s}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i};\mathcal{M}\right)\leq r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}),

(299)

and

\displaystyle l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{n}}\left[Y\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{n};\mathcal{M}\right]\leq r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}).

(300)

3:

for all
episodes
t=1,2,\dots

do

4:

Construct a set of plausible SCMs
\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)
with
\delta=t^{-4}
following Steps 2-4 of Alg.
6
.

5:

Find the optimal policy
\pi^{(t)}
of an optimistic SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)\cap\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(t)}}\left[Y;\mathcal{M}^{(t)}\right]=\max_{\pi,\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\Big[Y;\mathcal{M}\Big]\;\;\text{s.t.}\;\;\pi\in\Pi,\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}_{t-1}(\delta)\cap\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}.

(301)

6:

Perform
\text{do}\left(\pi^{(t)}\right)
for episode
t
and receive observations
\bm{V}^{(t)}
.

7:

end

for

We are ready to introduce a generalized
UCB
+
algorithm utilizing causal bounds in the sequential decision-making setting. Alg.
7
summarizes the details of its implementation. It takes as input arguments a policy space
\Pi
, and bounds over transition probabilities
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})\right]
,
i=1,\dots,H-1
, and rewards
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\right]
computed from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
, following the derivation in Thms.
77
and
79
. More specifically, in Step 1,
UCB
+
constructs a family
\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}
of plausible SCMs with transition distributions
P_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}\left(\bm{s}_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right)\in\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})\right]
and with rewards
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}\left[Y\mid\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]\in\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\right]
compatible with the provided causal bounds. Since bounds in Thms.
77
and
79
are sound and the observational data are often abundant, this ensures that the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}\in\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}
with high probability. For every episode
t
, it computes the optimal policy
\pi^{(t)}
of an optimistic SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(t)}
in set intersection
\mathbb{M}_{t}\cap\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}
(Step 4). The construction of the SCM family
\mathbb{M}_{t}
follows Steps 2-4 of
UCB
defined in Alg.
6
. Similar to the optimistic planning procedure described previously,
\pi^{(t)}
could be obtained by solving a polynomial program with the objective Eq.
263
, subject to interventional constraints of Eqs.
271
and
272
, and additional constraints of Eqs.
299
and
300
imposed by causal bounds.

The causal bounds in over transition distributions
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{i}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{i+1})\right]
,
i=1,\dots,H-1
, and rewards
\left[l_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}),r_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H}}(\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\right]
also permits a partial identification strategy to bound the expected rewards of candidate policies
\pi\in\Pi
. Formally, the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]\in[l_{\pi},r_{\pi}]
such that

\displaystyle l_{\pi}=\min_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}\right],

\displaystyle r_{\pi}=\max_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}\right].

(302)

where
\mathbb{M}_{\text{c}}
is the set of all SCMs compatible with constraints imposed by causal bounds. Interestingly, these bounds
[l_{\pi},r_{\pi}]
also characterize conditions under which the confounded observational data accelerate the performance of online learning algorithms.

Theorem 81
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a CDM where
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto[0,1]
. The regret of
UCB
+
in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
after
T>1
episodes is bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{o}}:\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta_{\pi}>0,\\
r_{\pi}\geq\mu^{*}\end{subarray}}{17^{2}H^{2}\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|\log(T)\over\Delta_{\pi}}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(303)

where
\mu^{*}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]
is the expected reward of an optimal policy
\pi^{*}\in\Pi
.
\blacksquare

Thm.
81
implies that
UCB
+
, utilizing the observational data, consistently dominates
UCB
(Alg.
6
) in terms of the performance. Broadly, it enjoys the same asymptotic regret bound as
UCB
, provided in Thm.
76
. When the causal bounds are informative, i.e., there exist some suboptimal policies
\pi
with the upper bound
r_{\pi}<\mu_{\pi^{*}}
smaller than the expected reward of the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
,
UCB
+
is able to outperform
UCB
that learns from scratch, without using any prior observations. For instance, consider a multi-armed bandit model with action
|\mathscr{D}(X)|=K
and states
\bm{S}=\emptyset
. The regret bound of
UCB
+
is
\mathcal{O}(K\log(T)/\Delta_{x})
where
\Delta_{x}
is the smallest gap among sub-optimal arms
x
with causal upper bound
r_{x}\geq\mu^{*}
. The improvement condition matches the analytical result in Thm.
75
, derived for the special case of MAB models.

Consider first when the decision horizon
H=1
and the input covariates

. In this case,

achieves a regret bound
\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathscr{D}(X)|\log(T)/\Delta\right)

x^{*}

x

\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathscr{D}(X)|\log(T)/\Delta_{*}\right)

the smallest performance gap between the optimal arm

x
with a causal bound

\Delta\leq\Delta_{*}
,

performs at least as well as
UCB
.

H\geq 2
and every actions
X_{i}

\bm{S}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,H
.
UCB
, described in Alg.
6
, is able to achieve a regret bound
\mathcal{O}\left(H^{2}\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|\log(T)/\Delta\right)
where
\Delta
is the smallest performance gap between the optimal policy
\pi^{*}
and a suboptimal deterministic policy
\pi
. Meanwhile,

+
(Alg.
7
) exploits the causal bounds and enjoys a regret bound
\mathcal{O}\left(H^{2}\left|\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})\right|\log(T)/\Delta_{*}\right)
where

is the smallest performance gap between the optimal policy
x^{*}
and a suboptimal deterministic policy
x
with a causal bound

. Again, since the performance gap
\Delta\leq\Delta_{*}
,
UCB
+
generally outperforms
UCB
in sequential settings.

After all, our analytical results reveal that causal bounds are robust to the confounding bias in the observational data, and could consistently improve the performance of online learners.

6
Mixed Policy Learning: Where to Intervene (CRL Task 2)

Agents are deployed in complex and uncertain environments, where they are exposed and need to process high volumes of information while being expected to operate efficiently, surgically, and safely.
This requires the agent to identify an optimal policy to bring about a desirable state of affairs.
A prevalent assumption in the literature, including the discussion in the previous sections, is that the action space is fixed.
For example, it could be defined over variables
\bm{X}
, where
|\bm{X}|=k
.
This implies that the agent will explore policies within the domain of
\bm{X}
, for example, encompassing
2^{k}
possible configurations in the binary case.

In this section, we will relax the assumption that the policy scope is fixed and explore more flexible action spaces, including situations where the agent is not required to perform interventions.
This is motivated by the observation that such a strategy, while viable in controlled, artificial environments (e.g., actions executed in a simulator or gaming scenarios), where interventions are harmless, becomes less ideal and sometimes infeasible in real-world settings due to their potentially harmful side effects.

Another complementary property, perhaps surprisingly, in non-Markovian causal systems is that controlling all intervenable variables, denoted as
do(\bm{X}\leftarrow\bm{x})
, does not necessarily lead to an optimal policy.
In particular, we will show that in certain settings, a partial intervention, where
do(\bm{X}^{\prime}\leftarrow\bm{x}^{\prime})
with
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subset\bm{X}
, can outperform the case where full control is exerted.
In such systems, a larger search would be required to examine all possible subsets of
\bm{X}
, including
3^{k}
possible configurations, which will need to be evaluated in a systematic manner.

In real-world causal systems, it’s interesting to recognize that full controllability is not always necessary.
For instance, natural mechanisms often govern the action variables
\bm{X}
in many scenarios, meaning that forcing the variable to take some value by external interventions might lead to undesirable effects.
Robots inevitably obey the laws of physics, such as inertia and gravity, which makes their joints move naturally when their gears are disengaged; similarly, physicians treat patients based on their experience while adhering to varying rules and regulations by location.
Therefore, in certain situations, it may be sufficient to intervene in only a subset of variables among
\bm{X}
, allowing the remaining variables to vary according to their natural dynamics, as determined by the underlying mechanism
f_{X}
.
Fig.
28
illustrates these dynamics, where the agent performs interventions over different sets of variables in each episode, and sometimes just acts naturally.

Figure 28
:
Temporal diagram showing the dynamics of a mixed policy learning while the agent interacts with the environment with different policy scopes at each episode.

Towards formalizing this setting, we put these observations together and define a
mixed policy space
, which is a collection of policy spaces in which each action and context are defined as subsets of intervenable variables
\bm{X}^{\star}
and context variables
\bm{S}^{\star}
, respectively. That is,

\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}=\{\{\langle X,\bm{S}_{X}\rangle\}_{X\in\bm{X}^{\prime}}:\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star},\bm{S}_{X}\subseteq\bm{S}^{\star}\}.

Every policy space in
\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
lies between two extremes — observational and experimental policies.

Definition 82

(Mixed Policy Space & Policy)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal diagram,
Y\in\bm{V}(\mathcal{G})
be a reward variable.

\bm{X}^{\star}\subseteq\bm{V}\setminus\{Y\}
be a set of intervenable variables,
and
\bm{S}^{\star}\subseteq\bm{V}\setminus\{Y\}
be a set of context variables.
A mixed policy space
\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
is the collection of policy spaces where each policy space
\Pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
is defined with actions
\bm{X}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
and contexts
\bm{S}\subseteq\bm{S}^{\star}
such that
\Pi=\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\}_{i\mid X_{i}\in\bm{X}}
,
\bm{S}=\bigcup_{i\mid X_{i}\in\bm{X}}\bm{S}_{i}
, and
\mathcal{G}_{\Pi}
is a DAG. Given a mixed policy space
\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
with respect to
\langle\mathcal{G},Y,\bm{X}^{\star},\bm{S}^{\star}\rangle
,
a
mixed policy

\pi\in\Pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
is a policy
\pi
following the policy space
\Pi
.
\blacksquare

For simplicity, we may use
\pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
, which is the shorthand notation for
\pi\in\Pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
.
The following task signature characterizes this learning setting involved in a mixed policy space:

\mathcal{T}_{\textsc{mix}}=\left\langle\mathcal{I}=\text{do},\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G},\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}=\left\{\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\right\rangle\mid\bm{S}_{i}\subseteq\bm{S}^{\star}\right\}_{X_{i}\in\bm{X}}\right\}_{\bm{X}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}},\mathcal{R}=\mathscr{D}(Y)\mapsto\mathbb{R}\right\rangle.

Note that the policy space is not fixed but an element of a mixed policy space. This means that the agent will search for a policy
\pi^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\pi^{*}=\argmax_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\bm{Y}\right)\;\bigg|\;\mathcal{G},\;\mathcal{D}_{\text{exp}}\sim P_{\bm{x}}\left(\bm{V}\right)\right],

(304)

where the distinct feature of the task is the mixed policy scope.

In Section
6.1
, we introduce the marginal case of mixed policy spaces, where no context variables are present. Even without considering contexts, the problem of deciding where the agent should intervene in the system is challenging and sets the ground for further explorations.
We then investigate in Section
6.2
mixed policy spaces with context variables, or where the agent should intervene (do) and look (see) to determine the optimal policy.

6.1
Mixed Policy with No Context

In this section, we investigate how an agent should behave to efficiently identify an optimal action given a mixed policy space and an underlying causal diagram.
For simplicity, we focus on MAB settings where each policy
\pi\in\Pi_{\text{mix}}
is an experimental policy
(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{|{\bm{X}}^{\prime}|})
over a subset of actions
{\bm{X}}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
with each decision rule
\pi(x)
involved in an empty context. Thus,
\pi(\bm{x}^{\prime})\in\Pi_{\text{mix}}
for
\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}^{\prime})
and
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
.
The following example illustrates the challenge of this task.

\bm{X}

\{\}

:

(a) True causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
for environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. (b) Hypothesized model in the agent’s mind, after intervention. (c) Structure of the action space. Example 83

(Where to intervene)
.

Consider a MAB environment described by an SCM

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{1},U_{2}\},\bm{V}=\{X_{1},X_{2},Y\},\mathscr{F},P(U_{1},U_{2})\rangle,

(305)

where the endogenous variables
\bm{V}
are all binary.
The causal mechanisms are the following:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}=\begin{cases}X_{1}&\leftarrow U_{1},\\
X_{2}&\leftarrow X_{1}\oplus U_{2},\\
Y&\leftarrow X_{2}\oplus U_{2},\end{cases}

(306)

and the exogenous distribution:

\displaystyle P(U_{1})=P(U_{2})=1/2.

(307)

The causal diagram associated with
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is shown in Fig.
29(a)
.

We now consider an agent deployed in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
with the goal of optimizing the outcome variable
Y
while being capable of intervening on (or controlling) variables
X_{1},X_{2}
. Specifically, the goal of the agent is to find

\displaystyle\pi^{*}=\argmax_{x_{1},x_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{1}\leftarrow x_{1},X_{2}\leftarrow x_{2}}\left[Y;\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]

(308)

The structure of the mixed policy space is shown in Fig.
29(c)
, which highlights the various policy scopes available.
In particular, this space represents the power set of action variables
\bm{X}^{\star}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
, which entails
2^{|\bm{X}^{\star}|}
possible intervention sets.
This setting may be translated to a traditional MAB instance such that each arm corresponds to intervening on a subset of
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
to a specific value, which results in 9 arms in this case.
46
46

46

\{\text{do}(\emptyset),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 0),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1),\text{do}(X_{2}\leftarrow 0),\text{do}(X_{2}\leftarrow 1),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 0),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1,X_{2}\leftarrow 0),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1,X_{2}\leftarrow 1)\}

,

47
47

47

The same observation applies for MDPs or any more complex model, as discussed later on in the section.

We start our analysis with an agent that is oblivious to the causal structure underlying the action space and adheres to a strict experimentalist approach.
In other words, this means that it will abstract away the causal diagram and perform interventions on one action variable
\bm{X}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
.
We call the agent following such a strategy ‘‘all-at-once,’’ since all variables are intervened together.

48
48

48

Formally, this can be thought of as a specific instance of a cluster causal diagram, an object that has been studied in the literature; for a more detailed discussion, refer to
(
4
)
.

Each intervention on
\text{do}(\bm{X})
corresponds to interventions on the lower-level variables
\{\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 0),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 0,X_{2}\leftarrow 1),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1,X_{2}\leftarrow 0),\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1,X_{2}\leftarrow 1)\}
.
Under an interventional regime, the causal structure compatible with this strategy is shown in Fig.
29(b)
, which is clearly different from the environment’s causal diagram.

Despite what is in the agent’s mind, or optimization function, it will still be evaluated by the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
The natural question that arises here is whether it is okay to be oblivious to the pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{M})
; would it be sufficient to perform more interventions to make up for the ignorance of the causal structure?
In other words, more samples from the
\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow x_{1},X_{2}\leftarrow x_{2})
distribution should be sufficient to eventually learn an optimal policy?

This agent is deployed in the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and after some interventions, it is able to learn a policy and obtain the following reward:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[x_{2}\oplus U_{X_{2},Y}]=0.5x_{2}+0.5(1-x_{2})=0.5.

(309)

This policy is, in fact, independent of the specific values of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, and
Y
reaches its highest value at most 0.5 of the time.
At this point, the expectation by some is that there is an issue of sample complexity, but not of asymptotic convergence. In other words, the agent can be oblivious to the causal structure,
\mathcal{G}
, compensating by accumulating more samples, but it would eventually be able to learn the optimal policy.

Now we examine an alternative policy within the mixed policy space in which the agent controls only one variable
X_{1}
.
The evaluation of such a policy goes as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{1}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(x_{1}\oplus U_{X_{2},Y})\oplus U_{X_{2},Y}]=x_{1}

(310)

This means that if the goal is to keep
Y
as high as possible, the agent should perform an intervention
\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1)
, which would imply that
Y=1
in each subsequent round.

The implication of such a result is that the strategy “all-at-once”, oblivious to the environment’s structure, will never converge, no matter how many interactions are allowed to the agent.
We compare empirically this with an alternative strategy called “brute-force”, which searches over the entire policy space, including all possible subsets of
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
.
The performance of both agents is shown in Figure
29(d)
, where the y-axis represents a cumulative regret.
In fact, the all-at-once agent does not converge while the brute-force approach is able to find the optimal policy, since
\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1)
is inside the mixed policy space.

The question here is whether we can do better by leveraging the underlying causal invariances of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, as represented in
\mathcal{G}
.
We answer this question by examining the expected rewards over the entire mixed policy space.
First, using Rule 3 of do-calculus, we note that
P(y\mid\text{do}(x_{1},x_{2}))=P(y\mid\text{do}(x_{2}))
, since
X_{1}
has no effect on
Y
under intervention on
X_{2}
.
That is, the corresponding expected rewards are equivalent,
\mu_{x_{1},x_{2}}=\mu_{x_{2}}
, for any
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
.
Hence,
\mu^{*}_{X_{1},X_{2}}=\mu^{*}_{X_{2}}
.
Since the all-at-once strategy can be discarded, we examine 5 arms based on 3 intervention sets as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(X_{1}\oplus U_{X_{2},Y})\oplus U_{X_{2},Y}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]=0.5

(311)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{1}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(x_{1}\oplus U_{X_{2},Y})\oplus U_{X_{2},Y}]=x_{1}

(312)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[x_{2}\oplus U_{X_{2},Y}]=0.5x_{2}+0.5(1-x_{2})=0.5.

(313)

Therefore, the optimal action is
\text{do}(X_{1}\leftarrow 1)
for the model with
\mu_{X_{1}\leftarrow 1}^{*}=1
. Again, intervening
\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
will incur regrets and cannot converge to the optimal solution.

To explain this further, we now investigate the relationships among interventional probabilities in the causal model in Fig.
29(a)
.
The observational probability
P(y)=P(y\mid\text{do}(\emptyset))
can be viewed as a convex combination of
\{P(y\mid\text{do}(x_{1}))\}_{x_{1}\in\mathscr{D}(X_{1})}
,

\displaystyle P(y)=\sum_{x_{1}}P(y\mid x_{1})P(x_{1})=\sum_{x_{1}}P_{x_{1}}\left(y\right)P(x_{1}).

(314)

That is,
\mu_{\emptyset}=\sum_{x_{1}}\mu_{x_{1}}P(x_{1})
. By replacing
\mu_{x_{1}}
to
\mu^{*}_{X_{1}}=\max_{x_{1}}\mu_{x_{1}}
, then,

\displaystyle\mu_{\emptyset}=\sum_{x_{1}}\mu_{x_{1}}P(x_{1})\leq\sum_{x_{1}}\mu^{*}_{X_{1}}P(x_{1})=\mu^{*}_{X_{1}}.

(315)

This equation holds for any model conforming to the causal diagram in Fig.
29(a)
, namely, it affects whether the agent should play some arms since playing non-optimal arms will incur regrets.
At this point, playing the arms over
X_{2}
is preferred to the arms over both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
(i.e.,
\text{do}(X_{1},X_{2})
), since it minimizes the number of arms that need to be played to find the optimal, and
\text{do}(X_{1})
is preferred to
\text{do}()
as
\mu_{\emptyset}
cannot be strictly better than the best achievable expected reward obtainable by intervening on
X_{1}
to
{x^{*}_{1}}
.

interventions
\text{do}(\emptyset)
\text{do}(x_{2})
\text{do}(x_{1})
\text{do}(x_{1},x_{2})
distributions
P(y,x_{1},x_{2})
P_{x_{2}}(y,x_{1})
P_{x_{1}}(y,x_{2})
P_{x_{1},x_{2}}(y)
expected rewards
\mu_{\emptyset}
\mu_{x_{2}}
\mu_{x_{1}}
\mu_{x_{1},x_{2}}
\sum_{x_{1}}\mu_{x_{1}}P(x_{1})
P(y,x_{2}\mid x_{1})
P_{x_{2}}(y)
P(x_{1})
P(x_{1})=P_{x_{2}}(x_{1})

Figure 30
:
Relationships among quantities such as probability distributions and expected rewards arising in the mixed policy relative to causal model in Fig.
29(a)
.

Regarding the superiority of intervening on a set of variables over other set of variables, a natural question is, then, whether the comparisons among
\mu_{x_{2}}
and
\mu_{x_{1}}
can be made as well, noting that
\mu^{*}_{X_{2}}<\mu^{*}_{X_{1}}
in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
In fact, we can show that the inequality
\mu^{*}_{X_{2}}>\mu^{*}_{X_{1}}
is also realizable.
To witness, consider an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
identical to the one defined previously (Eq.
306
) but for
Y
’s mechanism:

\displaystyle f_{Y}\leftarrow X_{2}+U_{X_{2},Y}.

(316)

Then, we can evaluate the expected rewards in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(X_{1}\oplus U_{X_{2},Y})+U_{X_{2},Y}]=1

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{1}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(x_{1}\oplus U_{X_{2},Y})+U_{X_{2},Y}]=0.5x_{1}+0.5(2-x_{1})=1

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{2}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[x_{2}+U_{X_{2},Y}]=x_{2}+0.5

Hence, the optimal action is
\text{do}(X_{2}\leftarrow 1)
with
\mu^{*}_{X_{2}\leftarrow 1}=1.5
.
This demonstrates the impossibility that arises in some cases of deciding a priori to prefer one interventional scope over the other solely based on the causal diagram, and this depends on the specific instantiation of the environment.
\blacksquare

Considering the example, we note that ignoring the underlying causal structure, and the interplay between action space and reward, may result in a suboptimal performance due to playing such regret-incurring arms.
If one is negligent to the influence of unobserved confounder and simply chooses to intervene on every variable,
\text{do}(x_{1},x_{2})
, or to intervene on the one closest to
Y
,
\text{do}(x_{2})
, it is possible that the agent will never converge to the optimal arm, e.g.,
\text{do}(x_{1}^{*})
.

Exploring this example, we have shown the existence of equivalence classes among actions with respect to their expected rewards.
Also, certain partial-orders emerge among subsets of action variables with respect to their optimal expected rewards.
Also, the expected reward of an action is related to other actions, e.g., an observational probability written with probabilities from
\text{do}(x_{1})
and
\text{do}(x_{2})
,
P(y)=\sum_{x_{1}}P_{x_{1}}(y)P(x_{1})=\sum_{x_{1}}P_{x_{1}}(y)P_{x_{2}}(x_{1})
.
Figure
30
illustrates relationships among different distributions and their rewards.
Four different interventions are shown with their distributions where some distributions can yield other distributions, e.g.,
P_{x_{1}}(y,x_{2})
from
P(y,x_{1},x_{2})
. Further, the expected reward for observation (mentioned above) can be represented as an expression made of probabilities from other arms.
We will later see that such a formula improves the performance of online learners. We now more formally investigate these phenomena as studied in
(
102
;
100
;
104
)
.

6.1.1
Structural Properties in Mixed policy Learning in a MAB setting

Here, we provide three structural properties emerging in a bandit setting with a mixed policy. These properties among different actions arise due to the shared causal mechanisms and can be understood through do-calculus and related machinery.

Property 1. Equivalence among actions

Do-calculus provides rules to examine equivalence relationships in the space of conditional interventional distributions. Hence, it naturally partitions the space into equivalence classes.
In particular, we focus on Rule 3, which ascertains a graphical condition such that a set of interventions does not have an effect on the outcome variable, i.e.,

P(y\mid\text{do}(\bm{x},\bm{z}),\bm{w})=P(y\mid\text{do}(\bm{x}),\bm{w})
. Since actions correspond to interventions (including the null intervention) and there is no contextual information, we consider examining
P(y\mid\text{do}(\bm{x},\bm{z}))=P(y\mid\text{do}(\bm{x}))
through
(Y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\bm{Z}\mid\bm{X})
in
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\bm{X}\cup\bm{Z}}}
, which implies that
\mu_{\bm{x},\bm{z}}=\mu_{\bm{x}}
.
If d-separation holds in the manipulated graph, this condition implies that it is sufficient to play only one action among actions in the equivalence class regarding finding an optimal arm efficiently. In an online learning setting where its objective is minimizing a cumulative regret, it is desired to play a smaller subset of arms given a set of arms as far as the subset contains the best arm. Against this background, we define a minimal intervention set.

Definition 84

(Minimal Intervention Set (MIS))
.

A subset of action variables
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}

is said to be a minimal intervention set relative to
\mathcal{G}
,
\bm{X}^{\star}
, and
Y
if
there is no proper subset
\bm{X}^{\prime\prime}\subset\bm{X}^{\prime}

such that
\mu_{\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}}=\mu_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}

for every SCM conforming to
\mathcal{G}
and
\bm{x}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\bm{X}^{\prime\prime})
consistent with
\bm{x}^{\prime}
.
\blacksquare

Whether a subset of action variables
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
is an MIS can be examined through a rather simple procedure involving in an ancestral relationship.

Proposition 85

(Minimality)
.

A set of variables
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
is a minimal
intervention set for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
Y
if and only if
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq an(Y)_{\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\bm{X}^{\prime}}}}
.
\blacksquare

This characterization demonstrates that one can consider only directed edges among variables, not the unobserved variables, in acquiring MISes. Intervening nothing or a single variable (an ancestor of
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
) constitutes MISes. Further, intervening on
\bm{W}=pa(\bm{Z})\setminus\bm{Z}
is also an MIS for any
\bm{Z}\subseteq an(Y)_{\mathcal{G}}
since each variable
W\in\bm{W}
has a directed path towards
Y
without passing through the rest of
\bm{W}
, i.e.,
\bm{W}\setminus\{W\}
.

Property 2. Partial-orders among minimal intervention sets

We now explore the partial-orders among the subsets of
\bm{X}^{\star}
within the MISes.
Given a causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
, it is possible that intervening on some variables is
always
as good as intervening on another set of variables. Formally, there can be two different
sets of variables
\bm{W},\bm{Z}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}

such that

\max_{\bm{w}\in\mathscr{D}(\bm{W})}\mu_{\bm{w}}\leq\max_{\bm{z}\in\mathscr{D}(\bm{Z})}\mu_{\bm{z}}

in every possible SCM conforming to
\mathcal{G}
. If that is the case, it would be unnecessary (and possibly harmful in terms of the sample efficiency) to play actions over
\mathscr{D}(\bm{W})
.
We define Possibly-Optimal MIS, which incorporates the partial-orderedness among MISes denoting the optimal value for
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
given an SCM by
\bm{x}^{\prime*}
.

Definition 86

(Possibly-Optimal Minimal Intervention Set (POMIS))
.

Given
\mathcal{G}
,
\bm{X}^{\star}
and
Y
, let
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
be a MIS. If there exists an SCM
conforming to
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mu_{\bm{x}^{\prime*}}>\forall_{\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{Z}{\setminus}\{\bm{X}^{\prime}\}}\mu_{\bm{z}^{*}}
,
where
\mathbb{Z}
is the set of MISes with respect to
\mathcal{G}
,
\bm{X}^{\star}
and
Y
,
then
\bm{X}^{\prime}
is a possibly-optimal minimal intervention
set with respect to
\mathcal{G}
,
\bm{X}^{\star}
and
Y
.
\blacksquare

To determine whether intervening on a subset of
\bm{X}^{\star}
is a POMIS or not, one may list all possible partial-orders among MISes in a brute-force manner, and select those that are not dominated by any other MISes. However it is unclear whether we can compare two arbitrary MISes under what conditions and, further, whether such conditions are complete. As a starting point, we provide a way to obtain a single partial-order among two MISes. Consider intervening on an MIS
\bm{W}
. By basic algebra, we can express the expected reward for
\text{do}(\bm{w})
for some
\bm{Z}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{w}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{z}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{w}}\left[Y\mid\bm{z}\right]P_{\bm{w}}\left(\bm{z}\right).

If it is possible to exchange the observation
\bm{z}
to an intervention
\bm{z}
using Rule 2 of do-calculus, then the expression becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{w}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{z}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{z},\bm{w}}\left[Y\right]P_{\bm{w}}\left(\bm{z}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\bm{z}}\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{z},\bm{w})^{*}}\left[Y\right]P_{\bm{w}}\left(\bm{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{z},\bm{w})^{*}}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{z}^{\prime},\bm{w}^{\prime})^{*}}\left[Y\right].

where
\bm{Z}^{\prime}\cup\bm{W}^{\prime}
is an MIS corresponding to the intervention set
\bm{Z}\cup\bm{W}
and
∗
over
(\bm{z},\bm{w})
indicates the values for
(\bm{z},\bm{w})
maximizing the expectation.

:

Illustrative examples demonstrating how partial-orders can be obtained from Figure

\text{do}(X_{1})
is obtained, (

to

\mu^{*}_{X_{2}}\leq\mu^{*}_{X_{3}}
. Light blue areas represent variables having a backdoor path to
Y
under the intervention.

To find such
\bm{Z}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
under the intervention on
\bm{W}
,
we examine Rule 2 of do-calculus:
\bm{Z}
must satisfy
(Y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\bm{Z}\mid\bm{W})
in
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\bm{W}}\underline{\bm{Z}}}
, which is equivalent to
(Y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\bm{Z})
in
(\mathcal{G}\setminus\bm{W})_{\underline{\bm{Z}}}
. If no backdoor path from
\bm{Z}
to
Y
exists in
\mathcal{G}\setminus\bm{W}
,
then
\mu^{*}_{\bm{W}}\leq\mu^{*}_{\bm{W},\bm{Z}}=\mu^{*}_{\bm{W}^{\prime},\bm{Z}^{\prime}}
. One may iteratively apply this idea to find a set which leads to a higher expected reward when intervened on until stuck at a graph where every non-intervened subset of
\bm{X}^{\star}
in the graph involves a backdoor path to
Y
.

A few examples are illustrated in Figure
31
. In the example with a causal diagram where none is intervened on (Figure
31(a)
), every
X_{i}
with
i>1
has a backdoor path through
Z
while
X_{4}
is also directly confounded with
Y
. The light blue area in Figure
31(b)
covers backdoor paths from a subset of
\bm{X}
to
Y
in
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{X_{1}}}
. Thus,
\mu_{\emptyset}\leq\mu_{x_{1}}
can be inferred. One may directly facilitate a graph obtained by projecting out variables neither
\bm{X}
nor
Y
, and still backdoor paths can be equally examined in the resulting graph (Figure
31(c)
). For intervening on
X_{2}
(Figure
31(d)
), both
X_{1}
and
X_{3}
have no backdoor path to
Y
(Figure
31(e)
). Considering the minimality, we derive
\mu^{*}_{X_{2}}\leq\mu^{*}_{X_{2},X_{3},X_{1}}\leq\mu^{*}_{X_{3}}
(Figure
31(f)
). We can avoid considering
X_{1}
in the beginning by excluding variables ineffective to
Y
under the intervention
\text{do}(x_{2})
.
We define a set of variables having backdoor paths to
Y
in a given graph:

Definition 87

(Minimal Unobserved-Confounders’ Territory)
.

Given a diagram
\mathcal{G}
and a node
Y
,
let
\mathcal{H}
be
\mathcal{G}[An(Y)_{\mathcal{G}}]
. A set of variables

\bm{T}\subseteq\bm{V}(\mathcal{H})
containing
Y
is
called a UC-territory on
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
Y
if
\bm{T}
is closed under descendants and c-component, that is,
De(\bm{T})_{\mathcal{H}}=\bm{T}
and there is no bidirected edge between
\bm{T}
and
\bm{V}\setminus\bm{T}
in
\mathcal{H}
. If there is no UC-territory
\bm{T}^{\prime}\subsetneq\bm{T}
, then
\bm{T}
is a minimal UC-territory.
\blacksquare

The subgraph induced by Minimal Unobserved-Confounders’ Territory (MUCT) represents how
Y
is determined through the variables ruled by unobserved confounders under the intervention outside the MUCT. MUCT is tightly related to Rule 2 of do-calculus, and the procedure iteratively extends variables that have a backdoor path to
Y
so that the rest of the variables can be exchangeable between condition and intervention.

This has a connection to the partial-orders we are seeking — this demonstrates that
\mu_{\emptyset}\leq\mu^{*}_{\bm{V}\setminus\bm{T}}=\mu_{pa(\bm{T})\setminus\bm{T}}
if
\bm{X}^{\star}
is defined as
\bm{V}\setminus\{Y\}
. We define
\operatorname{MUCT}(\mathcal{G},Y)
as the MUCT of
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
Y
and
\operatorname{IB}(\mathcal{G},Y)=pa(\bm{T})_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\bm{T}
as the Interventional Border (IB) of
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
Y
where
\bm{T}=\operatorname{MUCT}(\mathcal{G},Y)
.
For an arbitrary
\bm{X}^{\star}\subseteq\bm{V}\setminus\{Y\}
, one can obtain a MUCT and IB from, say
\mathcal{H}
, the latent projection of
\mathcal{G}
onto
\bm{X}\cup\{Y\}
. Then,
\mu^{*}_{\bm{W}}\leq\mu^{*}_{\operatorname{IB}({\mathcal{H}}_{\overline{\bm{W}}},Y)}
.
A more involved example is shown in Figure
32
where MUCT can be procedurally constructed by iteratively updating
\{Y\}
by including the variables connected with it via bidirected edges and descendants of them. For example, MUCT under
\text{do}(B)
in Figure
32(c)
can be obtained by first removing
A
in the graph and expanding
\{Y\}
with its confounded variables
C
, its descendants
E
, and, again, its confounded variable
F
to ultimately arrive at
\{Y,C,E,F\}
.

Figure 32
:
Obtaining MUCTs (variables in light blue areas) and IBs (variable in green) under intervention (variables in blue) with
\bm{X}^{\star}=\bm{V}\setminus\{Y\}
(or equivalently
\mathcal{G}
maybe viewed as the latent projection of an original graph by retaining only
\bm{X}^{\star}\cup\{Y\}
). Here,
\mu^{*}_{B}\leq\mu^{*}_{D}
and
\mu^{*}_{E}\leq\mu^{*}_{E,F}
.

Equipped with MUCT and IB, one can check whether there exists a better MIS than a given MIS with respect to their maximum achievable expected rewards. However, the current procedure does not tell us whether there are other MISes better than the one obtained by an interventional border. The following theorem asserts that the interventional border approach provides a way to establishing a complete collection of POMISes given
\mathcal{G}
,
\bm{X}^{\star}
, and
Y
:

Theorem 88
.

Given
\mathcal{G}
,
\bm{X}^{\star}
, and
Y
, let
\bm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{X}^{\star}
be an MIS and let
\mathcal{H}
be the latent projection of
\mathcal{G}
onto
\bm{X}\cup\{Y\}
. Then,
\bm{X}^{\prime}
is a POMIS if and only if
\operatorname{IB}(\mathcal{H}_{\overline{\bm{X}^{\prime}}},Y)=\bm{X}^{\prime}
.
\blacksquare

This result
(
100
;
102
)
can be best explained by that, under the distributions of the set of unobserved confounders in a MUCT, the mechanisms of the variables in the MUCT are orchestrated to yield the best result given a configuration (values set outside the MUCT). When any external force (interventions on any variables in the MUCT) is applied, the delicately orchestrated mechanism is disrupted and a subpar reward is obtained. In Figure
31
, arms intervening on
\{X_{1}\}
,
\{X_{3}\}
, and
\{X_{4}\}
are POMISes.

Property 3. Expressions among actions

The two aforementioned structural properties help bandit agents to focus only on a set of minimal arms that can possibly be optimal without examining any collected data. We now consider the third property, which connects the causal effect of playing an arm and the distributions from data acquired through playing other arms.

Consider the causal diagram in Figure
33
with
\bm{X}^{\star}=\{B,D,E\}
where POMISes are
\emptyset
,
\{B\}
,
\{D\}
,
\{E\}
,
\{B,D\}
, and
\{D,E\}
. Then, an online agent playing POMIS arms will obtain samples from
P(\bm{V})
,
P_{b}(\bm{V}\setminus\{B\})
,
\dotsc
, and
P_{d,e}(\bm{V}\setminus\{D,E\})
. In this example, one can rewrite the expected reward for, e.g.,
\text{do}(\emptyset)
according to c-factorization
(
186
)
, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c,d,e,y}yP_{d,e}(y,a,b)P_{a,c}(e)P_{c}(d)P_{b}(c)

(317)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c,d,e,y}yP_{d,e}(y\mid a,b)P_{d,e}(a,b)P_{a,c}(e)P_{c}(d)P_{b}(c)

(318)

Other arms’ expected rewards can be similarly factorized.
Here, each term can be replaced by other probabilities obtainable from different POMIS arms with the help of do-calculus (Table
15
), which is derived from subsequent applications of do-calculus.

\text{do}()

\text{do}(b)

\text{do}(d)

\text{do}(e)

\text{do}(b,d)

\text{do}(d,e)

P_{d,e}(y\mid a,b)

P(y\mid a,b,d,e)

P_{d}(y\mid a,b,e)

P_{e}(y\mid a,b,d)

P_{d,e}(a,b)

P(a,b)

P_{d}(a,b)

P_{e}(a,b)

P_{a,c}(e)

P(e\mid a,c)

P_{b}(e\mid a,c)

P_{d}(e\mid a,c)

P_{b,d}(e\mid a,c)

P_{c}(d)

P(d\mid c)

P_{b}(d\mid c)

P_{e}(d\mid c)

P_{b}(c)

P(c\mid b)

P_{d}(c\mid b)

P_{e}(c\mid b)

P_{b,d}(c)

P_{d,e}(c\mid b)

Table 15
:
For each term shown in Equation
318
(rows), its equal probability quantities that are obtainable from data sampled by playing POMIS arms (columns) are shown. Note, for example, that
P_{e}(d\mid c)=P_{c}(d)
implies that this holds true for every
e\in\mathscr{D}(E)
.

These equalities not only imply that a term can be replaced by another but also suggest that each quantity can be estimated from the combination of them.
For example, the term
P_{c}(d)
can be estimated by a weighted combination of
P_{c}(d),P(d\mid c),P_{e}(d\mid c),P_{b}(d\mid c)
as

\frac{N_{c}\hat{P}_{c}(d)+N_{\emptyset\mid c}\hat{P}(d\mid c)+\sum_{e}N_{e\mid c}\hat{P}_{e}(d\mid c)+\sum_{b}N_{b\mid c}\hat{P}_{b}(d\mid c)}{N_{c}+N_{\emptyset\mid c}+\sum_{e}N_{e\mid c}+\sum_{b}N_{b\mid c}},

where
N_{\bm{w}\mid\bm{z}}
is the number of samples with
\bm{Z}=\bm{z}
in
\text{do}(\bm{W}=\bm{w})
. This expression is nothing but estimating the probability of
D=d
based on a maximum likelihood principle by aggregating compatible data instances together. Plugging in such estimator for each term in the expression for
\hat{\mathbb{E}}[Y]
results in an estimator taking advantage of other arms’ data.

Imagine an online learning scenario in which
\mu_{\emptyset}=\mathbb{E}[Y]
is relatively smaller than the optimal arm. The agent in the scenario will likely play more on other arms with higher rewards. The agent would occasionally play less-played arms when the agent is not completely confident that such arms are not the best arm (e.g., UCB or Thompson sampling). With the expression provided and other arms’ data utilized, the agent can improve confidence on what
\mu_{\emptyset}
is by playing other arms only avoiding accumulating regrets.

, the posterior of expected reward for each POMIS arm is approximated by bootstrapping samples from multiple data sources and integrated into Thompson sampling.

illustrates the cumulative regrets of

agents based on different arm candidates in an environment whose causal diagram is depicted in Figure

(leftmost) and Table

. In this experiment,

and

are not intervenable, and the agent can intervene in the all possible combinations of
\{B,D,E\}
. An agent with brute-force strategy attempts to learn the expected reward for each combination while other agent with MIS or POMIS strategy respectively employs only intervening MISes or POMISes. POMIS+ID indicates an agent actively infers each arm’s expected reward from other arms as well if possible.
Simulation results illustrate clear gaps among the performances of different strategies. At the end (10,000th episodes), Brute-force, MIS, POMIS, and POMIS+ID respectively yields cumulative regret (mean and standard deviation) of

655.96\pm 69.68
,

467.19\pm 66.85
,

358.86\pm 63.11
, and

280.80\pm 60.83
.
These results demonstrate that the refinement of arms by considering causal structure improves the efficiency of agents in them interacting with the underlying domain.
\blacksquare

In this section, we presented that an agent should be aware that choosing variables to intervene is not a trivial problem that can be simply answered like, i.e., intervene variables as many as it can, but a sophisticated problem that can be addressed with the knowledge of causal structure. In the next section, we further consider the case where agents can make use of states/contexts in their decision.

6.2
Mixed Policy with Context

As seen in the previous section, a causal understanding of the underlying world enables us to recognize a wide range of policies across various policy spaces, allowing agents to choose their mode of interaction. This involves decisions about not only which variables to intervene in but also to observe as part of the context. In light of this, we explore the use of causal relationships in systematic decision-making over mixed policies with contexts involved.
To illustrate the concept of mixed policy with context more clearly, let’s consider an agent operating within an environment depicted as in Figure
35(a)
.

Example 89
.

In this graph, we have
intervenable variables
\bm{X}^{\star}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
and contexts
\bm{S}^{\star}=\{C,X_{1}\}
.
The primary objective of the agent is to maximize the reward, denoted as
\mu_{\pi}
, which is defined as the expected value of
Y
when following a mixed policy
\pi
chosen from a mixed policy space
\Pi_{\textsc{mix}}
.

.

Figure 35
:
(a) a causal diagram, (b) abstract representation of a contextual bandit policy, and (c,d,e) policy-induced graphs.

\pi
nodes are intervention indicators, which will be left implicit throughout the section.

In the realm of contextual bandit (CB) problems, the objective is to optimize a policy denoted as
\pi_{\textsc{cb}}
(as depicted in Figure
35(b)
). This policy can be viewed as a stochastic mapping from contexts to actions. Alternatively, it can be represented as a pair of decision rules:
\pi_{\textsc{cb}}
can be expressed as
(\pi(X_{1}\mid C),\pi(X_{2}\mid X_{1},C))
or more generally
(\pi(X_{1},X_{2}\mid C))
(illustrated in Figure
35(c)
).
Traditionally, this policy is optimized within a constrained space denoted as
\Pi_{\textsc{cb}}
, which consists of pairs,
\langle X_{1},\{C\}\rangle
and
\langle X_{2},\{X_{1},C\}\rangle
. However, an issue arises in that the optimal policy
\pi_{\textsc{cb}}^{*}
, determined as
\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{cb}}}\mu_{\pi}
, may not necessarily be the best possible, i.e.,
\mu^{*}_{\Pi_{\textsc{cb}}}\triangleq\mu_{\pi_{\textsc{cb}}^{*}}<\mu^{*}
.

Consider a scenario where all variables are binary and
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are unobserved confounders connected to
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, respectively. Think of these as fair coin flips. Additionally, there’s a noise
\epsilon
associated with
X_{1}
, which follows a distribution with
P(\epsilon=1)=0.2
.

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}=\begin{cases}X_{1}&\leftarrow U_{1}\oplus\epsilon,\\
C&\leftarrow U_{1},\\
X_{2}&\leftarrow U_{2}\oplus X_{1}\oplus C,\\
Y&\leftarrow(1-(X_{2}\oplus U_{2}))\vee C\end{cases}

(319)

Given that the chosen policy renders
X_{2}
independent of
U_{2}
and that the context
C
is also unrelated to
U_{2}
, it’s deduced that the optimal value for
\mu^{*}_{\Pi_{\textsc{cb}}}
equals 0.75.
In this setup, the most effective policy involves intervening solely on
X_{1}
while considering the context
C
. This policy ensures that, when
X_{1}
is set equal to
C
, the noise
\epsilon
affecting
X_{1}
is eliminated, resulting in
X_{2}
becoming equivalent to
U_{2}
. Consequently, the policy attains an optimal expected reward of 1.0 in this environment.
\blacksquare

\Pi_{\textsc{cb}}=

\{\langle X_{1},\{C\}\rangle,

\langle X_{2},\{C,X_{1}\}\rangle\}

\Pi_{e}=

\{\langle X_{2},\{C\}\rangle\}

\Pi_{d}=

\{\langle X_{1},\{C\}\rangle\}

\Pi_{a}=

\emptyset

\supset,=_{\mu}
\subset
\supset,\geq_{\mu}
