Evaluation of the counterfactual expected reward
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i_{x_{i}}}\mid S_{i},X_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}]
evaluated in the MDP environment of Example
3
.

Similarly, note that values of the reward signal
Y_{i}\leftarrow S_{i}\oplus X_{i}\oplus U_{i,1}\oplus U_{i,2}
. Given state
S_{i}=s_{i}
, intended action
X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
and realized action
X_{i}=x_{i}
, event
Y_{i}=y_{i}
implies the following

\displaystyle U_{i,2}

\displaystyle=y_{i}\oplus s_{i}\oplus x_{i}\oplus U_{i,1}

(381)

\displaystyle=y_{i}\oplus s_{i}\oplus x_{i}\oplus s_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}

(382)

\displaystyle=y_{i}\oplus x_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}

(383)

The second step follows from Eq.
375
. Evaluating the expected reward
Y_{i}
conditioning on the state
S_{i}
, intended action
X^{\prime}_{i}
and realized action
X_{i}
in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i},X_{i}=x_{i}\right]

(384)

\displaystyle=P\left(Y_{i}=1\oplus x_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}\mid S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i},X_{i}=x_{i}\right)

(385)

\displaystyle=P\left(U_{i,2}=1\oplus x_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}\right)

(386)

Detailed parametrization of
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},x_{i}\right]
are computed and provided in Table
20
.
\blacksquare

More importantly, it is possible to show that the above conditional distributions in submodel coincide with ETTs of action
X_{i}
on the reward signal
Y_{i}
and next state
S_{i+1}
and action
X_{i+1}
, provided with the current state
S_{i}
. Such ETTs remain invariant across every stage
i=1,\dots,H
,

Lemma 105
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP environment,
\pi(X_{i}\mid S_{i},X^{\prime}_{i})
be a counterfactual policy over actions
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{\pi}
be an induced counterfactual submodel of
\mathcal{M}
. Then, for every
i=1,\dots,H
, the transition distribution over
S_{i+1}
and the expected reward over
Y_{i}
conditioning on
S_{i},X^{\prime}_{i},X_{i}
in submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\pi}
is equal to

\displaystyle P\left(S_{i+1},X_{i+1}\mid s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},x_{i};\mathcal{M}_{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(S_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}},X_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}\mid s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i};\mathcal{M}\right)

(387)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},x_{i};\mathcal{M}_{\pi}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}\mid s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i};\mathcal{M}\right]

(388)

Moreover, the above quantities remain invariant across stage
i=1,\dots,H
, i.e.,

\displaystyle P\left(S_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}},X_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}\mid s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\cdots=P\left(S_{2_{X_{1}\leftarrow x_{1}}},X_{2_{X_{1}\leftarrow x_{1}}}\mid s_{1},X^{\prime}_{1}=x^{\prime}_{1}\right)

(389)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}\mid s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\cdots=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1_{X_{1}\leftarrow x_{1}}}\mid s_{1},X^{\prime}_{1}=x^{\prime}_{1}\right]

(390)

\blacksquare

Among the above equations, Eqs.
387
and
388
follow from the definition of counterfactual intervention. Eqs.
389
and
390
hold since structural functions
f_{X},f_{Y},f_{S}
and the exogenous distribution
P(U_{i})
remain invariant across all stages of interventions
i=1,\dots,H
.

Example 106
.

Consider the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Eq.
5
. Given values of state
S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
, one could infer values of the unobserved confounder
U_{i,1}
as

\displaystyle U_{i,1}=x^{\prime}_{i}\oplus s_{i}

(391)

Given current state and action
S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
, the counterfactual event
S_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}=s_{i+1}
implies

\displaystyle U_{i,3}

\displaystyle=s_{i+1}\oplus(S_{i}\vee X^{\prime}_{i})\oplus U_{i,1}

(392)

\displaystyle=s_{i+1}\oplus(s_{i}\vee x_{i})\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}\oplus s_{i}

(393)

The last step follows from Eq.
391
.
Evaluating the ETT of action
X_{i}
on the next state
S_{i+1}
and action
X_{i+1}
conditioning on the current state
S_{i}
in the underlying MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
gives

\displaystyle P\left(S_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}=s_{i+1},X_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}=x_{i+1}\mid S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}\right)

(394)

\displaystyle=P\left(U_{i,3}=s_{i+1}\oplus(s_{i}\vee x_{i})\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}\oplus s_{i},X_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}=x^{\prime}_{i+1}\mid S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}\right)

(395)

\displaystyle=P\left(U_{i,3}=s_{i+1}\oplus(s_{i}\vee x_{i})\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}\oplus s_{i},U_{i+1,1}=x^{\prime}_{i+1}\oplus s_{i+1}\right)

(396)

The above equation coincides with Eq.
380
.
This means that the counterfactual ETT distribution
P\left(S_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}},X_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}\mid s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}\right)
evaluated in the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is equal to the conditional transition distribution
P\left(S_{i+1},X^{\prime}_{i+1}\mid s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},x_{i}\right)
evaluated in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
.
Its detailed parametrizations are provided in Table
19
. Moreover, this counterfactual distribution remains the same for all decision horizon
i=1,2,\dots
since structural functions
\mathscr{F}
and exogenous distribution
P(U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3})
are invariant with regard to the horizon
i
.

Similarly, given state and action
S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}
, the potential outcome
Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}=y_{i}
implies

\displaystyle U_{i,2}

\displaystyle=y_{i}\oplus s_{i}\oplus x_{i}\oplus U_{i,1}

(397)

\displaystyle=y_{i}\oplus s_{i}\oplus x_{i}\oplus s_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}

(398)

\displaystyle=y_{i}\oplus x_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}

(399)

The second step follows from Eq.
391
. Evaluating the ETT of action
X_{i}
on the reward signal
Y_{i}
conditioning on the current state
S_{i}
in the underlying MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}\mid S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=P\left(Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}=1\oplus x_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}\mid S_{i}=s_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}\right)

(400)

\displaystyle=P\left(U_{i,2}=1\oplus x_{i}\oplus x^{\prime}_{i}\right)

(401)

The last step coincides with Eq.
386
. This means that the conditional
x
-specific causal effects
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}}}\mid s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}\right]
evaluated in the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
equates to the conditional expected reward
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mid s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},x_{i}]
evaluated in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
.
Its detailed parametrizations remain invariant for every decision horizon
i=1,2,\dots
, and are provided in Table
20
.
\blacksquare

Lem.
105
permits us to represent the distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{S},\bm{X}^{\prime},\bm{X},\bm{Y}\right)
induced by a counterfactual policy
\pi=\left(\pi_{1}(X_{1}\mid S_{1},X^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,\pi_{H}(X_{H}\mid S_{H},X^{\prime}_{H})\right)
using a standard MDP (Def.
33
)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}\right\rangle

(402)

Here,
\mathscr{D}(S)
and
\mathscr{D}(X)
are, respectively, the domain of state
S_{i}
and action
X_{i}
for every stage
i=1,\dots,H
. The transitional distribution
\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}}
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}
are conditional ETTs evaluated in the underlying MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
given by, for any
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(S)
and any
x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(X)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}}(s,x,x^{\prime},s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=P\left(S_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}}}=s^{\prime},X_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}}}=x^{\prime\prime}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right)

(403)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}(s,x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}}}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]

(404)

Let
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\in\mathbb{R}
be a reward function taking reward signal
\bm{Y}=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{H}\}
as input. Our goal is to obtain an optimal counterfactual policy
\pi^{*}\in\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
maximizing the expected reward over
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
evaluated in the underlying MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. The reduction to a standard MDP model
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}\right\rangle
allows us to solve for an optimal counterfactual policy
\pi^{*}
using standard dynamic programming algorithms
(
21
)
, provided that the detailed parametrization of the underlying MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is available.

To make this argument more precise, we will consider a discounted cumulative reward function
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{i-1}Y_{i}
over an infinite horizon
H=\infty
, where the discount rate
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
The classic result in planning literature
(
151
)
implies that it is sufficient to consider a class of stationary counterfactual policies
\pi=\left(\pi_{1}(X_{1}\mid S_{1},X_{1}),\pi_{2}(X_{2}\mid S_{2},X_{2}),\dots\right)
such that the decision rule
\pi_{i}
remains invariant across the decision horizon
i=1,2,\dots
, i.e.,
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=\dots
.

The state-action value function
Q_{\pi}:\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X)\times\mathscr{D}(X)\to\mathbb{R}
for a counterfactual policy
\pi
evaluated in the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is defined as the expected cumulative reward following policy
\pi
, given the starting state
s
, intended action
x
, and realized action
x^{\prime}
. Formally,

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}(s,x,x^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{j}Y_{i+j}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x,X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime};\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}\right]

(405)

By exploring the Markov property in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
(Eqs.
370
and
371
, the above Q-function could be further written as an augmented
Bellman Equation
using the intended action
X
as an additional side information:

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}(s,x,x^{\prime})

(406)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}+\gamma Y_{i+1}+\gamma^{2}Y_{i+2}+\cdots\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x,X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime};\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}\right]

(407)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}+\gamma Q_{\pi}(S_{i+1},X_{i+1},X^{\prime}_{i+1})\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x,X^{\prime}_{i}=x^{\prime};\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}\right]

(408)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}(s,x,x^{\prime})+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}}(s,x,x^{\prime},s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\sum_{x^{\prime\prime\prime}}\pi_{i+1}(x^{\prime\prime\prime}\mid s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})Q_{\pi}(s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime\prime})

(409)

The last step follows from the equality relationships in Lem.
105
. An optimal counterfactual policy
\pi^{*}
is obtainable by recursively computing the Q-function and optimizing the realized action
x^{\prime}
for every state
s
and intended action
x
. The following example demonstrates such a procedure.

Example 107
.

Q_{*}

Table 21
:
Optimal augmented Q-function
Q_{*}(s,x,x^{\prime})
evaluated in the MDP model of Example
3
.

Consider the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
defined in Eq.
5
. Note that an optimal counterfactual policy
\pi^{*}
is such that its induced value function
Q_{\pi^{*}}(s,x,x^{\prime})\geq Q_{\pi}(s,x,x^{\prime})
for all state
s
, intended action
x
, and realized action
x^{\prime}
. Optimizing Q-function leads to a counterfactual augmented (ctf-augmented)
Bellman optimality equation
using the intended action
X
as an additional context, i.e,

\displaystyle Q_{*}(s,x,x^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}(s,x,x^{\prime})+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}}(s,x,x^{\prime},s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\max_{x^{\prime\prime\prime}}Q_{*}(s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime\prime})

(410)

The optimal policy
\pi^{*}
is given by, for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
, for any state
s\in\mathscr{D}(S)
,

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x)=\argmax_{x^{\prime}}Q_{*}(s,x,x^{\prime})

(411)

We compute the optimal augmented Q-function evaluated in the MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
using the value iteration algorithm
(
177
)
. Detailed parametrizations are provided in Table
21
. The optimal policy
\pi^{*}=\left(\pi^{*}_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{i},X_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
is given by
\pi^{*}_{i}\triangleq X_{i}\leftarrow\neg X_{i}
, for every
i=1,2,\dots
.

After all, this means that, in this case, the agent should always go against its intended action.
Evaluating its expected return gives
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{i-1}Y_{i}\right]=9
, which outperforms the best possible experimental policy
\pi_{i}\triangleq X_{i}\leftarrow\neg S_{i}
. Derivations of the best experimental policy are provided in Example
27
.
\blacksquare

More generally, experimental policies in
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\{S_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
are contained in the counterfactual policy space
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\{S_{i},X_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
. This means that one could simulate the expected reward of any experimental policy
\pi^{\prime}=\left(\pi^{\prime}_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{H}
using a counterfactual policy
\pi=\left(\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid S_{i},X_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{H}
such that
\pi^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\prime}\mid s)=\pi_{i}(x^{\prime}\mid s,x)
for all
s,x,x^{\prime}
. In this case, intended actions
\bm{X}
do not affect values of reward signals
\bm{Y}
in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
induced by counterfactual intervention
\text{ctf}(\pi)
. Marginalizing intended actions
\bm{X}
reduces
\mathcal{M}_{\pi}
to the experimental submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi^{\prime}}
induced by intervention
\text{do}(\pi^{\prime})
. The performance of the counterfactual policy
\pi
and the experimental policy
\pi^{\prime}
thus coincides, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\prime}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
. This observation implies that an agent optimizing the environment following counterfactual interventions
\text{ctf}(\pi)
must perform at least as well as its counterpart following experimental intervention
\text{do}(\pi)
. Formally,

Theorem 108

(Counterfactual dominates Interventional policies)
.

For an MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, let
\mathcal{R}
be a reward function over reward signals
\bm{Y}=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{H}\}
. Let policy spaces
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\{S_{i},X_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
and
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}=\left\{\left\langle X,\{S_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
. Then,

\displaystyle\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]\geq\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]

(412)

\blacksquare

Thm.
108
implies that it is preferable to learn an optimal counterfactual policy in the MDP environment when determining values for a sequence of actions, which consistently dominates the best possible experimental policy that does not account for the agent’s intended actions
X_{i}
for every stage of decision
i=1,\dots,H
. On the other hand, when the NUC holds in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Def.
40
), conditioning on current state
S_{i}
and realized action
X_{i}
d-separates the intended action
X_{i}
from all the future rewards
Y_{i},Y_{i+1},\dots
and states
S_{i+1},S_{i+2},\dots
. We thus have the following, for any
x_{i},s_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}
,

\displaystyle P\left(S_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}_{i}}},X_{i+1_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}_{i}}}\mid S_{i}=s_{i},X_{i}=x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=P_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}_{i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid S_{i}=s_{i}\right)

(413)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}_{i}}}\mid s_{i},X_{i}=x_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}_{i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=s_{i}\right]

(414)

In words, the counterfactual transition probabilities
\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}}
and reward function
\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}
coincide with their experimental counterparts
\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}
. An agent can thus simulate the performance of an optimal counterfactual policy using an experimental one; observing the agent’s intended action provides no value of information to the learning task and could be ignored.

7.2
Counterfactual Randomization

So far, we have described effective planning algorithms to obtain an optimal counterfactual policies that account for the agent’s intended actions in canonical decision environments, such as MABs and MDPs.
However, how can an optimal counterfactual policy be learned by computing the counterfactual quantities entailed by the underlying, unknown environment?
To illustrate, consider the MAB environment as an example.
When the intended action (coming from
f_{x}
) and the executed action match (i.e.,
x^{\prime}=x
), the counterfactual quantity
\mathbb{E}[Y_{x}\mid x]
coincides with the observational reward
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x]
, following the composition axiom
(
146
, Ch. 7.3)
.
For the general case when intended and executed actions do not match (
x^{\prime}\neq x
), the
x
-specific effect
\mathbb{E}[Y_{x}\mid x^{\prime}]
is not computable from any combination of passive observations and controlled experiments, without the detailed parametrization or additional assumptions of the underlying causal model.

58
58

58

One exception is the binary case, as elaborated in
(
146
, Sec. 8.2)
.

In settings where the
x
-specific effect is identifiable, a more challenging question arises: How can an agent implement the counterfactual policy in the environment?
This question stems from the observation that agents may consider various alternatives during the deliberation process and change their opinion about the best course of action.
Consequently, only the final choice matters, actually representing the agent’s natural predilections.
To illustrate this concept, the diagram in Fig.
45
depicts an example of an agent’s deliberation process.
Initially, the agent intends to play
X^{\prime}=x_{1}
but reconsiders, thinking it might be sub-optimal, and decides to switch to
X^{\prime}=x_{2}
, where
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
.
As time passes, the agent may realize that
X^{\prime}=x_{t-1}
was not ideal and switch to an alternative,
X^{\prime}=x_{t}
.
Ultimately, the final decision defines the agent’s individuality, irrespective of the path taken to reach it.
59
59

59

Note that whenever the agent pursues an interventional (layer 2) strategy by leveraging Fisherian randomization, the entire deliberation process is bypassed. In this case, the randomization itself determines which action should be executed. This is illustrated in Fig.
45
.

This challenge calls for novel counterfactual machinery to allow for the counterfactual interaction following layer 3 as discussed in the previous section, in theory.
Here, we introduce a novel type of randomization for intention-specific groups, namely, interrupt any reasoning agent before they execute their choice, treat this choice as their intention, deliberate, and then act.
The
x
-specific effect will then be computed in an alternative fashion, based on the idea of intention-specific randomization. This section discusses the algorithmic implementation of this randomization.

f^{x}
U
f^{x}_{1}
x_{1}
f^{x}_{2}
x_{2}
\cdots
f^{x}_{t-1}
x_{t-1}
f^{x}_{t}
x_{t-1}
X’

Figure 45
:
Illustration of decision flow,
f_{X}
, where
U
is taken as input and the natural predilections
X^{\prime}
is returned as output. The process is refined through multiple stages.

Alg.
8
shows the detailed design of randomized controlled trials augmented with counterfactual interventions, which we name
Ctf-RCT
. More specifically, it takes as input the domain of action
X
and an integer
N
indicating the total number of trials.
For every episode
t
, the agent first perceives its intended action
X^{(t)}
, decided by the behavioral policy
f_{X}
.
During the exploration phase (i.e., episode
t\leq N
), the algorithm selects a realized action
X^{(t)}
from the action space
\mathcal{D}(X)
uniformly at random.
During the exploitation phase (episode
t>N
), it selects a realized action maximizing the empirical estimates of the
x
-specific effect provided with the intended action
X=X^{(t)}
, computed from samples collected from the first
N
episodes of interventions. Formally, the empirical estimates of the counterfactual quantity
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{X\leftarrow x}\mid X=x^{\prime}\right]
computed from samples up to episode
t
are defined as:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}\left[Y_{X\leftarrow x}\mid X=x^{\prime}\right]=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{t}Y^{(i)}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{\prime(i)}=x^{\prime},X^{(i)}=x\right\}}{N_{t}\left(x^{\prime},x\right)}

(415)

where
N_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{(i)}=x,X^{\prime(i)}=x\right\}
is the total number of occurrence of intercepting intended actions
X^{(i)}=x
and selecting realized actions
X^{\prime(i)}=x^{\prime}
. It follows that Eq.
415
is a consistent estimate of the
x
-specific effect of
X
on
Y
.
Finally,
Ctf-RCT
performs an intervention
\text{do}(X^{\prime(t)})
following the selected action throughout episode
t
and receives subsequent reward
Y^{(t)}
.

Algorithm 8

Counterfactual Randomized Controlled Trials (
Ctf-RCT
) in MAB

1:

the domain of action
\mathscr{D}(X)
, the total number of trials
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

2:

for
episodes
t=1,2,\dots

do

3:

Perceive an intended action
X^{(t)}
and store it.

4:

Choose a realize action
X^{{}^{\prime}(t)}
as follows.

\displaystyle X^{\prime(t)}=\begin{cases}\texttt{Unif}(\mathscr{D}(X))&\mbox{if }t\leq N\\
\argmax_{x}\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(N)}\left[Y_{X\leftarrow x}\mid X=X^{(t)}\right]&\mbox{if }t>N\end{cases}

(416)

5:

Perform
\text{do}(X^{\prime(t)})
for episode
t
and receive reward
Y^{(t)}
.

for

One could also apply the same principle of counterfactual interventions to adaptive online randomization algorithms such as
UCB
(Alg.
3
) to obtain an optimal counterfactual policy with sublinear regret. Precisely, by applying Hoeffiding’s inequalities for every intended action
X=x^{\prime}
, we define the upper confidence bound over ETT
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{X\leftarrow x}\mid X=x^{\prime}\right]
computed from samples collected up to episode
t
as follows, for every pair
x^{\prime},x\in\mathscr{D}(X)
,

\displaystyle\text{UCB}_{t}\left(x,x^{\prime},\delta\right)=\hat{\mathbb{E}}^{(t)}\left[Y_{X\leftarrow x^{\prime}}\mid X=x\right]+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{2N_{t}(x,x^{\prime})}},

(417)

where the error probability
\delta\in(0,1)
is an arbitrary real value. For every episode
t=1,2,\dots
, the algorithm incorporating counterfactual interventions first perceives and intercepts the agent’s intended action
X^{(t)}
. It then computes the confidence bounds
\text{UCB}_{t-1}\left(x,X^{(t)},\delta\right)
for every arm
x^{\prime}
from samples collected up to episode
t-1
, and picks a realized action
X^{(t)}
with the highest UCB estimates provided with the intended action
X^{\prime(t)}
. Finally, the algorithm performs an intervention
\text{do}(X^{(t)})
following the selected action throughout episode
t
and receives subsequent reward
Y^{(t)}
.

The detailed implementation of the counterfactual
UCB
algorithm, named
Ctf-UCB
, is summarized in Alg.
9
. For every episode
t=1,2,\dots
, the error probability
\delta
is set as a non-increasing function of the total occurrences
N_{t}\left(X^{(t)}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{t}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{(j)}=X^{(t)}\right\}
of the agent’s intended action perceived at episode
t
from past samples collected so far. In words, the algorithm uses a separate instance of
UCB
for every intended action
X=x
. Let
K=|\mathscr{D}(X)|
denote the total number of candidate arms. The cumulative regret of
Ctf-UCB
after
T
episodes of interventions is bounded by summing the regrets of each
UCB
instance for every intended action
X=x
. Specifically, summing regrets in Eq.
185
gives

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq\sum_{x}C\sqrt{K\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}\left\{X^{(t)}=x\right\}\log(T)},

(418)

where
C
is a universal constant. Since the square root is a concave function, applying Jensen’s inequality allows us to further bound the regret as follows.

Theorem 109

(Regrets of
Ctf-UCB
in MABs)
.

For an MAB
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,Y\rangle
, let
\Pi
be a counterfactual policy space
\left\{\left\langle X,\{X\}\right\rangle\right\}
,
Y
be the reward variable with support on
[0,1]
, and let the domain of action
X
be
\mathscr{D}(X)=\{1,\dots,K\}
. The regret of
Ctf-UCB
in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
after
T>1
episodes is bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq CK\sqrt{T\log(T)}

(419)

where
C
is a universal constant.
\blacksquare

Algorithm 9

Counterfactual Upper Confidence Bound (
Ctf-UCB
) in MAB

1:

the domain of action
\mathscr{D}(X)
.

2:

for
episodes
t=1,2,\dots

do

3:

Receive an intended action
X^{(t)}
.

4:

Choose an arm
X^{\prime(t)}=\argmax_{x}\text{UCB}_{t-1}\left(x,X^{(t)},\delta\right)
where
\delta=N_{t}\left(X^{(t)}\right)^{-4}
.

5:

Perform
\text{do}(X^{\prime(t)})
for episode
t
and receive reward
Y^{(t)}
.

6:

end

for

Thm.
109
implies that
Ctf-UCB
is able to eventually learn an optimal counterfactual policy
\pi^{*}(X\mid X^{\prime})
that consistently improves the agent’s intended action.
On the other hand, without perceiving the agent’s intended action, the standard
UCB
algorithm only performs atomic interventions
\text{do}(x)
.
Note that an optimal counterfactual policy consistently dominates the best possible interventional one (Thm.
100
).
One important observation is that the standard
UCB
generally experiences linear regret when compared with an optimal counterfactual agent.

Corollary 110
.

Let
\Pi
be an experimental policy space
\left\{\left\langle X,\emptyset\right\rangle\right\}
,
Y
be the reward variable with support on
[0,1]
, and let the domain of action
X
be
\mathscr{D}(X)=\{1,\dots,K\}
. There exists an MAB environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
such that for any algorithm (e.g.,
UCB
) optimizing over space
\Pi
after
T>1
episodes is lower bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\geq 0.5T

(420)

\blacksquare

In words, there is an MAB environment such that for any online algorithm employing Fisherian randomization, it must incur at least
0.5
regret on average per episode of interaction. It is thus unable to achieve an optimal counterfactual policy accounting for the agent’s intuition.
60
60

60

See Example
94
for details of the construction of this MAB environment.

The proposed augmentation procedure may appear to be an immediate extension of
UCB
in contextual bandits using the agent’s natural predilection as an extra context. However, the augmented
Ctf-UCB
differs from contextual
UCB
in the following.
61
61

61

This augmentation procedure is applicable to empower other bandit algorithms, including Thompson sampling
(
16
;
53
;
52
)
, with the capability of counterfactual randomization and obtain an optimal counterfactual policy.

1.

The agent’s intended action
X
is semantically different from a context
S
. The former is a variable only existing under the observational regime (see), as shown in Fig.
43(a)
. It is replaced by the agent’s realized action and does not appear under the interventional regime (do), as shown in Fig.
43(b)
. On the other hand, the context variable
S
is not affected by the agent’s interaction regime with the environment.

2.

The realization of using the agent’s intended action
X
as an additional context is a consequence of counterfactual intervention (ctf-do). On the other hand, online RL algorithms interacts with the environment by repeatedly performing randomized interventions (do), discarding the agent’s natural predilection.

(a)

(b)

Figure 46
:
Performance of standard
UCB
performing atomic interventions and the augmented
Ctf-UCB
using counterfactual interventions; x-axis represents the total episodes of interactions. The x-axis represents, respectively, the probability of picking an optimal action and the cumulative regret in (
46
) and (
46
); the y-axis represents the number of episodes in both (
46
) and (
46
).

Experiment 9
.

We evaluate the standard
UCB
algorithm that attempts to maximize rewards based on
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[Y\right]
, ignoring the agent’s intended arm choice, and
Ctf-UCB
described in Alg.
9
, which maximizes the rewards based on ETT
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{X\leftarrow x^{\prime}}\mid X=x\right]
via counterfactual interventions.
All reported simulations are partitioned into rounds of
T=1,000
trials averaged over
N=1,000
repetitions.

The Greedy Casino parameterization (specified in Table
16
) illustrates the scenario where each arm’s payout appears to be equivalent under the observational and experimental distributions alone. Only when we concert the two distributions and condition on a player’s predilection the optimal policy can be obtained.
Fig.
46
shows the cumulative regret and probability of selecting optimal arms for evaluated algorithms. Simulations support the efficacy of counterfactual interventions. Analyses revealed a significant difference in the regret experienced by
Ctf-UCB
compared to standard
UCB
, which, predictably, is not a competitor experiencing linear regret.
\blacksquare

So far we have introduced a novel type of interaction between the agent and the environment, i.e., the counterfactual randomization, in single-stage decision-making settings such as MABs. We showed online learning agents using counterfactual randomizations consistently outperform their experimental counterparts that do not actively consider the agent’s intended action. Novel adaptive counterfactual randomization procedures were proposed to optimize an unknown MAB environment.

7.2.1
Counterfactual Randomization for MDPs

In this section, we generalize the counterfactual randomization to the more generalized sequential decision-making setting, e.g., MDPs.
We will endow online algorithms in an unknown MDP with the ability of counterfactual reasoning so that they can learn optimal counterfactual policies, accounting for the agent’s intended action and natural predilections.

To make the argument more precise, we will focus on the episodic learning setting in an unknown MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
with a finite horizon
H
. The algorithm will interact with
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for repeated episodes
t=1,2,\dots,T
.
For every episode
t
, the algorithm picks a counterfactual policy
\pi^{(t)}=\left(\pi^{(t)}_{1}(X_{1}\mid S_{1},X^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,\pi^{(t)}_{H}(X_{H}\mid S_{H},X^{\prime}_{H})\right)
in the counterfactual space
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
, performs intervention
\text{ctf}\left(\pi^{(t)}\right)
, and receives subsequent reward signals
\bm{Y}^{(t)}=\left\{Y^{(t)}_{1},\dots,Y^{(t)}_{H}\right\}
. We are interested in maximizing the undiscounted cumulative reward
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{H}Y_{i}
.
62
62

62

If the decision horizon
H
is sufficiently large, the undiscounted cumulative reward provides an approximation to the discounted cumulative reward with an infinite horizon
(
81
)
.
For the convenience of the analysis, we will assume that parameters of the counterfactual reward function
\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}(s,x,x^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i_{X_{i}\leftarrow x^{\prime}}}\mid S_{i}=s,X_{i}=x\right]
are known. However, our analysis generalizes immediately to settings where the reward function is not accessible.

Algorithm 10

UCBVI
in MDP (
Ctf-UCBVI
)

a policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\{S_{i},X_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
, a reward function
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{H}Y_{i}
.

2:

Initialize data
\bm{H}^{(0)}=\emptyset
.

3:

for

4:

.

5:

for
step
i=1,\dots,H

do

S_{i}=S^{(t)}_{i}
.

7:

Intercept the agent’s intended action
X_{i}=X^{(t)}_{i}
.

8:

Pick a new action
X^{\prime(t)}_{i}=\argmax_{x}\hat{Q}^{(i)}_{t-1}\left(S^{(t)}_{i},X^{(t)}_{i},x\right)
.

9:

Perform
\text{do}(X_{t}\leftarrow X^{\prime(t)}_{i})
and receive reward
Y^{(t)}_{i}
.

Update data

.

11:

Algorithm 11

UCB-Q-values

1:

Data
\bm{H}^{(t)}=\left\{\bm{S}^{(i)},\bm{X}^{(i)},\bm{X}^{\prime(i)},\bm{Y}^{(i)}\right\}_{i=1}^{t}

2:

For all
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(S)
and all
x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(X)
, compute from data
\bm{H}^{(t)}

\displaystyle N_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{H}\mathds{1}\left\{S^{(k)}_{i}=s,X^{(k)}_{i}=x,X^{\prime(k)}_{i}=x^{\prime}\right\}

(421)

\displaystyle N^{\prime}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{H}Y^{(k)}_{i}\mathds{1}\left\{S^{(k)}_{i}=s,X^{(k)}_{i}=x,X^{\prime(k)}_{i}=x^{\prime}\right\}

(422)

\displaystyle N_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime},s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{H}\mathds{1}\left\{S^{(k)}_{i}=s,X^{(k)}_{i}=x,X^{\prime(k)}_{i}=x^{\prime},S^{(k)}_{i+1}=s,X^{\prime(k)}_{i+1}=x^{\prime\prime}\right\}

(423)

3:

Let
\mathcal{K}=\left\{(s,x,x^{\prime})\in\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X)\times\mathscr{D}(X)\mid N_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})>0\right\}
.

4:

For
(s,x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}
, compute estimates

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime},s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})=\frac{N_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime},s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})}{N_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})=\frac{N^{\prime}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})}{N_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})}

(424)

5:

Initialize
V^{(H+1)}_{t}(s,x)=0
,
Q^{(H)}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})=H
for all
(s,x,x^{\prime})\in\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X)\times\mathscr{D}(X)
.

6:

for

i=H,H-1,\dots,1

do

7:

For all
(s,x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}
, compute function
Q^{(h)}_{t}
as

\displaystyle\!\!Q^{(h)}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})=\min\left\{Q^{(h)}_{t-1}(s,x,x^{\prime}),H,\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})+\left(\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}V^{(h+1)}_{t}\right)(s,x,x^{\prime})+b^{(h)}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})\right\}

where the bonus function
b^{(h)}_{t}
is defined as

\displaystyle b^{(h)}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})=7H\sqrt{\ln\left(5|\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X)\times\mathscr{D}(X)|T/\delta\right)\over N_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})}

(425)

8:

Let
V^{(h)}_{t}(s,x)=\max_{x^{\prime}}Q^{(h)}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime})
.

9:

end

for

We will utilize
UCBVI

(
12
)
, an online reinforcement learning algorithm that can learn the best possible experimental policy
\pi\in\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}
in a finite-horizon MDP environment.
Alg.
10
shows an augmented procedure that incorporates counterfactual randomization which we call
Ctf-UCBVI
.
More specifically, for every episode
t
, it computes a policy
\pi^{(t)}
based on the data
\bm{H}^{(t-1)}
collected prior to episode
t
. At Step 3, it calls
UCB-Q-values
(Alg.
11
), which returns upper confidence bounds on the optimal Q-values
Q_{*}(s,x,x^{\prime})
. This is computed using an empirical Bellman operator with an additional confidence bonus, estimated based on Chernoff-Hoeffding’s concentration inequality. The empirical estimates of the counterfactual transition distributions
\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}}
and the conditional reward
\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}
are consistent following Lem.
105
. At Step 6 in
UCB-Q-values
, the linear operator
\left(\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}\cdot V^{(h+1)}_{t}\right)(s,x,x^{\prime})
is defined as,

\displaystyle\left(\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}\cdot V^{(h+1)}_{t}\right)(s,x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}}\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}(s,x,x^{\prime},s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})V^{(h+1)}_{t}(s^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})

(426)

At Steps 5 – 9,
Ctf-UCBVI
sequentially performs counterfactual intervention
ctf
on every action
X_{1},\dots,X_{H}
. For every decision horizon
i=1,\dots,H
, it observes the current state
S_{i}=S^{(t)}_{i}
, and intercepts the agent’s intended action
X^{\prime}_{i}=X^{(t)}_{i}
.
The algorithm then computes an alternative action
X^{(t)}_{i}
by maximizing the empirical Q-values
\hat{Q}_{t-1}
computed from data
\bm{H}^{(t)}
. Finally, it performs the selected action
\text{do}(X_{t}\leftarrow X^{\prime(t)}_{i})
and receives a subsequent reward
Y^{(t)}_{i}
.

Following the derivation in
(
12
)
, it is possible to show that
Ctf-UCBVI
, empowered with counterfactual randomization, is able to obtain an optimal counterfactual policy in
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
while achieving a sublinear regret. Formally,

Theorem 111

(Regrets of
Ctf-UCBVI
in MDPs)
.

For an MDP
\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\rangle
with horizon
H\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Pi
be a counterfactual policy space
\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\{S_{i},X_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{H}Y_{i}
be a cumulative reward function over bounded reward signals
Y_{i}\in[0,1]
. It holds the regret of
Ctf-UCBVI
in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
after
T>1
episodes is bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\leq CH^{3/2}\sqrt{|\mathscr{D}(S)\times\mathscr{D}(X)|T\log(T)}

(427)

where
C
is a universal constant;
\mathscr{D}(S)
and
\mathscr{D}(X)
are domains of every state
S_{i}
and action
X_{i}
,
i=1,2,\dots
, respectively.
\blacksquare

On the other hand, without considering the agent’s intended action, online algorithms performing randomized experiments may never be able to converge to an optimal counterfactual policy. The linear regret could occur when the intended action
X_{i}
reveals valuable information about the unobserved confounder
U_{i}
, as highlighted by the next proposition.

Corollary 112
.

Let
\Pi
be an experimental policy space
\left\{\left\langle X_{i},\{S_{i},X_{i}\}\right\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
,
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{H}Y_{i}
be a reward function over bounded reward signal
Y_{i}\in[0,1]
. There exists an MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
such that for any algorithm (e.g.,
UCBVI
) optimizing over space
\Pi
after
T>1
episodes is lower bounded by

\displaystyle R(T,\mathcal{M}^{*})\geq 0.08HT

(428)

\blacksquare

Similarly to the MAB setting, the above proposition implies that there is an MDP environment such that for any online algorithm following Fisherian randomization, it suffers at least a constant regret on average per every step of the interaction.
Therefore, these existing algorithms are generally incapable of obtaining an optimal counterfactual policy in MDPs while achieving a sublinear regret. Fortunately, one could augment these RL algorithms with counterfactual reasoning by replacing standard interventions with counterfactual interventions.
The proposed
Ctf-UCBVI
(Alg.
10
) demonstrates this augmentation procedure in the
UCBVI
algorithm. The following simulation demonstrates the performance of
Ctf-UCBVI
in a simple MDP environment.

(a)

(b)

Figure 47
:
Performance of standard
UCBVI
performing atomic interventions and the augmented
Ctf-UCBVI
using counterfactual interventions.

Experiment 10
.

We evaluate standard
UCBVI
that attempts to maximize rewards based on interventional transitional probabilities
\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}
and reward function
\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}
, while it ignores the agent’s intended actions.
We also evaluate the augmented
Ctf-UCBVI
algorithm described in Alg.
10
which attempts to maximize cumulative reward based on counterfactual transitional probabilities
\mathcal{T}_{\text{ctf}}
and reward function
\mathcal{R}_{\text{ctf}}
. It actively accounts for the agent’s intended actions by performing counterfactual interventions. All reported simulations are partitioned into rounds of
T=5000
trials averaged over
N=1000
repetitions.

The detailed parameterization of the MDP environment with unobserved confounders is provided in Eq.
5
where the decision horizon
H=10
. Fig.
47
shows the cumulative regret and probability of selecting optimal arms for evaluated algorithms. Simulation results support our proposed online RL algorithms using counterfactual interventions. Analyses revealed that standard
UCBVI
suffered from a linear regret. Meanwhile, the augmented
Ctf-UCBVI
achieved a sublinear regret, showing that it is able to obtain an optimal counterfactual policy actively accounting for the agent’s intended actions in the decision-making process.
\blacksquare

7.3
The Tradeoff between Autonomy and Optimality

Environment

Structural Assumptions

Optimality

Autonomy

\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}

\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}

MAB

NUC

✓

✓

✓

-

✗

✓

✗

MDP

NUC

✓

✓

✓

-

✗

✓

✗

Table 22
:
The performance of counterfactual policies
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}
and experimental policies
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
in canonical environments including MABs and MDPs.

We have established that an agent following a counterfactual policy generally outperforms an interventional one, as summarized in Table
22
.
The “Environment” column lists canonical decision-making environments covered in this section, such as MABs and MDPs.
The “Structural Assumptions” column details additional structural assumptions applied to the environment, including no unmeasured confounding (NUC, Def.
40
).
The “Optimality” column indicates whether optimizing within the corresponding policy space could lead to an optimal decision strategy, using an optimal counterfactual policy in
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
as the baseline. Here, a check (cross) mark in
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}
under “Optimality” denotes whether experimental policies are capable (or not) of achieving optimal performance in the respective environment, compared to their counterfactual counterparts.
In environments where unobserved confounders generally exist, full autonomy (as indicated in the “Autonomy” column) is attainable only when it does not compromise optimality. This occurs when the performance of experimental policies
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}
and counterfactual policies
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
coincide.

This suggests a fundamental tradeoff between optimality and autonomy in the design of RL systems. While full autonomy is preferable, the agent could potentially achieve superior performance by leveraging a human’s capabilities through counterfactual reasoning.
For instance, as demonstrated in Example
104
, a counterfactual policy in
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
characterizes interactions in a semi-autonomous system that incorporates the human operator’s intended action as input.
On the other hand, deploying an interventional policy in
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}
eliminates the need for a human operator in the underlying environment, resulting in a fully autonomous system. Consequently, Thm.
108
implies that while full autonomy is preferable, the agent could potentially achieve better performance by leveraging the human’s intuition through a counterfactual decision criterion.

We will model this autonomy-optimality tradeoff as a constrained transfer of control (TOC) problem such that a decision system tries to maximize its rewards while repeatedly switching between experimental and counterfactual policies, subject to a budget constraint over the total time of using the agent’s intended action, i.e., no more than
\delta\in(0,1)
ratio of the total running time. For instance, an autonomous vehicle could ask for the human driver’s input when necessary, but no more than
\delta=10\%
of the total expected driving time.
Formally, we first define hybrid policies, which is a restricted family of counterfactual policies
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
that contains experimental policies
\Pi_{\textsc{exp}}
.

Definition 113

(Hybrid Policies)
.

For an MDP environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, a hybrid policy space
\Pi_{\textsc{hyb}}
is a subset of counterfactual policies
\Pi_{\textsc{ctf}}
. For any hybrid policy
\pi=\left(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{H}\right)
in
\Pi_{\textsc{hyb}}
, its decision rule
\pi_{i}=(f_{i}\circ g_{i})
is a composition of functions
f_{i},g_{i}
such that, for
i=1,\dots,H
,

•

g_{i}
is a probability distribution mapping from the current state
S_{i}
to an extended action
A_{i}\in\{0,1\}
where “
0
” stands for
experimental decision criterion
and “
1
” for
counterfactual decision criterion
. That is,

\displaystyle A_{i}\sim g_{i}\left(A_{i}\mid S_{i}\right)

(429)

•

f_{i}
is a probability distribution mapping from the current state
S_{i}
, the intended action
X_{i}
, and the extended acton
A_{i}
to the realized action
X^{\prime}_{i}
. Moreover, the extended action
A_{i}
decides the realized action
X_{i}
as follows:

\displaystyle f_{i}\left(X_{i}\mid S_{i},X^{\prime}_{i},A_{i}\right)=\begin{cases}f_{i}\left(X_{i}\mid S_{i}\right),&\text{if }A_{i}=0\\
f_{i}\left(X_{i}\mid S_{i},X^{\prime}_{i}\right),&\text{if }A_{i}=1\end{cases}

(430)

\blacksquare

In words, the decision-making process of a hybrid policy consists of two phases. First, it determines an extended action
A_{i}\in\{0,1\}
based on the current state
S_{i}
. Here,
A_{i}
is a switch variable indicating whether to utilize counterfactual reasoning (
A_{i}=1
), or stay in the standard experimental decision criterion (
A_{i}=0
). The human’s intuition
X_{i}
is included as an evidence to decide the realized decision
X^{\prime}_{i}
if
A_{i}=1
; otherwise, it is ignored. Fig.
48
shows the augmented causal diagram of an MDP environment induced by a hybrid policy where extended actions
A_{i}
are added to control the modes of decision-making criterion. Fix a budget
\delta\in[0,1]
. For a hybrid policy
\pi
that does not apply counterfactual decision criterion with a probability higher than
\delta
as the decision stage
i
grows, it must satisfy the following constraint:

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}P_{\pi}\left(A_{i}=1\right)\leq\delta.

(431)

If the above equation holds, this means that an agent following policy
\pi
will not utilize the human’s intuition more than
\delta\times 100\%
of the total running time in long-term.

S
1
X
1
X’
1
A
1
Y
1
S
2
X
2
X’
2
A
2

3

3
