from behavioral cloning using different input states in randomly sampled SCMs consistent with each causal diagram.

Experiment 12
.

We evaluate BC algorithms in randomly sampled SCMs consistent with various causal diagrams. These algorithms use different criteria to select input states/features for every action, which we summarize as follows. (1) Our proposed causal BC selects input states using the imitation backdoor condition, following the procedure described in Thm.
127
. (2) Standard BC algorithm mimics the expert’s nominal policy, taking observed direct parents for every action as input. (3) Standard BC algorithm considers all state variables available to the imitator at the time of each action, described by the policy space.

For each causal diagram,
10,000
random discrete causal models are sampled, the expert’s performance is measured, and then the expert’s policy is replaced with imitating policies
\pi_{i}(X_{i}\mid\bm{Z}_{i})=P(X_{i}|\bm{Z}_{i})
for every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
, with input covariates
\bm{Z}_{i}
determined by the tested BC algorithm described above. The performance of algorithms is evaluated using the gap between the expert’s performance
\mathbb{E}[Y]
and the expected reward of the policy
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]
obtained by the imitator. Simulation results are shown in Table
23
, with causal diagrams and policy spaces described in the first column, followed by the performance gap between the expert and the imitator.

For the diagram
\mathcal{G}_{1}
, including
Z
when developing a policy for
X_{1},X_{2}
leads to a biased answer, which makes the average error of using all observed covariates (red) larger than just the sampling fluctuations present in the other columns.
Similarly,
Z
needs to be considered in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
, but it is not explicitly used by
X_{2}
, so a method relying only on observed parents leads to bias here. In
\mathcal{G}_{3}
,
Z
is not observed at the time of determining action
X_{1}
, making standard BC algorithms fail to achieve the expert’s performance. Our method recognizes that
X_{2}
’s policy can fix the imitation error made at
X_{1}
, and is the only method that leads to an unbiased result. Finally, in
\mathcal{G}_{4}
, the non-causal approaches cannot determine non-clonability, and return biased results in all such cases.
\blacksquare

8.2
Causal Inverse RL

This section studies an alternative imitation learning strategy, called
inverse reinforcement learning
(IRL,
136
;
213
;
66
;
55
), in a CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
. Similar to behavioral cloning, detailed parametrizations of the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and the reward function
\mathcal{R}
are not fully unknown. However, the imitator now has access to a parametric family
\mathscr{R}\subseteq\left\{\forall\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\right\}
containing the actual reward function
\mathcal{R}
. We will start the discussion by describing an IRL strategy when the NUC assumption (Def.
40
) holds. We will next relax the NUC and study inverse RL in a more general class of causal models, provided with a causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
encoding its qualitative knowledge.

Following the game-theoretic approach introduced in
(
180
)
, we formulate imitating learning (Def.
117
) as learning to play a two-player zero-sum game in which the agent chooses a policy, and the adversarial (e.g., Nature) chooses a worst-case reward function from the parametric reward family
\mathscr{R}
. Now consider the optimization problem defined as follows.

\displaystyle\nu^{*}=\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\max_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathscr{R}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right].

(474)

The inner maximization in the above equation can be viewed as a
causal IRL
step where we attempt to “guess” a worst-case reward function
\hat{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathscr{R}
that prioritizes the expert’s policy. That is, the gap in the performance between the expert’s and the imitator’s policies is maximized. Meanwhile, note that the expert’s reward
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]
is not affected by the imitating’s policy
\pi
. The outer minimization is equivalent to a planning step that finds a policy
\pi^{*}
optimizing a CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\hat{\mathcal{R}}\right\rangle
with the worst-case reward
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
. Obviously, the solution
\pi^{*}
is an imitating policy if the performance gap
\nu^{*}=0
.
In cases where the expert is sub-optimal, we may have
\nu^{*}<0
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]<\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right],\;\exists\pi\in\Pi

(475)

In words, the solution
\pi^{*}
will dominate the expert’s policy
f_{\bm{X}}
in the worst-case scenario, regardless of the detailed form of the reward function
\mathcal{R}
. To some extent, the imitating policy
\pi^{*}
ignores the sub-optimal expert and instead exploits prior knowledge about the unknown reward function. When the prior knowledge is informative, solving the optimization program in Eq.
474
could produce a policy that could significantly outperform the expert in the underlying environment with respect to the unknown reward function, while at the same time guaranteed to be no worse.

Example 129

(Inverse RL in MAB)
.

Consider again the MAB environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Eq.
442
concerning learning a driving policy from highway footage. Suppose that the reward function
\mathcal{R}(Y)=\alpha Y
is linear with a positive coefficient
\alpha>0
. The minimax program in Eq.
474
could be written as

\displaystyle\nu^{*}

\displaystyle=\min_{\pi(X)}\max_{\alpha>0}\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right]-\alpha\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]

(476)

Evaluating the distance from the front car
Y
in the environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y]

\displaystyle=P(X=1)=0.9

(477)

Note that the NUC condition holds in this environment. The interventional quantity
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]
is a function of the observational distribution
P(X,Y)
and policy
\pi(x)
is given by, following the DP formula in Thm.
45
(or the IPW formula in Thm.
43
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=0]\pi(X=0)+\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=1]\pi(X=1)

(478)

\displaystyle=\pi(X=1)

(479)

The last step holds since values of
Y
are determined by
Y\leftarrow X
. By substituting Eqs.
477
and
479
into Eq.
476
, we can further write the performance gap
\nu^{*}
as:

\displaystyle\nu^{*}

\displaystyle=\min_{\pi(X)}\max_{\alpha>0}\alpha\left(0.9-\pi(X=1)\right)

(480)

For any coefficient
\alpha>0
, the above program is minimized with a solution
\pi(X=1)=1
. Solving the above equation thus leads to an IRL policy
\pi_{\textsc{irl}}:X\leftarrow 1
. In this case, the performance gap is equal to
\nu^{*}=-0.1\alpha<0
, which means that the IRL imitator outperforms the expert.

To verify this intuition, we evaluate the expected reward
\mathcal{R}(Y)
in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi_{\textsc{irl}}}
, following the evaluation formula in Eq.
444
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\textsc{irl}}}\left[\mathcal{R}(Y)\right]=\alpha\pi_{\textsc{irl}}(X=1)

(481)

This means that the IRL policy achieves the expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\textsc{irl}}}\left[\mathcal{R}(Y)\right]=\alpha
, which outperforms both the expert and BC’s policies
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(Y)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\textsc{bc}}}\left[\mathcal{R}(Y)\right]=0.9\alpha
(see Example
119
for detailed computations).
\blacksquare

Despite its clear semantics, solving the optimization problem in Eq.
474
requires the detailed parametrization of the underlying SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, which is not accessible to the agent in most real-world settings. It is then important to study conditions under which the solution of Eq.
474
is identifiable and could be formulated from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
. Fix a reward function
\mathcal{R}\in\mathscr{R}
. First, the expect’s performance
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]
is obtainable from
P(\bm{V})
by computing the arithmetic mean of
\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})
weighted by the marginal distribution
P(\bm{Y})
. If the NUC assumption (Def.
40
) holds, the imitator’s performance
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y});\mathcal{M}^{*}\right]
is computable from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
, following off-policy learning algorithms including IPW (Thm.
43
) and DP (Thm.
45
). The imitator could then formulate the minimax program in Eq.
474
from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
, the hypothesis reward class
\mathscr{R}
, and the policy space
\Pi
. Solving this optimization program leads to an imitating policy. We demonstrate in Example
129
the IRL strategy under NUC in an MAB environment.

On the other hand, however, when the input variables of the expert and the imitator’s policies mismatch, and unobserved confounders generally exist, performing IRL with the standard off-policy evaluation does not necessarily lead to an imitating policy achieving the expert’s performance. The following example illustrates the challenges of unobserved confounders for IRL methods.

Example 130

(Inverse RL fails without NUC)
.

Consider the alternative MAB environment
\mathcal{M}
described in Eq.
462
where the front car’s deceleration
U
is an unobserved confounder affecting both the human demonstrator’s action
X
and the distance
Y
between the demonstrator and the front car; so the NUC does not hold in this environment.

Evaluating the expected value of
Y
in this MAB environment
\mathcal{M}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X\oplus U]

(482)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\neg U\oplus U]

(483)

Computing the above equation gives the evaluation
\mathbb{E}[Y]=1
. Applying the DP formula in Thm.
45
(or the IPW formula in Thm.
43
) gives the following evaluation, for any policy
\pi(X)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=0]\pi(X=0)+\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=1]\pi(X=1)

(484)

Among the above quantities, the conditional mean
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X]
is given by, for any
x
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X\oplus U\mid X=x]

(485)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[x\oplus\neg x\mid X=x]

(486)

The last step holds since values of
X
are given by
X\leftarrow\neg U
in the MAB environment
\mathcal{M}
. Computing the above equation gives
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x]=1
for
x=0,1
. Eq.
484
could be further written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]

\displaystyle=\pi(X=0)+\pi(X=1)=1

(487)

Again, let
\mathcal{R}(Y)=\alpha Y
be a linear reward function with a positive coefficient
\alpha>0
. By substituting evaluations
\mathbb{E}[Y]=1
and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]=1
into Eq.
476
, we obtain the following minimax program:

\displaystyle\nu^{*}

\displaystyle=\min_{\pi(X)}\max_{\alpha>0}\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[Y\right]-\alpha\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[Y\right]

(488)

\displaystyle=\min_{\pi(X)}\max_{\alpha>0}\alpha-\alpha

(489)

\displaystyle=0

(490)

This means that any fixed coefficient
\alpha>0
, the imitator is able to achieve the expert’s performance using any policy
\pi(x)
. To verify this conclusion, let an IRL policy
\pi_{\textsc{irl}}:X\leftarrow 1
. Evaluating the expected reward
\mathcal{R}(Y)
in submodel
\mathcal{M}_{\pi_{\textsc{irl}}}
implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\textsc{irl}}}\left[\mathcal{R}(Y)\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha\mathbb{E}_{X\leftarrow 1}\left[Y\right]

(491)

\displaystyle=\alpha\mathbb{E}[1\oplus U]

(492)

\displaystyle=0.5\alpha

(493)

The last step holds since
U
is uniformly drawn over the binary domain
\{0,1\}
. This means that the IRL policy (
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\textsc{irl}}}\left[\mathcal{R}(Y)\right]=0.5\alpha
) fails to achieve the expert’s performance
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}(Y)]=\alpha
.
\blacksquare

8.2.1
Minimal Imitation Backdoor

We will next study causal IRL in more general settings where the NUC assumption does not hold, and there exist unobserved confounders in the demonstration data affecting both actions and other variables in the environment. Our algorithm relies on a refinement of the imitation backdoor condition (Def.
123
), based on the concept of minimal
d
-separating sets.

Definition 131

(Minimal Imitation Backdoor)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal diagram and
\bm{X},\bm{Y}\in\bm{V}
be subsets of variables. An imitation admissible space
\Pi
over
\bm{X}
is said to be
minimal
if there exists no proper subspace
\Pi^{\prime}\subset\Pi
satisfying the imitation backdoor w.r.t.
\bm{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
.
\blacksquare

In words, an imitation admissible space
\Pi=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{S}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{X_{i}\in\bm{X}}
is minimal if for every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
,
\bm{S}_{i}
is a minimal
d
-separating set between action
X_{i}
and reward signals
\bm{Y}
is the manipulated diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{i}},\pi_{i+1},\dots,\pi_{H}}
; or states
\bm{S}_{i}=\emptyset
whenever
X_{i}
is not an ancestor of
\bm{Y}
in diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{i+1},\dots,\pi_{H}}
.

Z
1
X
1
Z
2
X
2
Y

(a)

Z
1
X
1
Z
2
X
2
Y

(b)

Z
1
X
1
Z
2
X
2
Y

(c)

Figure 52
:
Causal diagrams where
X
represents an action (shaded blue) and
Y
represents a latent reward (shaded red). Input covariates of the policy space
\Pi
are shaded in light blue.

Example 132
.

Consider the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
described in Fig.
52(a)
and a policy space
\Pi_{1}=\left\{\langle X_{1},\{Z_{1}\}\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{Z_{2}\}\rangle\right\}
. For a policy
\left(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\right)\in\Pi
, the manipulated diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi_{2}}
is shown in Fig.
52(b)
. It is verifiable that
\Pi_{1}
satisfies the imitation backdoor condition w.r.t. the outcome
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
since the following independence relationships hold:
(X_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z_{1})
in
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{1}},\pi_{2}}
and
(X_{2}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z_{2})
in
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{2}}}
, respectively. However, the same space
\Pi_{1}
is not minimal since
\{Z_{1}\}
is not a minimal
d
-separating set and
(X_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 3.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y)
holds in
\mathcal{G}^{(1)}_{\underline{X_{1}}}
. On the other hand,
\Pi_{2}=\left\{\langle X_{1},\emptyset\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{Z_{2}\}\rangle\right\}
is minimal imitation admissible since conditioning on the covariate set
\{Z_{2}\}

d
-separates the backdoor path
X_{2}\leftarrow Z_{2}\rightarrow Y
in diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{X_{2}}}
; removing node
Z_{2}
opens the backdoor path.
\blacksquare

A key property of a minimal imitation admissible space
\Pi
is that for every policy
\pi\sim\Pi
, the interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
is identifiable from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
, provided with the structural assumptions encoded in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
.

Theorem 133
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal diagram,
\Pi
be a policy space over actions
\bm{X}
, and
\bm{Y}\subseteq\bm{V}
be a subset of variables. If there exists a subspace
\Pi^{\prime}=\left\{\langle X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\rangle\right\}_{i=1}^{H}
contained in
\Pi
such that
\Pi^{\prime}
is minimal imitation admissible w.r.t.
\bm{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
, then for every policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}
, the interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
is computable from
P(\bm{V})
and given by

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left(\bm{y}\right)=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{z}}_{H}}P\left(\bm{y}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{z}}_{H}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{H}P\left(\bm{z}_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{z}}_{i-1}\right)\pi_{i}\left(x_{i}\mid\bm{z}_{i}\right).

(494)

where
\bar{\bm{X}}_{i}=\left\{X_{1},\dots,X_{i}\right\}
and
\bar{\bm{Z}}_{i}=\left\{\bm{Z}_{1},\dots,\bm{Z}_{i}\right\}
are sequences of actions and input covariates up to the decision horizon
i=1,\dots,H
.
\blacksquare

However, the same identifiability result does not generally hold for policies in a non-minimal imitation admissible space. The following example demonstrates such an instance.

Example 134
.

Consider the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
of Fig.
52(a)
again. Let us focus on the minimal imitation admissible space

\displaystyle\Pi_{2}=\left\{\langle X_{1},\emptyset\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{Z_{2}\}\rangle\right\}

(495)

For ever policy
\pi\in\Pi_{2}
, the post-interventional diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
is shown in Fig.
52(b)
. By applying Thm.
133
we obtain

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left(y\right)=\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},z_{2}}P\left(y\mid x_{1},x_{2},z_{2}\right)P(z_{2}\mid x_{1})\pi_{2}(x_{2}\mid z_{2})\pi_{1}(x_{1})

(496)

On the other hand, the same identification result in Thm.
133
does not necessarily hold for a non-minimal imitation admissible space.

More specifically, consider a policy space

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}=\left\{\langle X_{1},\{Z_{1}\}\rangle,\langle X_{2},\{Z_{2}\}\rangle\right\}

(497)

For ever policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1}
, the post-interventional diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
is shown in Fig.
52(c)
. As discussed previously (Example
132
),
\Pi_{1}
is not minimal. This means that, for any policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1}
, the interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(Y\right)
is not computable from the identification formula in Eq.
494
. More generally,
P_{\pi}\left(Y\right)
for policies
\pi\in\Pi_{1}
is not identifiable from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
in diagram
\mathcal{G}
. Following the decomposition in Eq.
212
,
P_{\pi}\left(Y\right)
can be written as,

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left(y\right)=\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},z_{1},z_{2}}P_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left(y,z_{1},z_{2}\right)\pi_{1}(x_{1}\mid z_{1})\pi_{2}(x_{2}\mid z_{2})

(498)

Prop.
64
implies that
P_{\pi}\left(Y\right)
is identifiable if and only if the interventional distribution
P_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left(Y,Z_{1},Z_{2}\right)
is identifiable in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
. However, such quantity
P_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left(Y,Z_{1},Z_{2}\right)
is not identifiable due to the presence of the bi-directed path
X_{2}\dashleftarrow\dasharrow Z_{2}\dashleftarrow\dasharrow Z_{1}\dashleftarrow\dasharrow Y

(
187
, Thm. 16)
. Indeed, the non-identifiability of the effects of policies
\pi\in\Pi_{1}
in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}
described in Fig.
52(a)
has been shown in
(
190
;
38
)
.
\blacksquare

The concept of minimal imitation backdoor in Def.
131
and the identification result in Thm.
133
provide a natural algorithm for performing causal IRL when unobserved confounders generally exist. Instead of searching for imitating policies in the policy space
\Pi
, the agent will focus on a minimal imitation admissible subspace
\Pi^{\prime}\subseteq\Pi
.
Specifically, as discussed previously in Sec.
8.1
, there exist efficient algorithms finding admissible policy subspaces satisfying the imitation backdoor. Once such an admissible subspace is found, one could obtain a minimal imitation admissible subspace by iteratively removing input state variables from
\bm{S}_{i}
for every action
X_{i}
until the imitation backdoor does not hold. This procedure could be done in polynomial steps with regard to the total number of states
\bm{S}
and actions
\bm{X}
variables.

8.2.2
Imitation via Inverse RL

Once a minimal imitation admissible subspace
\Pi^{\prime}\subseteq\Pi
is obtained, one could obtain an imitating policy by solving the minimax program in Eq.
474
with the policy space
\Pi
substituted with
\Pi^{\prime}
. By expanding values of
\bm{Y}
, this optimization program could be written as,

\displaystyle\nu^{*}=\min_{\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}}\max_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathscr{R}}\sum_{\bm{y}}\mathcal{R}(\bm{y})(\underbrace{P(\bm{y})}_{\text{expert's occupancy}}-\underbrace{P_{\pi}\left(\bm{y}\right)}_{\text{imitator's occupancy}})

(499)

Among quantities in the above equation, the first term is the expert’s occupancy measures over domains of signals
\bm{Y}
, which is a marginal observational distribution
P(\bm{Y})
. The second term is the expert’s occupancy measures over domains of
\bm{Y}
, which is an interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
. Since
\Pi^{\prime}
is a minimal subspace satisfying the imitation backdoor criterion, applying Thm.
133
permits one to compute
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
and the policy
\pi
.
65
65

65

More generally, the imitator could search over all policies
\pi\in\Pi
such that the imitator’s occupancy measure
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{Y}\right)
induced by
\text{do}(\pi)
is identifiable in diagram
\mathcal{G}
. This imitation approach has been studied in
(
160
)
.

Provided with some common choices of the hypothesis class
\mathscr{R}
, the minimax program in Eq.
499
is solvable using some state-of-art IRL algorithms. Due to this reason, we consistently refer to Eq.
499
as the
canonical IRL program
. To make this argument more precise, we will demonstrate this reduction procedure with the multiplicative-weights algorithm (MWAL)
(
180
)
and the generative adversarial imitation learning (GAIL)
(
66
)
.

Causal MWAL

(
1
;
180
)
study IRL in Markov decision processes where the reward function
\mathcal{R}(\bm{y})
is a linear combination of
k
-length
feature expectations
vectors
\bm{\phi}(\bm{y})
. Particularly, let
\mathcal{R}(\bm{y})=\bm{w}\cdot\bm{\phi}(\bm{y})
for a coefficient vector
\bm{w}
in a convex set

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{k}=\left\{\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\mid\lVert\bm{w}\rVert_{1}=1\text{ and }\bm{w}\succeq\bm{0}\right\}.

(500)

Let
\bm{\phi}^{(i)}
be the
i
-th component of feature vector
\bm{\phi}
and let deterministic policies with space
\Pi
be ordered by
\pi^{(1)},\dots,\pi^{(n)}
. The canonical program in Eq.
499
is reducible to a two-person zero-sum matrix game under linearity.

Proposition 135
.

For a hypothesis class
\mathscr{R}=\{\mathcal{R}=\bm{w}\cdot\bm{\phi}\mid\bm{w}\in\mathbb{P}^{k}\}
, the solution
\nu^{*}
of the canonical program in Eq.
499
is obtainable by solving the following minimax problem

\displaystyle\nu^{*}=\min_{\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}}\max_{\bm{w}\in\mathbb{P}^{k}}\bm{w}^{\top}\bm{G}\pi,

(501)

where
\bm{G}
is a
k\times n
matrix given by
\bm{G}(i,j)=\sum_{\bm{y}}\bm{\phi}^{(i)}(\bm{y})\left(P(\bm{y})-P_{\pi^{(j)}}(\bm{y})\right)
.
\blacksquare

There exist effective multiplicative weights algorithms for solving the matrix game in Eq.
501
, including MW
(
54
)
and MWAL
(
180
)
.

Causal GAIL

(
66
)
introduces the GAIL algorithm for learning an imitating policy in Markov decision processes with a general family of non-linear reward functions. In particular,
\mathcal{R}(\bm{y})
takes values in the real space
\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{\bm{Y}}
where
\mathbb{R}^{\bm{Y}}=\{r:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
. The complexity of reward function
\mathcal{R}
is penalized by a convex regularization function
\psi\left(\mathcal{R}\right)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\nu^{*}=\min_{\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}}\max_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{\bm{Y}}}\sum_{\bm{y}}\mathcal{R}(\bm{y})\left(P(\bm{y})-P_{\pi}(\bm{y})\right)-\psi(\mathcal{R})

(502)

Henceforth, we will consistently refer to Eq.
502
as the
penalized canonical program
of causal IRL. It is often preferable to solve its conjugate form. Formally,

Proposition 136
.

For a hypothesis class
\mathscr{R}=\{\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
regularized by
\psi
, the solution
\nu^{*}
of the penalized canonical program in Eq.
502
is obtainable by solving the following problem

\displaystyle\nu^{*}=\min_{\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}}\psi^{*}\left(P-P_{\pi}\right)

(503)

where
\psi^{*}
be a conjugate function of
\psi
and is given by
\psi^{*}=\max_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{\bm{Y}}}a^{\top}\mathcal{R}-\psi(\mathcal{R})
.
\blacksquare

Eq.
503
seeks a policy
\pi
which minimizes the divergence of joint probabilities over reward signals
\bm{Y}
between the imitator and the expert, as measured by the function
\psi^{*}
.
When we utilize a regularizer
\psi(r)
similar to
(
66
, Eq. 13)
, the convex conjugate function
\psi^{*}
in Eq.
503
is further written as:

\displaystyle\min_{\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}}\psi^{*}\left(P-P_{\pi}\right)=\min_{\pi\in\Pi^{\prime}}\max_{D\in(0,1)^{\bm{Y}}}E\left[\log(D(\bm{Y}))\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\log(1-D(\bm{Y}))\right],

(504)

where function
D\in\mathscr{D}(\bm{Y})\mapsto(0,1)
is a discriminator classifier (e.g, a neural network). The above equation draws the connection between causal imitation learning and the computational framework of generative adversarial networks
(
57
)
, which could be viewed as two neural networks competing against each other in a zero-sum game. When the discriminator
D
cannot distinguish the occupancy measure generated by the policy
\pi
from the expert, then
\pi
has successfully matched the expert’s performance. Solving the minimax program of Eq.
504
requires finding a saddle point
(\pi,D)
. This could be done by iteratively optimizing policy parameters
\pi
and discriminator
D
following the implementation procedure of GAIL algorithm
(
66
)
.

Experiment 13
.

We demonstrate our causal imitation framework on an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
compatible with the causal diagram in Fig.
52(a)
. Particularly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}=\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{1},U_{2},U_{3},U_{4}\},\bm{L}=\emptyset,\bm{V}=\{X_{1},X_{2},Z_{1},X_{2},Y\},\mathscr{F},P(\bm{U})\rangle

(505)

where structural functions
\mathscr{F}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}=\begin{cases}Z_{1}\leftarrow U_{1}\oplus U_{3},\\
X_{1}\sim\texttt{Bern}(0.68)\\
Z_{2}\leftarrow U_{1}\oplus U_{2}\oplus U_{4},\\
X_{2}\leftarrow U_{2}\oplus Z_{2}\\
Y\leftarrow\left(X_{1},X_{2},Z_{1},Z_{2},U_{3}\right)\end{cases}

(506)

Among quantities in the above equation, reward signal
Y=\left(Y_{1},\dots Y_{5}\right)
is a feature vector containing
5
elements; the exogenous distribution
P(U_{1},U_{2},U_{3},U_{4})
is defined such that
U_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,4
are independent variables given by

\displaystyle U_{1}\sim\texttt{Bern}(0.8),

\displaystyle U_{2}\sim\texttt{Bern}(0.8),

\displaystyle U_{3}\sim\texttt{Bern}(0.2)

\displaystyle U_{4}\sim\texttt{Bern}(0.1)

(507)

The agent’s goal is to optimize a CDM
\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
where environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is defined in Eq.
505
; the policy space
\Pi=\left\{\left\langle X_{1},\{Z_{1}\}\right\rangle,\left\langle X_{2},\{Z_{1},X_{1},Z_{2}\}\right\rangle\right\}
; and the reward function
\mathcal{R}(Y)=\oplus_{i=1}^{5}Y_{i}
.

Figure 53
:
Simulation results evaluating causal IRL when imitation backdoor condition holds.

We will then apply different imitation strategies to learn an imitating policy in space
\Pi
without detailed parametrization of the reward function
\mathcal{R}(Y)
. These imitation algorithms are

•

Standard BC algorithm utilizes all observed states
\bm{S}_{i}
for every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
.

•

Standard IRL algorithm utilizes all observed states
\bm{S}_{i}
for every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
. We will apply GAIL algorithm
(
180
)
when
\mathcal{R}(Y)
is non-linear;

•

Causal-BC algorithm, described in Thm.
127
, selects a set of covariates
\bm{Z}_{i}
for every action
X_{i}\in\bm{X}
following the imitation backdoor criterion (Def.
123
). It then learns an imitating policy
\pi
with subspace
\Pi^{\prime}=\{\langle X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\rangle\}_{X_{i}\in\bm{X}}
using standard BC algorithms.

•

Our proposed Causal-IRL algorithm first finds a minimal imitation admissible subspace
\Pi^{\prime}
(Thm.
133
) and then obtains an imitating policy by solving the canonical program in Eq.
499
. We will use Causal GAIL algorithm since
\mathcal{R}(Y)
is non-linear. Reward augmentation (RA) is performed to incorporate the parametric knowledge that
\mathcal{R}(Y)
is a monotone function concerning values of
Y_{1},Y_{2}

(
111
)
. This is done by adding an additional regularization function in Eq.
504
to encourage assigning higher values of features
Y_{1},Y_{2}
.

Simulation results are shown in Fig.
53
. The analysis reveals that Causal-IRL consistently outperforms the expert’s policy and other imitation strategies by exploiting additional parametric knowledge about the reward function; Causal-BC obtains a policy that mimics the expert’s performance. As expected, BC and IRL failed to obtain a policy that matches the expert’s performance.
\blacksquare

This section investigates imitation learning in the semantics of structural causal models. The goal is to find an imitating policy that can perform at least as well as the expert behaviors from combinations of demonstration data and qualitative knowledge about the data-generating process represented as a causal diagram. First, we provided a novel graphical criterion that is sufficient for determining the feasibility of learning an imitating policy that mimics the expert’s performance. When such a condition holds, one could obtain an effective imitating learning using standard behavioral cloning. We also investigate imitation learning via inverse reinforcement learning (IRL), provided with additional quantitative knowledge about the reward function. We provide a graphical criterion based on the sequential backdoor, which allows one to obtain an imitating policy by solving a canonical optimization equation of causal IRL. Such a canonical formulation addresses the challenge of the presence of unobserved confounders (UCs) and is solvable by leveraging standard IRL algorithms.

9
Conclusions

The current generation of AI agents capable of optimal decision-making builds on the theoretical framework of reinforcement learning (RL). Most of these RL systems do not explicitly represent the underlying causal models or engage in causal reasoning. On the other hand, there is a growing recognition across many fields and sciences that effective decision-making relies on an understanding of the causal mechanisms in the environment. For example, an intelligent robot needs to grasp the cause-and-effect relationships within its surroundings to plan its actions effectively; a physician must understand the effects of available medications to devise a suitable treatment strategy for her patients; an economist, too, needs to envision the relationship between skill sets and the future job market in order to create an effective educational policy. These scenarios illustrate how decision-making across various sectors of society depends on understanding complex, dynamic, and often unobserved causal mechanisms. Although there have been some attempts to integrate causal knowledge into RL tasks, a systematic approach and a cohesive foundation are still lacking.

To address this challenge, we combine the capabilities of RL agents with Pearl’s Structural Causal Models (SCMs) theory to encode causal knowledge and perform counterfactual reasoning. This marriage leads to an algorithmic and theoretical framework for robust decision-making under uncertainties, which is part of an emerging branch of research called
Causal Reinforcement Learning
(CRL). Building on this framework, we are able to bring improvement to RL algorithms in some key aspects. First, The CRL framework enables us to relax certain key assumptions regarding the causal mechanisms that generate the observed data. We have developed innovative algorithms that are resilient to unobserved confounding bias in offline settings, which include off-policy learning and imitation learning. Additionally, we proposed more efficient online learning algorithms that effectively identify optimal policies while achieving near-optimal regret. These advancements leverage causal conclusions drawn from biased offline data.

The final important distinction presented in this manuscript is the difference between the regimes in which AI agents operate to interact with their environment. Specifically, supervised learning agents identify patterns from data gathered through passive observation, while reinforcement learning (RL) agents actively engage with the system and modify their policies based on the responses they receive. By generalizing these interaction regimes, we open up new learning possibilities that have not been explored in the existing literature. The problem of where to intervene allows us to design agents to achieve better performance by combining both passive observation and active intervention. Counterfactual randomization generalizes the classic Fisherian randomized experience, enriching agents with capabilities of counterfactual reasoning, which we believe is critical to design the next generation of AI systems.

Acknowledgements

This research was supported in part by the NSF, ONR, AFOSR, DARPA, DoE, Amazon, JP Morgan,
and The Alfred P. Sloan Foundation.

Appendix A
Comparison with Partially Observed MDPs

In this section, we will extend the Causal Hierachy Theorem (CHT,
(
15
, Thm. 1)
) to a general family of stochastic processes where the Markov property does not hold for states and actions across time steps. Specifically, we focus on the partially observed Markov decision processes (POMDP,
170
) that model the dependence between observations over a long sequence of time steps through latent states and dynamics. Formally,

Definition 137

(Standard POMDP
(
170
)
)
.

A partially observable Markov decision process is tuple
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathscr{D}(O),\mathcal{T},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{O}\right\rangle
where

1.

\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
describe a Markov decision process;

2.

\mathscr{D}(O)
is a finite set of observations the agent can perceive of its world, called the observation space;

3.

\mathcal{O}(x,s,o)
is the observation function, which gives, for each action
X_{i}=x
and resulting state
S_{i+1}=s
, a probability distribution over possible observations
O_{i+1}=o
.

A policy
\pi
in a standard POMDP is a sequence of stochastic decision rules
\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\dots\}
; each decision rule
\pi_{i}
is a function mapping from the observations and actions history
\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i-1}
to a probability distribution over the action space
\mathcal{D}(X)
. Given a policy
\pi
and a distribution over the initial state and observation
P(S_{1},O_{1})
, every standard PMDP model defines a joint distribution over observations
\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:H}
, actions
\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:H}
, and rewards
\bar{\bm{Y}}_{1:H}
up to decision horizon
H
, i.e.,

\begin{split}&P_{\pi}(\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})\\
&=\sum_{\bar{\bm{s}}_{1:H}}P(s_{1},o_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{H}\pi(x_{i}\mid s_{i})\mathcal{T}(s_{i},x_{i},s_{i+1})\mathcal{O}(x_{i},s_{i+1},o_{i+1})\mathds{1}\{\mathcal{R}(s_{i},x_{i})=y_{i}\}\end{split}

(508)

Due to the presence of latent states, the Markov property no longer holds with regard to the perceived observations. This argument is corroborated with the network structure in the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{pomdp}}
of Fig.
10(e)
. For every stage
i=1,2,\dots
, conditioning on the observation
O_{i}
and action
X_{i}
fails to block all paths from observed history
O_{j},X_{j},Y_{j}
for
j<i
to any future observation
O_{k}
, action
X_{k}
, and reward
Y_{k}
for
k>i
(Def.
18
). Specifically, such long-sequence dependency is generated from the latent states
S_{i}
, e.g., the open causal path
O_{1}\leftarrow S_{1}\rightarrow S_{2}\rightarrow O_{2}
, which could be represented using a standard POMDP.

Example 138

(POMDP, Observational)
.

Consider the following SCM environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
adapted from Eq.
5
, unrolling over stages
i=1,2,\dots

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3}\},\bm{V}=\{X_{i},Y_{i},S_{i},O_{i}\},\mathscr{F}=\left\{\mathscr{F}^{*}_{i}\right\},P^{*}(\bm{U})\right\rangle_{i=1,2,\dots}.

(509)

The above SCM is identical to the model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
defined in Eq.
5
, except that the underlying state
S_{i}
is now latent to the learner; and the endogenous variables include an observation
O_{i}
fixed at a constant
O_{i}\leftarrow 0
. This means that the learning agent, interacting with the environment, accesses samples drawn from marginal observational
P(\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})
or interventional distribution
P_{\pi}(\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:H},\bar{\bm{y}}_{1:H})
, depending on the regimes of interactions.

We compute the observational distributions
P\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}\right]
and summarize them using a standard POMDP
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathscr{D}(O),\mathcal{T}_{\text{obs}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{obs}},\mathcal{O}\right\rangle
where
\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{obs}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{obs}}
form a standard MDP described in Example
34
; the observation function
\mathcal{O}(x,s,o)=1
for observation
o=0
given any action
X_{i}=x
and subsequent state
S_{i+1}=s
. Fig.
54
(a) shows a finite automaton that describes its detailed system dynamics. The shaded ellipse around the states
S=0
and
S=1
indicates that both states yield the same observation.
\blacksquare

Following a similar argument, we could show that any interventional distribution evaluated in a SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
graphically described in Fig.
10(e)
violates the Markov property, leading to an alternative standard POMDP representation.

Example 139

(POMDP, Interventional)
.

Consider again the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Example
138
. Its interventional distributions
P_{\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}}\left[Y\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i}\right]
a standard POMDP
\left\langle\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathscr{D}(O),\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}},\mathcal{O}\right\rangle
where

\mathscr{D}(S),\mathscr{D}(X),\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}},\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}
are described in the standard MDP of Example
35
; the observation function
\mathcal{O}(x,s,o=0)=1
given any action
X_{i}=x
and state
S_{i+1}=s
. The system dynamics of this POMDP are described in the finite automaton of Fig.
54
(b). The ellipse around the states indicates that both states yield the same observation.
\blacksquare

In both examples above, the observational and interventional distributions evaluated in the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
could be represented using standard POMDPs. However, the detailed system dynamics in these standard POMDPs differ, as illustrated in the finite automata shown in Fig.
54
. One may wonder if it is possible to recover interventional quantities
\mathcal{T}_{\text{exp}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\text{exp}}
from the observational data in POMDP environments. Our next result shows this is not the case.

Causal Hierarchy Theorem (CHT) in POMDP environments.

Proposition 140
.

For any SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
compatible with the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{pomdp}}
of Fig.
10(e)
, there is an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
compatible with
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{pomdp}}
such that for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle P^{(1)}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right)=P^{*}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{*}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right]

(510)

while

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right)\neq P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]\neq\mathbb{E}^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]

(511)

\blacksquare

The following example constructs an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
that generates the observational distribution as the underlying environment
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, but differs significantly in interventional distributions.

Example 141

(POMDP, Observational
\not\Rightarrow
Interventional)
.

We will construct an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
where its system dynamics collapse to the associational layer (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
) with respect to the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Example
138
. More specifically,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3}\},\bm{V}=\{X_{i},Y_{i},S_{i},O_{i}\},\mathscr{F}=\left\{\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{i}\right\},P^{(1)}(\bm{U})\right\rangle_{i=1,2,\dots}.

(512)

Similarly to the construction of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, the above SCM is identical to the model
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
defined in Example
37
except that for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
, the learner does not observe the state
S_{i}
, but only receives new endogenous variable
O_{i}\leftarrow 0
. We compute the observational distributions
P\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}\right]
and the interventional distributions
P_{\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}}\left[Y\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i}\right]
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
, following the discussion in Example
37
. The analysis suggests that the observational and interventional distributions collapse in the model
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
, which could be described using the same finite-state automaton shown in Fig.
54
(a).

Compared with system dynamics in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, the model
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
coincides with
\mathcal{M}^{*}
in the observational distributions (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
), but deviates significantly in the interventional distributions (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
). More specifically, given any observations and actions’ history
\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}
, the induced reward function in
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(1)}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right]

(513)

\displaystyle=0.1

(514)

On the other hand, suppose the state occupancy rate
P(s_{i})=0.5
for any state
s_{i}=0,1
in the model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Evaluating the transition distribution in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
gives, for any action
x_{i}=0,1
and any state
s_{i+1}=0,1
,

\displaystyle P_{x_{i}}\left(s_{i+1}\right)=0.5.

(515)

For instance, let
x_{i}=0
and
s_{i+1}=1
. Expanding on the current state
S_{i}
gives,

\displaystyle P_{X_{i}\leftarrow 0}\left(S_{i+1}=1\right)

(516)

\displaystyle=P_{X_{i}\leftarrow 0}\left(S_{i+1}=1\mid S_{i}=0\right)P(S_{i}=0)+P_{X_{i}\leftarrow 0}\left(S_{i+1}=1\mid S_{i}=1\right)P(S_{i}=1)

(517)

\displaystyle=0.82\times 0.5+0.18\times 0.5

(518)

\displaystyle=0.5

(519)

The above equations imply the following intermediate reward evaluated in the model
\mathcal{M}^{*}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{i}}\mathbb{E}^{*}_{x_{i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid s_{i}\right]P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i-1}}(s_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i})

(520)

\displaystyle=0.5,

(521)

which deviates from the corresponding query in
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
. For instance, let
x_{i}=0
. Expanding on the current state
S_{i}
gives,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]

(522)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{*}_{X_{i}\leftarrow 0}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=0\right]P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i-1}}(S_{i}=0\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i})+\mathbb{E}^{*}_{X_{i}\leftarrow 0}\left[Y_{i}\mid S_{i}=1\right]P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i-1}}(S_{i}=1\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i})

(523)

\displaystyle=0.82P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i-1}}(S_{i}=0\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i})+0.18P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i-1}}(S_{i}=1\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i})

(524)

\displaystyle=0.5

(525)

The last step holds since the occupancy rate
P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i-1}}(s_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i})=0.5
for any state
s_{i}=0,1
. This example corroborates Prop.
140
and illustrates that observational queries are generally under-determined by randomized experiments in POMDP environments.
\blacksquare

The above example shows that interventional distributions in an unknown POMDP environment are generally not fully determined from the observational distribution. Conversely, we also show that it is generally infeasible to recover observational quantities from randomized experiments in non-Markov processes.

Proposition 142
.

For any SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
compatible with the causal diagram
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{pomdp}}
of Fig.
10(e)
, there is an SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
compatible with
\mathcal{G}_{\textsc{pomdp}}
such that for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle P^{(2)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right)=P^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(2)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]=\mathbb{E}^{*}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]

(526)

while

\displaystyle P^{(1)}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right)\neq P^{*}\left(o_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(1)}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right]\neq\mathbb{E}^{*}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right]

(527)

\blacksquare

The following example corroborates the proposition mentioned above by constructing an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
compatible with the causal diagram of Fig.
10(e)
that induces the same interventional distribution in the underlying environment but generates different observations.

Example 143

(POMDP, Interventional
\not\Rightarrow
Observational)
.

Consider the following SCM environment rolling over stages
i=1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(2)}=\left\langle\bm{U}=\{U_{i,1},U_{i,2},U_{i,3}\},\bm{V}=\{X_{i},Y_{i},S_{i},O_{i}\},\mathscr{F}=\left\{\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{i}\right\},P^{(2)}(\bm{U})\right\rangle_{i=1,2,\dots}.

(528)

Similar to the previous example, the above SCM is identical to the model
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
defined in Example
39
except that for every stage
i=1,2,\dots
, the learner does not observe the state
S_{i}
, but only receives new endogenous variable
O_{i}\leftarrow 0
. We compute the observational distributions
P\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}\right]
and the interventional distributions
P_{\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}}\left(S_{i+1}\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i}\right)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\bm{X}}_{1:i}}\left[Y\mid\bar{\bm{O}}_{1:i}\right]
evaluated in
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
, following the discussion in Example
39
. The analysis suggests that the observational and interventional distributions coincide in the model
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
, which could be described using the same finite automaton of Fig.
54
(b).

Comparing with system dynamics in the SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Example
141
, we find that model
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
coincides with
\mathcal{M}^{*}
in the interventional distributions (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
), but disagree in the observational distributions (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
). More specifically, given any observations and actions’ history
\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}
, the observed intermediate reward in
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(2)}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{(2)}_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i}\right]

(529)

\displaystyle=0.5

(530)

On the other hand, evaluating the observed intermediate reward given the history
\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}
in the model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}\left[Y_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{o}}_{1:i},\bar{\bm{x}}_{1:i}\right]=0.1,

(531)

which differs from the corresponding query in
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
. This complements previous examples and illustrates that interventional queries are generally non-identifiable from the observational data in POMDP environments.
\blacksquare

We organize the examples and results discussed in this section and summarize them in Fig.
54
. The agent interacts with the ground-truth SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(Example
141
) in the middle, through passive observation or active intervention, and generates the observational and interventional distributions. The system dynamics of these distributions do not satisfy the Markov property and can be represented using the latent states in standard POMDPs. The finite automata in Fig.
54
(a, b) describes these latent dynamics, respectively.

Assuming only observational data (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
) is available, one can construct an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}
(left side) that generates the same the observational data but have different interventional distributions (i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1}=\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{2}\neq\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_{2}
). This implies that, in practice, natural trajectories of other behavioral agents collected from passive observations are generally insufficient to make claims about the learning agent’s actions and performance. Conversely, whenever the interventional data (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) is available, one can construct an alternative SCM
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}
(right side) that generates the same interventional distribution but has a different observational one (i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1}\neq\mathcal{L}^{(2)}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{2}=\mathcal{L}^{(2)}_{2}
). This might seem counterintuitive, as interventions are generally thought to be more informative than simply observing a system as it evolves over time. However, in practice, this approach does not enable the learning agent to predict how other agents will behave within the same environment.

Appendix G
Counterfactual Distributions and Axioms

This section will review the formal definition of counterfactual distributions in the language of structural causality and related axioms for inference. Specifically, counterfactual reasoning refers to thinking about alternative ways the world could be, including ways that might conflict with how the world, in fact, currently is. For instance, if the patient took the aspirin and the headache was cured, would the headache still be gone had they not taken the drug? Counterfactual queries can be directly determined from a fully specified structural causal model, as described below.

Definition 144

(Counterfactual Distribution)
.

An SCM
\mathcal{M}=\langle\bm{U},\bm{V},\mathscr{F},P\rangle
induces a family of joint distributions over counterfactual events
\bm{Y}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}},\ldots,\bm{Z}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}
, for any
\bm{Y},\bm{Z},\dots,\bm{X},\bm{W}\subseteq\bm{V}
:

\displaystyle P\left(\bm{y}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}},\dots,\bm{z}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}\right)\equiv\sum_{\bm{u}}\mathds{1}\left\{\bm{Y}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}}(\bm{u})=\bm{y},\dots,\bm{Z}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}(\bm{u})=\bm{w}\right\}P(\bm{u}),

(532)

Note that the l.h.s. of Eq.
532
contains variables with different subscripts, which, syntactically, encode different counterfactual “worlds.” The evaluation implied by this equation can be described as the following process:

1.

For each set of subscripts relative to each set of variables (e.g.,
\pi_{\bm{X}},\dots,\pi_{\bm{W}}
for
\bm{Y},\dots,\bm{Z}
, respectively), replace the corresponding mechanisms with the appropriate constants and generate
\mathscr{F}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}},\dots,\mathscr{F}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}
(Eq.
7
), creating submodels
\mathcal{M}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}
(of
\mathcal{M}
);

2.

For each unit
\bm{U}=\bm{u}
, Nature evaluates the modified mechanisms (e.g.,
\mathscr{F}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}},\dots,\mathscr{F}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}
) following a valid order (i.e., any variable in the l.h.s. is evaluated after the ones in the r.h.s.) to obtain the potential responses of the observables, and

3.

The probability mass
P(\bm{U}=\bm{u})
is then accumulated for each instantiation
U=u
that is consistent with the events over the counterfactual variables – for instance,
{\bm{Y}}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}}={\bm{y}}\dots,{\bm{Z}}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}={\bm{z}}
, i.e.,
{\bm{Y}}={\bm{y}},\dots,{\bm{Z}}={\bm{z}}
in the submodels
\mathcal{M}_{\pi_{\bm{X}}},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{\pi_{\bm{W}}}
, respectively.

As a special case, we denote by
P\left(\bm{Y}_{\bm{x}},\dots,\bm{Z}_{\bm{w}}\right)
the counterfactual distributions entailed by atomic interventions
\text{do}(\bm{x}),\dots,\text{do}(\bm{w})
, which is a joint distribution over variables
\bm{Y},\dots,\bm{Z}
in submodels
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{x}},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{\bm{w}}
respectively.

Example 145
.

Consider the MAB model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
described in Eq.
3
. Since there is a group of patients who did not receive the treatment and died (
X=0,Y=0
), one may wonder whether these patients would have been alive (
Y=1
) had they been given the treatment (
X=1
). This counterfactual question is written as
P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1\mid X=0,Y=0)
. Note that

\displaystyle P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1\mid X=0,Y=0)=\frac{P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1,X=0,Y=0)}{P(X=0,Y=0)}

(533)

where the denominator is trivially obtainable since it only involves observational probabilities. Following the calculation in Eq.
17
we obtain

\displaystyle P(X=0,Y=0)=P(U\geq 0.2)=0.2

(534)

On the other hand, the numerator,
P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1,X=0,Y=0)
, refers to two different worlds and cannot be written in the languages of observational and interventional distributions since they do not allow for probability expressions involving more than one subscript (each encoding a different world). Using the procedure dictated in Eq.
532
, we obtain

\displaystyle P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1,X=0,Y=0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathds{1}\{u<0.4-\Delta,u\geq 0.8,u\geq 0.4\}du

(535)

\displaystyle=P(U<0.4-\Delta,U\geq 0.8)

(536)

Since
\Delta\in(0,0.4)
, compute Eq.
536
gives,
P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1,X=0,Y=0)=0
. This implies

\displaystyle P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1\mid X=0,Y=0)=\frac{P(Y_{X\leftarrow 1}=1,X=0,Y=0)}{P(X=0,Y=0)}=0

(537)

The conclusion following this counterfactual analysis is clear: even if we had given the treatment to everyone who did not survive, none of them would have survived. In other words, the treatment would not have prevented their death.

Counterfactual variables follow three properties of composition, effectiveness, and reversibility, which hold in all structural causal models.

Theorem 146

(Counterfactual Axioms)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\bm{U},\bm{L},\bm{V},\mathscr{F},P\rangle
be an SCM. For any three sets of variables
\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{W}\subseteq\bm{V}
, the following properties hold

1.

Composition.

\bm{W}_{\bm{x}}(\bm{u})=\bm{w}\Rightarrow\bm{Y}_{\bm{x},\bm{w}}(\bm{u})=\bm{Y}_{\bm{x}}(\bm{u})
;

2.

Effectiveness.

\bm{X}_{\bm{x},\bm{w}}(\bm{u})=\bm{x}
;

3.

Reversibility

\left(\bm{Y}_{\bm{x},\bm{w}}(\bm{u})=\bm{y}\right)\&\left(\bm{W}_{\bm{x},\bm{y}}(\bm{u})=\bm{w}\right)\Rightarrow\bm{Y}_{\bm{x}}(\bm{u})=\bm{y}
.

Appendix H
Proofs for Chapter 4

See
43

Proof.

Note that the interventional distribution
P_{\pi}\left(\bm{v}\right)
is the joint distribution over
\bm{Y}
evaluated in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
. By factorizing over variables in submodel
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi}
we have

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left(\bm{v}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{u}}\prod_{V\in\bm{V}\setminus\bm{X}}P(v\mid\bm{\mathit{pa}}_{V},\bm{u}_{V})\prod_{i=1}^{H}\pi_{i}(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i})P(\bm{u})

(538)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{H}\pi_{i}(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i})\sum_{\bm{u}}\prod_{V\in\bm{V}\setminus\bm{X}}P(v\mid\bm{\mathit{pa}}_{V},\bm{u}_{V})P(\bm{u})

(539)

The last step holds since actions and states
\bm{X}\cup\bm{S}\subseteq\bm{V}
are all observed. Summing over unobserved variables
\bm{U}
gives

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left(\bm{v}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{H}\pi_{i}(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i})P_{\bm{x}}\left(\bm{v}\setminus\bm{x}\right)

(540)

Similarly, factorizing variables in SCM
\mathcal{M}^{*}
gives

\displaystyle P(\bm{v})=\sum_{\bm{u}}\prod_{V\in\bm{V}\setminus\bm{X}}P(v\mid\bm{\mathit{pa}}_{V},\bm{u}_{V})\prod_{i=1}^{H}P(x_{i}\mid\bm{\mathit{pa}}_{i},\bm{u}_{i})P(\bm{u})

(541)

Since the NUC holds, Condition (1) of Def.
40
says that for every action
X_{i}
, endogenous parents
\bm{\mathit{PA}}_{i}\subseteq\bar{\bm{X}}_{i-1}\cup\bar{\bm{S}}_{i}
. The above equation can be further written as

\displaystyle P(\bm{v})=\sum_{\bm{u}}\prod_{V\in\bm{V}\setminus\bm{X}}P(v\mid\bm{\mathit{pa}}_{V},\bm{u}_{V})\prod_{i=1}^{H}P(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bm{u}_{i})P(\bm{u})

(542)

Condition (2) of Def.
40
says that for every action
X_{i}
, exogenous parents
\bm{U}_{i}
are indpendent noises affecting only
X_{i}
. We must have

\displaystyle P(\bm{v})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{H}\sum_{\bm{u}_{i}}P(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bm{u}_{i})P(\bm{u}_{i})\sum_{\bm{u}}\prod_{V\in\bm{V}\setminus\bm{X}}P(v\mid\bm{\mathit{pa}}_{V},\bm{u}_{V})P(\bm{u})

(543)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{H}P(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})P_{\bm{x}}\left(\bm{v}\setminus\bm{x}\right)

(544)

Eq.
544
implies the interventional distribution
P_{\bm{x}}\left(\bm{V}\setminus\bm{X}\right)
is identifiable from the observational distribution
P(\bm{V})
and is given by

\displaystyle P_{\bm{x}}\left(\bm{v}\setminus\bm{x}\right)=P(\bm{v})\prod_{i=1}^{H}\frac{1}{P(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})}

(545)

Eqs.
540
and
545
give

\displaystyle P_{\pi}\left(\bm{v}\right)=P(\bm{v})\prod_{i=1}^{H}\frac{\pi_{i}(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i})}{P(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})}

(546)

The expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
can thus be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{v}\setminus\bm{x},\bm{s}}\mathcal{R}(\bm{y})P_{\pi}\left(\bm{v}\right)

(547)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{v}\setminus\bm{x},\bm{s}}\mathcal{R}(\bm{y})P(\bm{v})\prod_{i=1}^{H}\frac{\pi_{i}(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i})}{P(x_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})}

(548)

Summing over domains of variables
\bm{V}\setminus(\bm{X}\cup\bm{S})
gives the IPW adjustment formula.

Proof.

Let function
Q_{\pi}^{(H)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}\right]
. The expected reward
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]
can thus be written as, following the decomposition in Eq.
144
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H}}Q_{\pi}^{(H)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\prod_{i=1}^{H}P\left(s_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i-1}\right)\pi_{i}\left(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i}\right)

(549)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1}}\left(\sum_{x_{H},\bm{s}_{H}}Q_{\pi}^{(H)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\pi_{H}(x_{H}\mid\bm{S}_{H})P(\bm{s}_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1})\right)

(550)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{H-1}P\left(s_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i-1}\right)\pi_{i}\left(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i}\right)

(551)

Summing over domains of states
\bm{S}_{H}
and
\bm{X}_{H}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]=\sum_{\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1}}Q_{\pi}^{(H-1)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1})\prod_{i=1}^{H-1}P\left(s_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{i-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i-1}\right)\pi_{i}\left(x_{i}\mid\bm{s}_{i}\right),

(552)

where function
Q_{\pi}^{(H-1)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1})
is defined as

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}^{(H-1)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{H},\bm{s}_{H}}Q_{\pi}^{(H)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H})\pi_{H}(x_{H}\mid\bm{S}_{H})P(\bm{s}_{i}\mid\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1})

(553)

\displaystyle=E\left[\sum_{x_{H}}Q_{\pi}^{(H)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{H},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1},\bm{S}_{H})\pi_{H}\left(x_{H}\mid\bm{S}_{H}\right)\,\middle|\,\bar{\bm{x}}_{H-1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{H-1}\right]

(554)

By repeatedly applying the above marginalization procedure, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\mathcal{R}(\bm{Y})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{1},\bm{s}_{1}}Q_{\pi}^{(1)}(x_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\pi_{1}(x_{1}\mid\bm{s}_{1})P(\bm{s}_{1})

(555)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x_{1}}Q_{\pi}^{(1)}(x_{1},\bm{S}_{1})\pi_{1}(x_{1}\mid\bm{S}_{1})\right],

(556)

where function
Q_{\pi}^{(i)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})
is given by, for
i=1,\dots,H-1
,

\displaystyle Q_{\pi}^{(i)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i})=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{x_{i+1}}Q_{\pi}^{(i+1)}(\bar{\bm{x}}_{i+1},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i},\bm{S}_{i+1})\pi_{i+1}\left(x_{i+1}\mid\bm{S}_{i+1}\right)\,\middle|\,\bar{\bm{x}}_{i},\bar{\bm{s}}_{i}\right].

(557)

This proves the DP identification formula.
∎

\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\pi},\Pi,\mathcal{R}\right\rangle

\text{do}(\pi)
